Boundary structure of gauge fields on asymptotically AdS spaces
Maxim Grigoriev † and Mikhail Markov

Abstract
We study boundary structure of asymptotically AdS gravity and (gauge) fields defined on this background by employing the gauge PDE approach. The essential step of the construction is the incorporation of the boundary-defining function among the fields of the theory, which allows us to realise the asymptotic boundary as a space-time submanifold by employing the gauge PDE implementation of Penrose’s concept of asymptotically-simple space. In so doing the gauge PDE describing the boundary structure is obtained by restricting to the boundary of spacetime and simultaneously restricting to the boundary of the field space by setting the boundary defining function to zero. To implement this step systematically we introduce a notion of Q Q -boundary which seems to be new. The main concrete result of this work is the construction of the efficient boundary calculus, which gives a recursive procedure to obtain the explicit form of the equations satisfied by the boundary fields and their gauge transformations for boundary dimension d ⩾ 3 d\,{\geqslant}\,3 . These include obstruction equations (such as Bach equation or Yang-Mills equation for d = 4 d=4 ) and generalised conservation equations in the subleading sector. In particular, we derive the explicit form of the higher conformal Yang-Mills equation for d = 8 d=8 . The approach is very general and, in principle, applies to generic (gauge) fields on the Einstein gravity background producing a conformally-invariant gauge theory on the boundary, which describes their boundary structure. It can be considered as an extension of the Fefferman-Graham construction that takes into account both the leading and the subleading sector of the bulk fields. a

1
Introduction

Gauge theories on manifolds with asymptotic boundaries emerge in the context of AdS/CFT correspondence
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
and flat-space holography
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
, see also

[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]

for important earlier developments and more recent contributions relevant in the present context.
If the bulk theory is local, it naturally induces a number of geometrical structures on the boundary, which can be again interpreted as a local gauge theory defined therein. For instance, asymptotically AdS (AAdS) gravity
1
1

1

In mathematical literature vacuum solutions of AAdS gravity are referred to as asymptotically Poincaré-Einstein manifolds.
induces conformal metric (subject to conformal gravity equations in the case of even boundary dimension) along with the so-called subleading mode.

The identification of the boundary structure is often performed by mapping the spacetime into the interior of a manifold with boundary so that the asymptotic boundary becomes a usual boundary. In so doing the metric diverges when approaching the boundary, but its conformal class remains well-defined so that one can pick a suitable representative, so-called “unphysical metric”, which is well defined at the boundary.
This idea was put forward by R. Penrose
[
9
]

and gives a precise meaning to the near-boundary analysis provided one has chosen coordinates and imposed a suitable gauge condition (if gauge fields are present). However, the subtlety is that the unphysical metric is defined modulo Weyl rescalings so that the boundary structure inherits Weyl symmetry and is naturally studied using the conformal geometry tools
[
16
,
17
,
12
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
,
see also
[
22
,
23
]
. In particular, the near boundary analysis of AAdS gravity amounts to the celebrated Fefferman-Graham (FG) construction
[
12
,
24
]
which has been initially proposed as a tool to study conformal invariants. Strictly speaking, FG construction operates in terms of the ambient spacetime extension of the AAdS space-time so that the conformal boundary is identified with a suitable subquotient of the ambient spacetime.

Although gravity is a gauge theory, its gauge invariance is usually controlled by geometrical methods which allows one to study boundary structure without resorting to gauge-theoretical techniques. Moreover, even in the case of Yang-Mills (YM) theory on AAdS space a geometrical approach can be developed
[
25
]
. Nevertheless, the general setup requires a specifically designed formalism. Of course, one can always get rid of the gauge invariance by imposing suitable gauge conditions but this complicates the technique and hides the critically important structures and symmetries.

As far as general local gauge theories on manifolds with boundaries are concerned, a systematic framework to study the boundary structure is based on representing the system as a so-called gauge PDE and was put forward in
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
, see also
[
29
,
30
]
for a more recent discussion. Gauge PDE (gPDE) is a very general and flexible generalisation of the Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV)
[
31
,
32
]
formulation on jet-bundles
[
33
,
34
]
known in the context of local BRST cohomology, see
[
35
]
for further details and
[
36
,
37
]
for the earlier but less geometrical version of the gPDE concept. As particular cases, gPDE approach includes AKSZ construction of topological theories
[
38
]
, unfolded formalism of higher spin theories
[
39
,
40
]
, and the aforementioned jet-bundle BV formulation. Let us stress that here we refer to the formulations at the level of equations of motion. Moreover, all considerations of this work are limited to this level. In particular, presymplectic structures, Lagrangians, charges etc. are not discussed. For the Lagrangian version of gPDE formalism see e.g.
[
29
,
41
]
and reference therein. Alternative approaches to gauge theories on manifolds with boundaries, which are based on one or another extension of the BV formalism can be found in
[
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
.

In the gPDE approach the structure of a local gauge field theory is encoded in the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded fibre bundle over
T[1]X
(shifted tangent bundle over the spacetime
X
), equipped with a compatible homological vector field
Q
. Solutions and their gauge transformation can be expressed in terms of section of the underlying bundle. A crucial property that makes gPDEs useful in the context of spacetimes with boundaries is that they behave well with respect to restricting to spacetime submanifolds and boundaries in particular, giving a systematic way to describe boundary structure without the need to fix gauges or pick particular spacetime or field-space coordinates. Of course, using one or another coordinate system can be very convenient in analysing the boundary structure of a given gauge theory but the gauge PDE encoding this structure is defined in a coordinate-free way. The gauge PDE approach to boundary structure was successfully applied to generic gauge fields on AdS space in
[
26
,
27
,
46
]
by employing the ambient space representation so that the conformal boundary is identified with a submanifold of the ambient space.

In this work we employ the version of gauge PDE approach to boundary structure of asymptotically flat/AdS gravity developed in
[
30
]
in the study of asymptotic symmetries. In contrast to the ambient space FG construction, it operates in terms of spacetime at the price of introducing an additional compensator field
\Omega
playing a role of the boundary defining functions and the auxiliary Weyl symmetry
\delta_{\bar{\lambda}}\Omega=\bar{\lambda}\Omega
. This allows us to reformulate Penrose’s definition of asymptotically simple space in the gauge-theoretical and more specifically gauge PDE language, giving a systematic derivation of the gPDE describing the boundary structure of asymptotically AdS/flat gravity. The extension of this prescription to systems involving (gauge) fields on the gravity background turns out to be rather natural and is constructed in this work. The equations appearing in the sector of gravity are often similar to those encountered in the context of conformal geometrical approach to boundary structure though their interpretation is somewhat different.

Although identification of the boundary gPDE for AAdS gravity (possibly with additional gauge fields) is straightforward, its structure is generally rather involved and the underlying gauge theory is to be reformulated in the human-readable form by e.g. giving a set of fields, the PDEs they satisfy, and the gauge transformations they are subject to. While for linearized systems this can be done in a more or less straightforward way, see e.g.
[
27
,
46
]
, the analysis of nonlinear systems requires a specific technique which we call boundary calculus. At a more technical level, we identify a certain boundary gauge PDE that describes unconstrained fields and their gauge transformations in a tractable way. This gauge PDE comes equipped with a system of functions and vector fields that can be computed recursively and in terms of which the boundary equations of motion and gauge transformation can be explicitly found.

In this work we explicitly construct the boundary calculus for a number of typical examples such as AAdS gravity, critical scalar field and YM theory on the generic AAdS-gravity background. In particular, we explicitly present the, apparently new, higher conformal YM equation induced on 8-dimensional boundary as well as the corresponding equations of motion and gauge transformations for the YM subleading. The construction of general boundary calculus for AAdS gravity and scalar field and YM field defined on its background as well as the explicit form of the boundary structure in lower dimensions are the main results presented in this work.

While the structure of leading modes of AAdS gravity and gauge fields on its background is relatively well studied in the existing literature, the subleading modes are often discussed less explicitly. Our work partially covers this gap by giving an exhaustive description of the equations of motion and gauge transformations for the subleading sector of AAdS gravity together with YM theory and scalar field. It turns out that in all these cases the boundary system has the structure of a linear subleading defined on the background of the generally nonlinear system describing the leading mode. In particular, equations of motion and gauge transformations for the subleading depends on the leading mode and its gauge parameters. In the gPDE language this situation is described by a bundle of gauge PDEs where the base and the fibre describes, respectively, the leading and the subleading. Such objects are known as gPDEs over background
[
41
]
and, surprisingly, they naturally emerge on the boundary of irreducible systems such as AAdS gravity or YM theory. Note that in the particular case of linear gauge fields on AdS space this was observed already in
[
27
]
.

The paper is organized as follows: Section
2
introduces the basics of the gPDE approach to boundary structure and recalls the gPDE formulation of conformal-like GR. In particular, the notion of
Q
-boundary is also proposed there. In Section
3
we discuss preminimal model of conformal-like GR and use it to introduce a number of objects and techniques needed in the further analysis. Section
4
contains the construction of the boundary calculus in the case of gravity and is central for this work. The boundary calculus is explicitly applied to study the boundary structure of GR in Section
5
where we also explain how the explicit space-time form of the equations of motion and gauge transformations on the boundary are obtained. Finally, Section
6
is devoted to the boundary structure of (gauge) fields on the AAdS gravity background. The corresponding version of boundary calculus is constructed for Klein-Gordon field and YM theory and the explicit description of YM boundary structure for
d=4,6,8
is obtained.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Gauge PDEs

In this section we recall basics of the gauge PDE approach to local gauge theories and its extension to the case of theories on manifolds with boundaries.

The main building block is a notion of
Q
-manifold which is a pair
(M,Q)
, where
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded supermanifold, equipped with degree-one odd vector field
Q
satisfying
Q^{2}=0
. We denote the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading by
\mathrm{gh}(\cdot)
and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-parity by
|\cdot|
. The systems considered in this work do not involve physical fermionic fields and hence the two gradings are compatible in the sense that
|\cdot|=\mathrm{gh}(\cdot)\,\text{mod}\,2
, so that it is enough to keep track of the ghost degree only.

The basic example of a
Q
-manifold is the shifted tangent bundle
T[1]X
of a smooth manifold
X
or, more generally, a graded supermanifold. Its local coordinates can be chosen as
\{x^{\mu},\theta^{\mu}\}
, where
x^{\mu}
– are local coordinates on the base
X
and
\theta^{\mu}
are the graded-anticommuting fibre coordinates in the coordinate frame. Functions on
T[1]X
are isomorphic as an algebra to differential forms on
X
and this identifies the de Rham differential of
X
with the homological vector field
\mathrm{d}_{X}\equiv\theta^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
.

Natural morphisms between
Q
-manifolds, so called
Q
-maps, are degree-preserving maps compatible with the
Q
-structures. In terms of algebras of functions on
(M_{1},Q_{1})
and
(M_{2},Q_{2})
such map
\mu:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is a degree-preserving homomorphism
\mu^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M_{2})\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M_{1})
such that
Q_{1}\circ\mu^{*}=\mu^{*}\circ Q_{2}
. In particular, one has a natural notion of
Q
-submanifold and
Q
-bundle. In the latter case both the total space and the base are
Q
-manifolds and the projection is required to be a
Q
map
[
47
]
.

If one disregards space-time locality,
Q
-manifold is precisely a geometrical object corresponding to a gauge theory defined at the level of equations of motion
and formulated in terms of the non-Lagrangian version of BV formalism, see
[
37
,
48
]
. If one prefers to maintain space-time locality in a manifest way, one can for instance resort to non-Lagrangian BV defined on a suitable jet-bundle
[
37
]
, see also
[
34
]
for a usual BV formalism on jet-bundles. However, a more flexible and geometrical approach which suits our needs in this work is provided by so-called gauge PDEs.

Definition 2.1
.

1) Gauge PDE
(E,Q,T[1]X)
is a
Q
-bundle
\pi:(E,Q)\rightarrow(T[1]X,d_{X})
, where
X
is a space-time manifold.

2) Solutions to
(E,Q,T[1]X)
are sections
\sigma:T[1]X\to E
satisfying

\displaystyle d_{X}\circ\sigma^{*}=\sigma^{*}\circ Q

(2.1)

3) Infinitesimal gauge transformations of section
\sigma
are defined as

\displaystyle\delta\sigma^{*}=\sigma^{*}[Q,Y],

(2.2)

where
Y
– is a vertical field of degree
-1
on
E
, called a gauge parameter. In a similar way one defines gauge-for-gauge symmetries etc.

This definition may be further restricted in order to exclude gPDEs corresponding to pathological field theoretical systems, see see
[
35
,
29
]
for more details.

Given a local gauge theory defined at the level of equation of motion it can be reformulated as a local BV system, i.e. a graded fibre bundle
\mathcal{E}\to X
, where
X
denotes the space-time manifold, together with the evolutionary homological vector field
s
, called BRST differential, defined on the infinite-jet bundle
J^{\infty}(\mathcal{E})
of
\mathcal{E}\to X
. In its turn, a local BV system naturally defines a gauge PDE, at least locally. Indeed, taking as
E
the pullback of
J^{\infty}(\mathcal{E})
by the projection
T[1]X\to X
one can identify functions on
E
with horizontal forms on
J^{\infty}(\mathcal{E})
. On the horizontal forms one has two anticommuting differentials: the horizontal differential
\mathrm{d_{h}}
and the Lie derivative
L_{s}
with respect to
s
. Seen as a vector field on
E
the total differential
\mathrm{d_{h}}+L_{s}
makes
(E,Q,T[1]X)
into a gPDE. At least locally, the gauge theory encoded by this gPDE is equivalent to the starting point BV system
[
36
]
.

An interesting phenomenon happens if the starting point gauge theory is diffeomorphism invariant, i.e. if space-time diffeomorphisms are among the gauge transformations encoded in
s
, i.e. the term in
s
linear in the respective ghost variables
\xi^{a}
has the structure
\xi^{a}D_{a}+\ldots
, where
D_{a}
are total derivatives and
\ldots
denote terms not involving undifferentiated
\xi^{a}
. It turns out that in this case one can change fibre coordinates in such a way that the total
Q
structure factorizes as
Q=s+\mathrm{d_{X}}
, where
s
is a vector field on the typical fibre. Geometrically, this means that the underlying
Q
-bundle is locally trivial. Moreover, in a suitable coordinate system the expression of
s
coincides with the initial
s
restricted to a fibre. This gives a very simple procedure to build gPDE formulations for diffeomorphism invariant systems.

When dealing with gauge theories one often finds that the same theory can be formulated using different set of fields and gauge generators. Such equivalent formulations can be related via the elimination of auxiliary fields and gauge-fixing algebraic gauge symmetries. In the gPDE language this equivalence is implemented as follows:

Definition 2.2
.

Sub-gPDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q_{\mathrm{red}},T[1]X)\subset(E,Q,T[1]X)
is called an equivalent reduction of
E
if locally there exists a set of functions
w^{\alpha}
such that
w^{\alpha},v^{\alpha}\equiv Qw^{\alpha}
are independent and
E_{\mathrm{red}}
is locally a zero locus of
w^{\alpha},v^{\alpha}
.

In other words, locally the equivalence reduces to the elimination of contractible pairs
w^{\alpha}
and
Qw^{\alpha}
. If one forgets about the bundle structure the notion reduces to the usual elimination of contractible pairs as it is known in the context of local BRST cohomology. A more geometrical version of the definition and further details can be found
[
35
]
.

If it is clear from context what the space-time manifold of the system is, we tend to omit the explicit mentioning of
T[1]X
in denoting gPDEs to keep conventions light, i.e. we simply use
(E,Q)
instead of
(E,Q,T[1]X)
.

2.2
Gauge PDEs on manifolds with boundaries

It turned out that gauge PDEs are especially useful if one is interested in the structure induced by a local gauge theory on a space-time submanifold and, particularly, boundary and/or low-dimensional space-time strata. The idea is very simple: consider a gauge PDE
(E,Q,T[1]X)
, where
X
possesses a nontrivial boundary
\Sigma=\partial X
and denote the embedding map by
i:\Sigma\hookrightarrow X
. To be concrete, here we talk about boundary
\Sigma
but the general discussion applies to any submanifold
\Sigma\subset X
. This induces a new gPDE
i^{*}E
on
\Sigma
, which is simply the restriction (also called pullback) of
E
to
T[1]\Sigma\subset T[1]X
. Here, by abusing notations, we also denote by
i
the corresponding pushforward
T[1]\Sigma\hookrightarrow T[1]X
. It is easy to verify that
i^{*}E
is again a gPDE. In particular,
Q
is tangent to
i^{*}E\subset E
.
In a slightly less general form this approach to boundary values was originally employed to study the boundary values of gauge fields in
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
49
]
.

The pullback
i^{*}E
of the original gPDE to the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
describes the unconstrained boundary values of the fields of the original
E
. In general, one in addition wants to be able to impose additional conditions on the boundary values of fields, gauge parameters, etc. This leads to the following more general notion:

Definition 2.3
.

[
30
]

1) By a gauge PDE with boundaries we mean the following data:
(E,Q,T[1]X,E_{\Sigma},T[1]\Sigma)
, where gPDE
(E,Q,T[1]X)
is a gPDE on
X
and
(E_{\Sigma},Q_{\Sigma},T[1]\Sigma)
is a gPDE on the boundary
\Sigma=\partial X
, which is a sub-gPDE of
i^{*}E
.
Q_{\Sigma}
denotes the restriction of
Q
on
E_{\Sigma}
.

2) Solutions to
(E,Q,T[1]X,E_{\Sigma},T[1]\Sigma)
are sections
\sigma:T[1]X\to E
such that the restriction of
\sigma
to
T[1]\Sigma
is a solution to
(E_{\Sigma},Q_{\Sigma},T[1]\Sigma)

3) Gauge symmetries are generated by the vector field of the form
[Q{,}\,Y]
, where
Y
is a vertical vector field on
E
required to be tangent to
E_{\Sigma}
.

Although the above definition can be often applied in a straightforward way, its implementation in the case of asymptotic boundary requires some extra care. The point is that in this case the boundary defining function is an independent field and it has to be set to zero in a way compatible with
Q
. In other words the fibre of
E
over the interior differs from the fibre over the boundary or, in other words,
E
is slightly not locally trivial. But the restrictions of
E
to the interior and to the boundary are locally trivial.

To be more precise, in the case of gravity we employ a gauge theoretical implementation of the Penrose’s notion of asymptotically-simple space
[
50
]
. Namely, one of the fibre coordinates is the boundary defining function
\Omega
. In the interior of
X
one requires
\Omega>0
while on the boundary
\partial X
one sets
\Omega=0
(in the gPDE setup one also sets
Q\Omega=0
). Roughly speaking, typical fibre
F
of
E
is a manifold with boundary and if we want to describe the system without asymptotic boundary we not only take the interior
Int(X)
of the base but also take as fibre the interior
Int(F)
of the initial fibre. At the same time the system induced on the boundary has
\partial X
as the base space and
\partial F
as the fibre.

A possible way to incorporate this setup in a more precise and geometrical way is as follows: introduce a notion of
Q
-manifold with
Q
-boundary. The
Q
-boundary has codimension
2
and is such that near the boundary there is a coordinate system
\Omega,\lambda,z^{I}
,
\mathrm{gh}(\Omega)=p,\mathrm{gh}(\lambda)=p+1
such that
Q\Omega=\lambda
and the
Q
-boundary is singled out by
\Omega=0,\lambda=0
.

Forgetting for the moment about the differential-graded structure lets us define a smooth geometry counterpart of what we need. We say that
E\to X
is a bundle with asymptotic boundary if, roughly speaking, both the base and the fibre are manifolds with boundaries. More formally,
E
is a manifold with corners and
X
is a manifold with boundary. Moreover,
E\to X
is a locally-trivial fibre bundle whose typical fibre is a manifold with boundary. Given a bundle with asymptotic boundary there are few other bundles associated to it: the “interior” of
E
, which is a bundle
Int(E)\to Int(X)
, and the “boundary bundle”
\partial(\pi^{-1}(\partial X))\to\partial X
. Some other combinations are also possible. The setup we need for gauge theories with asymptotic boundaries is obtained by considering
Q
-boundaries and taking the bulk system to be
Int(E)\to Int(X)
and the boundary system to be
\partial(\pi^{-1}(\partial X))\to\partial X
.

2.3
Implicit definition of gPDEs

Although the language of gPDEs is very geometrical and concise it is often difficult to apply it in concrete examples because in realistic applications the explicit form of
Q
in terms of local coordinates on
E
is complicated or not even known. This generally happens if the underlying gauge theory is nontopological and nonlinear, see e.g.
[
51
,
52
]
for a recent discussion.

A possible way out is to define
(E,Q)
as a sub-gPDE of a bigger gPDE, e.g. the one arising from the jet-bundle formulation of the system. This can be naturally done by allowing
E
to have negative ghost degree coordinates in such a way that the extended
Q
incorporates a Koszul-Tate differential that implements restriction to the surface. This is the standard idea of Batalin-Vilkovisky formalism and its implementation in the gauge PDE setup leads to a relatively concise gPDE formulation of gauge theories. In particular, the fact that a given gauge theory formulated in BV terms admits an equivalent gPDE representation was shown in exactly this way
[
36
]
, see also
[
37
,
35
]
.

In this work we refrain from employing negative degree variables but simply allow our actual gauge PDE to be a subbundle in the ambient bundle
\pi:E\to T[1]X
equipped with a
\pi
-projectable vector field
Q
of degree
1
. If
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
is the ideal of functions vanishing on the subbundle we denote this data as
(E,Q,\mathcal{I},T[1]X)
. We assume that the zero locus of
\mathcal{I}
is a regular subbundle of
E
and that
Q\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{I}
(i.e.
Q
is tangent to the zero-locus of
\mathcal{I}
). Moreover, in all our example the ambient
Q
is nilpotent so that
(E,Q,\mathcal{I},T[1]X)
is itself a gPDE. Of course, one could generally ask less, namely, that
Q^{2}f\in\mathcal{I}
for any function
f
. This is enough to have a nilpotent
Q
on the zero locus.

If we define our gPDE as
(E,Q,\mathcal{I})
and assume
Q^{2}=0
then its solutions are solutions to the underlying gPDE
(E,Q)
that satisfy in addition
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{I})=0
. Such formulation of the system is especially useful if
(E,Q,T[1]X)
is off-shell. This means that its space of solutions is locally parametrised by a set of unconstrained fields. More formally, as a gPDE
(E,Q)
is equivalent to a nonegatively-graded jet-bundle equipped with an evolutionary homological vector field. More precisely, as we recalled earlier, a jet-bundle equipped with an evolutionary homological vector field
s
can be pulled back to
T[1]X
and equipped with
\mathrm{d_{h}}+L_{s}
, giving a gPDE. If
(E,Q)
is off-shell, all the nontrivial differential equations on our fields originate from
\mathcal{I}
. Mention that somewhat similar formulation are known in the literature, see e.g.
[
40
,
53
,
54
]
.

If
(E,Q)
is off-shell, one can usually define a concept of prolongation. Namely, one can equip
E
with a distribution generated by vector fields
\nabla_{A}
,
\mathrm{gh}(\nabla_{A})=0
, and
[Q{,}\,\nabla_{A}]=0
, such that
\mathcal{I}
is generated by

\nabla_{A_{1}}\ldots\nabla_{A_{k}}{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha}
,
k\,{\geqslant}\,0
, where
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha}
are some functions on
E
. If in addition
\sigma^{*}({\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha})=0
implies
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{I})=0
for any
\sigma
solving
(E,Q)
, we refer to
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha}
as to the symbols of the equations of motion.

If we work with implicitly defined gPDEs, it is natural to call two such implicit gPDEs equivalent if the corresponding zero locus are equivalent gPDEs. In particular, the equivalent reduction of the underlying gPDE
(E,Q,T[1]X)
induces an equivalent reduction of the implicitly defined gPDE provided the corresponding contractible pairs
w^{\alpha}
and
Qw^{\alpha}
remain independent functions when restricted to the zero locus of
\mathcal{I}
.
2
2

2

Of course this is only a sufficient condition. The reduction could well affect the ideal without spoiling the equivalence.
More precisely, if
i:\widetilde{E}\to E
is an equivalent reduction then under the above condition
(\widetilde{E},Q,i^{*}(\mathcal{I}))
is an equivalent gPDE.

An additional natural equivalence of implicit gPDEs relates
(E,Q,\mathcal{I})
and

(\widetilde{E},Q,\widetilde{\mathcal{I}})
, where
(\widetilde{E},Q)\subset(E,Q)
is a sub-gPDE and the zero locus of
\mathcal{I}
in
E
coincides with the zero locus of
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
in
\widetilde{E}
. The passage from
E
to
\widetilde{E}
corresponds to solving some of the constraints from
\mathcal{I}
. Note that in this situation
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
is a restriction of
\mathcal{I}
to
\widetilde{E}
.

Finally, let us mention that there are less obvious ways to encode a given gauge PDE in terms of an explicitly defined and often finite-dimensional geometrical object. This can be constructed as a sort of finite-dimensional consistent truncation of a gPDE and is know as a weak gPDE
[
51
]
.

2.4
Conformal-like gravity

Our starting point is the equivalent reformulation of gravity (GR) in the bulk, obtained by adding artificial Weyl symmetry together with its associated compensator field. In the presence of boundaries this gives the gauge theoretical implementation of the Penrose description of the asymptotically-simple spaces. Throughout this work we assume that the space-time dimension
D\equiv\dim X
satisfies
D\geq 4
and for definiteness, we fix the metric signature to be Lorentzian
(+,-\dots,-)
, although none of the results in this work depend on the choice of signature. The boundary dimension is denoted by
d\equiv D-1
.

More precisely, in addition to the metric
g_{ab}
one introduces a scalar field
\Omega
subject to
\Omega>0
. The gauge transformations read as

\delta g_{bc}=\xi^{a}\partial_{a}g_{bc}+g_{ac}\partial_{b}\xi^{a}+g_{ba}\partial_{c}\xi^{a}+2\lambda g_{bc},\hskip 20.00003pt\delta\Omega=\xi^{a}\partial_{a}\Omega+\lambda\Omega\,,

(2.3)

where gauge parameters
\xi^{a},\lambda
are components of the vector field and scalar, respectively. The above system is equivalent to the off-shell (i.e. with trivial equations of motion) GR theory and called off-shell conformal like GR. The equivalence is easily seen by observing that the gauge
\Omega=1
is reachable and completely kills the
\lambda
parameter. In this gauge one stays with metric and its diffeomorphisms only, resulting in the off-shell GR.
Let us stress that this equivalence holds if
\Omega>0
.

We can now reformulate the above system as a gPDE
(E_{cl},Q)
following the steps recalled in Section
2.1
. More precisely,
(E_{cl},Q)
is defined as follows: its typical fibre can be identified with the fibre of the jet-bundle for fields
g_{ab},\Omega
and ghosts
\xi^{a},\lambda
, whose fibres are coordinatised by

D_{(c)}g_{ab}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptD_{(c)}\Omega\,,\hskip 10.00002ptD_{(c)}\xi^{a}\hskip 10.00002ptD_{(c)}\lambda\,.

(2.4)

where
D_{a}
denote the usual total derivatives on the jet-bundle and
{(a)}
denotes a symmetric multiindex. The action of
Q
in this coordinates is given by:

\begin{gathered}Qx^{\mu}=\theta^{\mu},\quad Qg_{bc}=\xi^{a}D_{a}g_{bc}+g_{ac}D_{b}\xi^{a}+g_{ba}D_{c}\xi^{a}+2\lambda g_{bc},\quad Q\xi^{b}=\xi^{a}D_{a}\xi^{b}\,,\\
Q\Omega=\xi^{a}D_{a}\Omega+\lambda\Omega,\qquad Q\lambda=\xi^{a}D_{a}\lambda,\qquad[D_{a}{,}\,Q]=0\,.\end{gathered}

(2.5)

It is important to stress that although we have constructed
E_{cl}
in terms of the starting point jet-bundle, the jet-bundle structure is not fully encoded in the gPDE structure of
E_{cl}
.

In the above gPDE for gravity and later in this work we systematically avoid discussing the global structure of the underlying bundles by assuming that all the bundles involved are globally trivial. Under this assumption all the constructions are coordinate independent in the sense that one is free to use any coordinates on the base but only
x,\theta
-independent coordinate change is allowed in the fibre. For instance, the structure of
Q
in (
2.5
) is clearly not invariant under the naive orthogonal transformations with
x
-dependent parameters and these are to be adjusted to preserve
Q
. Of course, all the general notions such as gPDEs and their morphisms are perfectly global and the bundles encountered in this work can be defined globally. However, for simplicity we restrict ourselves to the case of paralelisable spacetimes where all the relevant bundles can be assumed trivial.

So far we considered the off-shell system. In terms of the above gPDE formulation, the on shell version can be obtained by restricting to a sub-gPDE determined by the prolongation of the Einstein equations for
\widetilde{g}_{ab}=\Omega^{-2}g_{ab}
, rewritten in terms of
g,\Omega
. More precisely,

Proposition 2.4
.

The on-shell GR is described by the implicit gPDE
(E_{cl},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl})
, where the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{cl}
is generated by

\displaystyle D_{(a)}G_{bc}=0\,,\hskip 10.00002ptS=0\,,

(2.6)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{bc}&\equiv D_{b}D_{c}\Omega-\Gamma_{bc}^{d}D_{d}\Omega+\Omega P_{bc}+\rho g_{bc},\\
\rho&\equiv-\dfrac{1}{d}g^{bc}(D_{b}D_{c}\Omega-\Gamma_{bc}^{d}D_{d}\Omega+P_{bc}\Omega)\\
S&\equiv\Omega\rho+\dfrac{1}{2}D_{a}\Omega D^{a}\Omega-\dfrac{\Lambda}{(D-1)(D-2)}\,,\end{split}

(2.7)

and
\Gamma_{bc}^{d}
,
P_{bc}
are, respectively, the Christoffel symbols and the Schouten tensor seen as functions in the jets of the metric.

The proof is based on the transformation properties of the Schouten tensor under Weyl transformations and imposing the gauge condition
\Omega=1
. Details can be found in
[
30
]
. Equations (
2.6
) are known as the almost Einstein equation.

3
Pre-minimal model of the conformal-like GR

3.1
Pre-minimal model

Starting from the off-shell conformal GR represented by the gPDE
(E_{cl},Q)
one can equivalently reduce it
in the sense of Definition
2.2
. For a locally-trivial gPDEs a simple class of equivalent reductions originate from equivalent reductions of the fibre
Q
-manifolds. The one we are interested in is locally of this type, i.e. the base space plays a passive role.

Consider the sub-gPDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
,
r:E_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow E_{cl}
singled out by

\displaystyle D_{(a)}D_{(c}\Gamma_{b)d}^{e}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptD_{(c)}D_{(a}P_{b)c}=0,

(3.1)

\displaystyle Q(D_{(a)}D_{(c}\Gamma_{b)d}^{e})=0,\hskip 20.00003ptQ(D_{(c)}D_{(a}P_{b)c})=0\,.

(3.2)

The equations of the second line can be solved with respect to

D_{(c)}D_{a}D_{b}\xi^{d}
and
D_{(c)}D_{a}D_{b}\lambda
and hence this sub-gPDE is an equivalent reduction, at least locally. It is clear that this reduction does not affect the
\Omega
-sector of the system. In particular, it is perfectly defined even if we let
\Omega
to vanish. In this case the underlying system is strictly speaking not equivalent to GR. Note that the total derivative
D_{a}
is not tangent to this surface.

As part of the coordinates on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
we take the pullbacks of the degree
1
coordinates
\xi^{a},\lambda
and their derivatives
C_{a}{}^{b}\equiv D_{a}\xi^{b}
,
\lambda_{a}\equiv D_{a}\lambda
together with
g_{ab}
,
\Omega
. By some abuse of notation we use the same symbols to denote the degree
1
coordinates and their pullbacks to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
. The same applies to
g_{ab}
. The action of
Q
on these coordinates read as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Qg_{bc}=C_{b}{}^{a}g_{ac}+C_{c}{}^{a}g_{ba}+2\lambda g_{bc},\qquad Q\xi^{b}=\xi^{a}C_{a}{}^{b},\\
&Q\lambda=\xi^{a}\lambda_{a},\qquad Q\lambda^{b}=\lambda^{a}C_{a}{}^{b}+\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\xi^{a}\xi^{d}\mathrm{C}^{b}{}_{ad},\\
&QC_{b}{}^{c}=C_{b}{}^{a}C_{a}{}^{c}+\lambda_{b}\xi^{c}-\lambda^{c}\xi_{b}+\delta_{b}^{c}\lambda_{a}\xi^{a}+\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\xi^{a}\xi^{d}\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{bad},\\
&Qr^{*}\Omega=\xi^{a}r^{*}D_{a}\Omega+\lambda r^{*}\Omega\,,\end{split}

(3.3)

where
\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{bad}
and
\mathrm{C}^{b}{}_{ad}
are respectively the Weyl and Cotton tensors build out of
D_{(c)}g_{ab}
and pulled back to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
. In particular, they posses the standard symmetry properties:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{abcd}=-\mathrm{W}_{bacd}=-\mathrm{W}_{abdc},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{W}_{a[bcd]}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{C}_{abc}=-\mathrm{C}_{acb},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{C}_{[abc]}=0.

(3.4)

Functions
\xi^{a},\lambda,C_{a}{}^{b},\lambda_{a}
exhaust the independent coordinates of degree
1
on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
.

It is clear that the above reduction does not affect the sector of
D_{(a)}\Omega
variables and in this sense amounts to the equivalent reduction of the gPDE
(E_{cl}^{conf},Q)
describing the conformal geometry, which can be identified with
D_{(a)}\Omega
-independent sector of
(E_{cl},Q)
. More geometrically, the initial
(E_{cl},Q)
is a
Q
-bundle over the
(E_{cl}^{conf},Q)
with the fibre being a jet of
\Omega
with coordinates
D_{(a)}\Omega
. Functions on the base can be identified with the functions on the total space that do not depend on
D_{(a)}\Omega
. The above equivalent reduction
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)\subset(E_{cl},Q)
originates from the equivalent reduction of
(E_{cl}^{conf},Q)
to
(E_{\mathrm{red}}^{conf},Q)
and in this form was initially constructed and employed in
[
55
,
56
]
, see also
[
57
,
30
]
. More precisely,

Proposition 3.1
.

(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
is a
Q
-bundle over
(E^{conf}_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
.

Recall that both
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
and
(E^{conf}_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
are
Q
-bundles over
T[1]X
and the projection

(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)\to(E^{conf}_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
is a map of bundles over
T[1]X
.

We refer to
(E^{conf}_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
as to the pre-minimal model of conformal geometry. At the same time, it is natural to call
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
a pre-minimal model of conformal-like off-shell GR. The term pre-minimal reflects that one can still eliminate the metric
g_{ab}
together with the symmetric part of of the ghosts
C_{a}{}^{b}
to obtain the minimal model, see
[
57
,
30
]
for more details on the minimal model.

Finally, recall that the on-shell conformal-like GR is described by
(E_{cl},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl})
, where
\mathcal{I}_{cl}
is generated by (
2.6
) which in turn originate form the Einstein equations. Pulling back
\mathcal{I}_{cl}
to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
gives an ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}}
which, in turn, allows us to define
the pre-minimal model of the on-shell conformal-like GR as an implicit gPDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q,\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}})
.

3.2
Covariant derivatives

Our aim now is to introduce a set of functions on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
that completes
the coordinates involved in (
3.3
) to a useful (possibly overcomplete) coordinate system on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
, compatible with the bundle structure
E_{\mathrm{red}}\to E^{conf}_{\mathrm{red}}
. To this end we need some additional ingredients. Let
C^{M}
denote all the coordinates of ghost degree
1
on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
. Consider the following vector fields:

\displaystyle\nabla_{M}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}},Q].

(3.5)

Note that becuase
C^{M}
exhaust the degree
1
fibre coordinates, vector fields
\nabla_{M}
do not depend on the choice of the degree
0
coordinates completing
C^{M},x^{\mu},\theta^{\mu}
to a coordinate system on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Vector fields
\nabla_{M}
satisfy the following “open-algebra” relations:

\displaystyle[\nabla_{M},\nabla_{N}]=\epsilon^{L}_{MN}\nabla_{L}+(Q\epsilon^{L}_{MN})\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{L}}\,,

(3.6)

where
\epsilon^{L}_{NM}=-\epsilon^{L}_{MN}
are degree zero functions on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
. To see this, note that

\displaystyle[\nabla_{M},\nabla_{N}]=[Q,r_{MN}],\hskip 20.00003ptr_{MN}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}},\nabla_{N}]\,.

(3.7)

Because
\mathrm{gh}(r_{MN})=-1
it can be represented as

\displaystyle r_{MN}=\epsilon_{MN}^{L}\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{L}}\,,

(3.8)

for some degree
0
functions
\epsilon^{L}_{NM}
. Note that
\epsilon^{L}_{NM}=-\epsilon^{L}_{MN}
thanks to

\displaystyle r_{MN}=[\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}},\nabla_{N}]=[\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}},[Q,\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{N}}]]=[\nabla_{M},\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{N}}]=-r_{NM}.

(3.9)

Of course, these considerations apply to any
Q
-manifold whose coordinates are allowed to have degree
0
and
1
only. Such
Q
manifolds are 1:1 with Lie algebroids. The restriction of
\nabla_{M}
to the base, i.e. a submanifolds singled out by
C^{M}=0
, are nothing but the components of the anchor map of this algebroid.

It is convenient to introduce the following notations for
\nabla_{M}
associated to different sectors of ghost variables:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{a}\equiv\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}},Q\right],&\qquad\Delta^{a}{}_{b}\equiv\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{a}{}^{b}},Q\right],\\
\Gamma_{a}\equiv\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{a}},Q\right],&\qquad\Delta\equiv\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda},Q\right]\,.\end{split}

(3.10)

We also employ collective notations
\Delta_{A}
for
\Delta^{a}{}_{b},\Gamma_{a},\Delta
, i.e. all components of
\nabla_{M}
save for
\nabla_{a}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Relations (
3.6
) read explicitly as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\left[\Delta^{a}{}_{b},\Gamma^{c}\right]}=-\delta_{b}^{c}\Gamma^{a},\qquad{\left[\Gamma^{a},\nabla_{b}\right]}=-\mathcal{P}^{ac}_{db}\Delta^{d}{}_{c}+\delta^{a}_{b}\Delta\,,\\
&{\left[\Delta^{a}{}_{b},\nabla_{c}\right]}=\delta_{c}^{a}\nabla_{b},\qquad{\left[\Delta^{a}{}_{b},\Delta^{c}{}_{d}\right]=\delta^{a}_{d}\Delta^{c}{}}_{b}-\delta^{c}_{b}\Delta^{a}{}_{d}\\
&{\left[\nabla_{a},\nabla_{b}\right]=-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cab}\Delta^{c}{}_{d}-\mathrm{C}_{dab}\Gamma^{d}-(Q\mathrm{W}^{d}{{}_{cab})\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{d}}}-(Q\mathrm{C}_{dab})\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{d}}}\,,\\
\end{split}

(3.11)

where
\mathcal{P}^{de}_{ab}\equiv(-g^{de}g_{ab}+\delta^{d}_{a}\delta^{e}_{b}+\delta^{d}_{b}\delta^{e}_{a})
and all other commutators vanish.

Note that vector fields (
3.10
) are well-defined on the subalgebra of degree
0
functions where they coincide with the corresponding vector fields introduced in
[
55
]
while their analogues in the case of GR and YM theory were discussed already in
[
58
]
.

Note that functions of degree
0
are closed under the action of
\nabla_{M}
. Moreover, on this subalgebra
\nabla_{M}
form a representation of the extension of the conformal algebra
o(D,2)
. More precisely, the extension, where the Lorentz subalgebra is extended to a general linear algebra whose associated ghost variables are
C_{a}{}^{b}
. Alternatively,
if one sets to zero all the terms involving curvatures
\mathrm{W}
and
\mathrm{C}
, relations (
3.11
) are just the relation of this Lie algebra. That a Lie algebra and its module appears naturally can be understood from the perspective of
L_{\infty}
-algebra. More precisely, the fibre of our gPDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
(as well as that of
(E,Q)
) can be considered a formal
Q
-manifold and hence is equivalent to an
L_{\infty}
-algebra. At the same tyme an
L_{\infty}
-algebra whose associated
Q
-manifold has coordinates of degree
0
and
1
only, is known to describe deformations of a Lie algebra and its module.

Proof.

One reads off the commutation relations from the relation (
3.3
). For instance, let
r_{ab}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}},\nabla_{b}]
. Because
\mathrm{gh}(r_{ab})=-1
it acts nontrivially on the degree
1
coordinates only. Explicitly, one has

\displaystyle r_{ab}C_{c}{}^{d}=[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}},\nabla_{b}]C_{c}{}^{d}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\nabla_{b}C_{c}{}^{d}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{b}}QC_{c}{}^{d}=\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cba},

(3.12)

\displaystyle r_{ab}\lambda^{c}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{b}}Q\lambda^{c}=\mathrm{C}^{c}{}_{ba}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptr_{ab}\lambda=r_{ab}\xi^{c}=0\,.

(3.13)

so that

\displaystyle r_{ab}=-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cab}\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{d}}-\mathrm{C}^{c}{}_{ab}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{c}}\,,

(3.14)

and hence

\displaystyle[\nabla_{a},\nabla_{b}]=[Q,r_{ab}]=-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cab}\Delta^{c}{}_{d}-\mathrm{C}_{dab}\Gamma^{d}-(Q\mathrm{W}^{d}{{}_{cab})\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{d}}}-(Q\mathrm{C}_{dab})\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{d}}.

(3.15)

The remaining relations are obtained in a similar way.
∎

3.3
Ideals and coordinates

In our studies of the boundary structures the following notion turns out to be useful:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(E,Q,T[1]X)
be a gPDE equipped with a set of functions
f_{\alpha}
and a set of vector fields

V_{i}\in Vect(E)
. We define the ideal
I^{n}(f,V)\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)\,,\,\,n\,{\geqslant}\,0
to be the ideal generated by

\displaystyle V_{i_{1}}\dots V_{i_{k}}f^{\alpha}\,\hskip 10.00002pt0\,{\leqslant}\,k\,{\leqslant}\,n\,.

(3.16)

We assume that
I^{n}
do not restrict the base space.
3
3

3

This can be formulated as the requirement that the image of the zero locus of
I^{n}(f,V)
under the canonical projection
E\to T[1]X
is
T[1]X
.
It is also useful to introduce
I^{\infty}(f,V)
which is generated by
V_{i_{1}}\dots V_{i_{k}}f^{\alpha}
with
k=0,1,2,\ldots
.

Note that
I^{0}(f,V)
does not depend on
V
and hence we also denote it as
I^{0}(f)
. There is a natural filtration on
I^{\infty}(f,V)
:

I^{0}(f)\subset I^{1}(f,V)\subset I^{2}(f,V)\subset\ldots

(3.17)

Note that
V_{j}I^{m}(f,V)\subseteq I^{m+1}(f,V)
.

If
(E,Q)
is the off-shell GR introduced above, we say that
I^{n}(f,D_{a})
is compatible if
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})I^{n}(f,D_{a})\subset I^{n}(f,D_{a})
. It is easy to check that if
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})I^{0}(f)\subset I^{0}(f)
then all
I^{n}(f,D_{a})
are also compatible.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
f_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}(E)
,
\mathrm{gh}(f_{\alpha})=0
be such that
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})I^{0}(f)\subset I^{0}(f)
and

r:E_{\mathrm{red}}\to E
. It follows

\displaystyle r^{*}I^{n}(f,D_{a})=I^{n}(r^{*}f,\nabla_{a}),

(3.18)

where
I^{n}(r^{*}f,\nabla_{a})
is the ideal in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E_{\mathrm{red}})
determined (in the sense of Definition
3.3
) by functions
r^{*}f^{\alpha}
and vector fields
\nabla_{a}\equiv[Q{,}\,\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}]
. Recall, that we assume that the coordinate system on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
in the degree 1 is fixed to be
\xi^{a},D_{a}\xi^{b},\lambda,D_{a}\lambda
pulled-back to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
.

4
4

4

This defines
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial(r^{*}\xi^{a})}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial(r^{*}\xi^{a})}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial(r^{*}\xi^{a})}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial(r^{*}\xi^{a})}$}\kern 1.0pt}
in the unambiguous way
.

Note that the condition of the statement is equivalent to requiring
\Delta,\Delta_{a}{}^{b},\Gamma^{a}
to preserve
I^{0}(f)\subset I^{0}(f)
.

Proof.

The statement is obvious for
n=0
.
For
n=1
we have

\displaystyle Qf=\xi^{a}D_{a}f+I^{0}(f).

(3.19)

Applying
\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{b}}r^{*}
to the above we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{b}r^{*}f=r^{*}D_{b}f+I^{0}(r^{*}f),

(3.20)

where we used
\nabla_{b}f\equiv[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{b}},Q]r^{*}f=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{b}}Qr^{*}f
which in turn employs
\mathrm{gh}(f)=0
. Equation (
3.20
) implies that
r^{*}(I^{1}(f,D_{a}))
can be generated by

r^{*}f^{\alpha}
and
\nabla_{a}r^{*}f^{\alpha}
and hence the statement for
n=1
.

Proceeding further by induction we assume that the statement holds for all
i<N
, i.e.

\displaystyle\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{i}}r^{*}f=r^{*}D_{a_{1}}\dots D_{a_{i}}f+I_{i-1}(r^{*}f,\nabla_{a}),\hskip 10.00002pti<N.

(3.21)

Applying
D_{a_{2}}\dots D_{a_{N}}
to
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})I^{0}(f)\subset I^{0}(f)
and using
D_{a}Q=QD_{a}
and
D_{a}I^{n-1}(f,D_{a})\subseteq I^{n}(f,D_{a})
) one has:

\displaystyle QD_{a_{2}}\dots D_{a_{N}}f-\xi^{a_{1}}D_{a_{1}}\dots D_{a_{N}}f\in I^{N-1}(f,D_{a}).

(3.22)

Finally, applying
\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a_{1}}}r^{*}
gives

\displaystyle\nabla_{a_{1}}r^{*}D_{a_{2}}\dots D_{a_{N}}f-r^{*}D_{a_{1}}\dots D_{a_{N}}f\in r^{*}I^{N-1}(f,D_{a})

(3.23)

which together with the induction assumption leads to

\displaystyle\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{N}}r^{*}f=r^{*}D_{a_{1}}\dots D_{a_{N}}f+I^{N-1}(r^{*}f,\nabla_{a})\,,

(3.24)

so that

r^{*}I^{N}(f,D_{a})=I^{N}(r^{*}f,\nabla_{a})
.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
f_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
,
\mathrm{gh}(f_{\alpha})=0
be such that
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})I^{0}(f)\subseteq I^{0}(f)
. Then

\displaystyle[\nabla_{a},\nabla_{b}]I^{n}(f,\nabla_{c})\subseteq I^{n}(f,\nabla_{c}).

(3.25)

In particular,
I^{\infty}(f,\nabla_{a})
is generated by
\nabla_{(a)}f^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
C^{M}
denote all the degree 1 coordinates but
\xi^{a}
and
\Delta_{M}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}},Q]
. Note that
[\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{,}\,\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}]=-\xi^{a}[\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{,}\,\nabla_{a}]
preserves
I^{n}(f,\nabla)
because any vector field of degree
-1
does so. It follows,
\Delta_{M}
preserves the ideal
as well because
[\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{M}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{,}\,Q-\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}]
clearly does. Finally, according to (
3.11
),
[\nabla_{a}{,}\,\nabla_{b}]
is proportional to
\Delta_{M}
so that (
3.25
) and

\displaystyle\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{m}}I^{n}(f,\nabla_{c})=\nabla_{(a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{m})}I^{n}(f,\nabla_{c})+I^{m+n-1}(f,\nabla_{c})\,.

(3.26)

∎

Corollary 3.6
.

Functions
\nabla_{(a)}(r^{*}\Omega)
can be taken as a part of a coordinate system on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Indeed, because
E_{cl}
is a bundle over
E^{conf}
and the equivalent reduction
r:E_{\mathrm{red}}\to E_{cl}
does not affect the fibres,
r^{*}(D_{(a)}\Omega)
are coordinates on the fibres of
E_{\mathrm{red}}\to E^{conf}_{\mathrm{red}}
. Thanks to
3.4
, the restriction of
r^{*}(I^{\infty}(\Omega,D_{a}))
to a fibre is isomorphic to the restriction of
I^{\infty}(r^{*}(\Omega),\nabla_{a})
to the same fibre. Moreover, thanks to Proposition
3.4
the ideals generated by
r^{*}D_{(a)}(\Omega-1)
and
\nabla_{(a)}r^{*}(\Omega-1))
are the same and so are their restrictions to a given fibre. This implies that
\nabla_{(a)}r^{*}\Omega
restricted to the fibre define a coordinate system therein.

Using Proposition
3.4
we can also characterize more explicitly the pullback
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}}
to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
of the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{cl}
generated by constraints (
2.6
) which, in turn, are
determined by the Einstein equations. More precisely,

Corollary 3.7
.

On
E_{\mathrm{red}}
we have:

\displaystyle r^{*}G_{ab}=\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}r^{*}\Omega+r^{*}(\rho g_{ab}),

(3.27)

\displaystyle r^{*}\rho=-\frac{1}{D}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{a}r^{*}\Omega\,

(3.28)

and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}}=r^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{cl})=I^{0}(r^{*}S)\cup I^{\infty}(r^{*}G_{ab},\nabla_{a})\,,

(3.29)

Proof.

To prove it is sufficient to observe that
r^{*}\Gamma_{ab}^{c}=r^{*}P_{ab}=0
by the definition of
E_{\mathrm{red}}
and that
r^{*}D_{a}D_{b}\Omega=\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}r^{*}\Omega
.
∎

It was shown in
[
55
,
56
]
that
\nabla_{(a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{a_{k+1}a_{k+2})c}
for
k\,{\geqslant}\,0
can be taken as coordinates on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
in the sector of
W
variable. In fact this can be also inferred as a Corollary of Proposition
3.5
. All in all we have the following

Proposition 3.8
.

As coordinates on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
in the sector of degree
1
variables one can take
\xi^{a},C_{a}{}^{b},\lambda,\lambda_{a}
pulled back to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
while in the sector of degree
0
one can take:

g_{ab}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{a_{k+1}a_{k+2})c}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(a)}(r^{*}\Omega)\,,\hskip 20.00003ptk\,{\geqslant}\,0\,.

(3.30)

In this coordinate system the action of
Q
is determined by
[Q{,}\,\nabla_{a}]=0
,
r^{*}\circ Q=Q\circ r^{*}
, relations (
3.3
), and

\displaystyle Q\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{bad}=\xi^{e}\nabla_{e}\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{bad}-C_{e}{}^{c}\mathrm{W}^{e}{}_{bad}+C_{b}{}^{e}\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{ead}+C_{a}{}^{e}\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{bed}+C_{d}{}^{e}\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{bae}

(3.31)

and

Q(r^{*}\Omega)=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}(r^{*}\Omega)+\lambda(r^{*}\Omega)\,.

(3.32)

Note that to determine explicitly the action of
Q
in terms of this coordinate system it is useful to employ relation (
3.11
).
Recall that by some abuse of notations we systematically omit
r^{*}
in the notations for degree
1
coordinates, e.g.
\xi^{a}
in the above Proposition mean
r^{*}(\xi^{a})
, and denote by
Q
the
Q
vector field on
E
and its restriction to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
as well. In what follows we also omit
r^{*}
in
r^{*}(\Omega)
, and
r^{*}(G_{ab})
,
r^{*}S
as we will not employ coordinates on the initial
E_{cl}\supset E_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Finally, starting from
Q^{2}C_{a}{}^{b}=0
and
Q^{2}\lambda_{a}=0
one can can obtain the following “Bianchi identities”:

\displaystyle\nabla_{[a}\mathrm{W}_{bc]de}-\mathrm{C}_{d[ab}g_{c]e}+\mathrm{C}_{e[ab}g_{c]d}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{[a}\mathrm{C}^{b}{}_{cd]}=0\,,

(3.33)

where
C_{bcd}\equiv-\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla_{a}W^{a}{}_{bcd}
. Thanks to these identities, we can express
\nabla_{(a)}\mathrm{W}_{bcde}
in terms of the coordinates
\nabla_{(a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{a_{k+1}a_{k+2})c}
introduced above. Equivalently, the
algebra of functions on
E_{\mathrm{red}}

can be identified as the quotient of the algebra generated by ghosts and

\nabla_{(a)}\mathrm{W}_{bcde}
by the relations generated by the
\nabla_{(c)}
-prolongations of the above Bianchi identities.

4
Boundary system

4.1
Natural gPDEs on the boundary

Until now we studied gravity on the manifold
X
with or without boundary and have equivalently reformulated it as a gauge PDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q,\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}})
subject to the additional condition
\Omega>0
. The important point is that negative powers of
\Omega
enter neither
Q
nor the generators of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}}
. This means that one can consistently extend
E_{\mathrm{red}}
by taking the range of
\Omega
to be
\Omega\,{\geqslant}\,0
rather than
\Omega>0
. In other words, from now on we add the boundary at
\Omega=0
to our total space
E_{\mathrm{red}}
and keep the same notation for it. More precisely, we assume that
X
is a conformally compactified spacetime and
\Sigma\equiv\partial X
is its boundary.

In this way we are in the setting of the general discussion of Section
2.2
. In particular, restricting
E_{\mathrm{red}}
to the interior of
X
and at the same time restricting to the interior of the fibres, i.e. requiring
\Omega>0
, gives precisely the equivalent reformulation of GR on
Int(X)
. At the same time the induced gPDE on the
Q
-corner (i.e. the
Q
-boundary of the restriction of
E_{\mathrm{red}}
to
T[1]\Sigma
) is the gPDE that describes the boundary structure.

Before passing to the gPDE on the boundary that describes the boundary structure it is convenient to describe in some more details the pullback of
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q,\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{red}})
to the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
. This gPDE plays an important technical role in our analysis and we denote it by
(E,Q,\mathcal{I})
in what follows. Let us stress that this gPDE is defined over the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
and the range of the fibre coordinate
\Omega
is
\Omega\,{\geqslant}\,0
.

From now on we only encounter gPDEs defined over the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
and hence it is legitimate to systematically exclude
T[1]\Sigma
from the notations. Moreover, unless otherwise specified, manifolds, bundles, etc. are always bundles, usually
Q
-bundles, over
T[1]\Sigma
. For instance, a bundle
\pi:E^{\prime}\to E
means that both
E
and
E^{\prime}
are bundles over
T[1]\Sigma
and
\pi
respects the bundle structure. In addition, defining a coordinate system on
E
we often do not explicitly mention coordinates originating form the base space as these are always taken to be
x^{\mu},\theta^{\mu}
,
\mu=0,\ldots D-2
. Of course, the fibre coordinates are inherited from
E_{\mathrm{red}}
and hence the notations are unchanged.

Because our aim is to study the gauge PDE induced by the conformal like GR on the boundary it is useful to work in the basis adapted to this setup. More precisely, all the relations of the previous section were invariant under the general linear transformations acting on all the coordinates with indices
a,b,\ldots
as the respective tensors
5
5

5

This can be naturally promoted to local
gl(D)
-symmetry but we do not need this now.
Now we want to treat one direction in the underlying linear space as the distinguished one and introduce the adapted basis where the components of e.g.
\xi^{a}
split into
\xi^{\Omega}
and
\xi^{A}
,
A=0,1,\ldots D-2
. For any (coordinate) function
f
we denote
f^{(N)}\equiv(\nabla_{\Omega})^{N}f
, in particular
f^{(0)}\equiv f
.
For the future use it is convenient to introduce the decomposition of the ideals of the type described in
3.5
, which is adapted to the decomposition
\xi^{\Omega},\xi^{A}
. Let
I^{\infty}(f,\nabla)
be an ideal generated by
\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\ldots f^{\alpha}
and such that
(Q-\xi^{a}\nabla_{a})I^{0}(f)\subset I^{0}(f)
then

I^{k}(f,\nabla_{a})\subset I^{(k)}(f,\nabla_{A}),\hskip 20.00003ptI^{(N)}(f,\nabla_{A})\equiv\bigcup_{i=0}^{N}I^{\infty}(f^{(i)},\nabla_{A}\}\,.

(4.1)

Indeed,
[\nabla_{a}{,}\,\nabla_{b}]I^{k}(f,\nabla_{c})\subset I^{k}(f,\nabla_{c})
and hence any generator of
I^{k}(f,\nabla_{c})
can be represented as a linear combination of generator of
I^{(k)}(f,\nabla_{A})
by taking all
\nabla_{\Omega}
to the right.

Another ingredient we need in what follows is the degree
-1
vector fields
\nu^{(N)}_{a}
defined in
E
as

\nu^{(N)}_{a}\equiv ad_{\nabla_{\Omega}}^{N}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}})\equiv[\nabla_{\Omega},\dots,[\nabla_{\Omega},\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}]],\hskip 10.00002pt\nu^{(0)}_{a}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}

(4.2)

Note that
\nu^{(N)}_{\Omega}=0
if
N>0
. It is also useful to define

\mathcal{D}_{a}^{(N)}\equiv[Q{,}\,\nu^{(N)}_{a}]=ad^{N}_{\nabla_{\Omega}}(\nabla_{A})=[\nabla_{\Omega},\dots,[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{a}]]\,.

(4.3)

Vector fields
\mathcal{D}_{a}^{(N)}
can be used to represent
[\nabla^{N}_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]
. More precisely,

Proposition 4.1
.

\displaystyle[\nabla^{N}_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-i)}_{A}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega},

(4.4)

where
C^{i}_{N}=\frac{N!}{(N-i)!i!}
are the binominal coefficients.

Proof.

Proved by induction starting from:

\displaystyle[\nabla^{2}_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]=\nabla_{\Omega}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]+[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{\Omega}=\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{A}+2\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{A}\nabla_{\Omega}.

(4.5)

Suppose
[\nabla_{\Omega}^{N},\nabla_{A}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-i)}_{A}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}
for a given
N
. Then

[\nabla_{\Omega}^{N+1},\nabla_{A}]=\nabla_{\Omega}[\nabla_{\Omega}^{N},\nabla_{A}]+[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla^{N}_{\Omega}=\\
=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-i+1)}_{A}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i+1}+\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(1)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}=\\
=\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N+1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}(C^{i}_{N}+C^{i-1}_{N})\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N+1-i)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i+1}+(N+1)\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(1)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N+1}\mathcal{D}^{(N+1-i)}_{A}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}

(4.6)

∎

It particular, for a general
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
one has:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f=\nabla_{A}f^{(N)}+[\nabla_{\Omega}^{N},\nabla_{A}]f=\nabla_{A}f^{(N)}+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-i)}_{A}f^{(i)}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-i)}_{A}f^{(i)}\,.

(4.7)

Finally, let us give the recursive formula for
\nu_{A}^{(N)}
:

\nu^{(N)}_{A}=-(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{c\Omega A}+(N-1)\mathcal{P}^{db}_{c\Omega}\nabla^{N-2}_{\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{d\Omega A})\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{b}}-\\
-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{d\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{d}}+(N-1)\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}+\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N-1}(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-1}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\frac{N-i-1}{i+1}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-2}\mathcal{P}^{db}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{{d\Omega A}})\nu^{(i-1)}_{b},\quad N\,{\geqslant}\,1\,,

(4.8)

where
\mathcal{P}^{de}_{ab}\equiv(-g^{de}g_{ab}+\delta^{d}_{a}\delta^{e}_{b}+\delta^{d}_{b}\delta^{e}_{a})
.

4.2
Boundary GR

The induced gPDE on the boundary is the sub-gPDE of
E\equiv i^{*}E_{\mathrm{red}}
,
i:T[1]\Sigma\hookrightarrow T[1]X
singled out by the following boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\Omega=0,\hskip 20.00003ptQ\Omega=0\,,

(4.9)

along with the extra requirement
\nabla_{a}\Omega\neq 0
. This is of course the realisation of the Penrose notion of asymptotic boundary within the present approach. Conditions (
4.9
) single out the
Q
-boundary of the fibre of
E
while
\nabla_{a}\Omega\neq 0
ensures that
\Omega
can be promoted to an eligible boundary-defining function.
6
6

6

More precisely, if
\sigma
is a solution to the off-shell gPDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
then this condition indeed ensures that
\sigma^{*}(\Omega)
has a nonvanishing gradient at the boundary.

It is also convenient to equivalently reduce the resulting gPDE by restricting to the subbundle
E_{B}\subset E
singled out by (
4.9
),
\nabla_{a}\Omega\neq 0
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Omega^{(1)}=g_{\Omega\Omega},\qquad C_{\Omega}{}^{\Omega}-g^{\Omega\Omega}\xi^{b}\nabla_{b}\Omega^{(1)}+\lambda=0\,,\\
&\nabla_{A}\Omega=0,\qquad C_{\Omega}{}^{A}-\xi^{b}\nabla_{b}\nabla^{A}\Omega=0,\\
&\Omega^{(2)}=0,\qquad\lambda_{\Omega}+g^{\Omega\Omega}\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega^{(2)}=0,\\
&g_{\Omega A}=0,\qquad C_{A}{}^{\Omega}+\frac{1}{g_{\Omega\Omega}}(\xi^{b}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{A}\Omega)=0\,.\end{split}

(4.10)

The constraints listed in the right column are obtained by acting with
Q
on the ones in the left and then using (
4.9
)
and the left column. This equivalent reduction is the minor modification of that from
[
30
]
to which we refer for further details. Let us note that
\xi^{\Omega}
reduced to
E_{B}
vanishes.

It is convenient to identify the resulting gPDE
E_{B}
as a
Q
-submanifold in
E
. More precisely, the algebra of functions on
E_{B}
can be seen as a quotient of
C^{\infty}(E)
by the ideal
\mathcal{K}_{B}
generated by the left hand sides of (
4.9
), (
4.10
). In other words, if
b
denotes the embedding
b:E_{B}\hookrightarrow E
then
\mathcal{K}_{B}=\ker b^{*}
.

4.3
Induced Einstein equations

We now recall that what we are interested in is not
E
or
E_{B}
but rather the sub-gPDE

\mathcal{E}\subset E_{B}\subset E
singled out by the restriction of
\mathcal{I}
to
E_{B}
, where
\mathcal{I}
originates from the Einstein equations. We denote by

\mathcal{K}\equiv\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{K}_{B}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
the ideal of functions vanishing on
\mathcal{E}\subset E
. This section is devoted to studying the structure of
\mathcal{K}
and identifying a suitable set of its generators.

It is convenient to introduce filtration components of
\mathcal{K}
as follows:

\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=I^{(N)}(G_{ab},\nabla_{C})\cup I^{0}(S)\cup\mathcal{K}_{B}\,,

(4.11)

where
\mathcal{K}_{B}
denotes the ideal of functions on
E
vanishing on
E_{B}\subset E
.
As
(N)
refers to
(\nabla_{\Omega})^{N}
, the ideal

\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
can be understood as

\mathcal{K}_{B}
extended by the leading part of the Einstein equations in the sense of near-boundary expansion. We have the following:

Lemma 4.2
.

1.

Vector fields
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt},\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
and
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
on
E
preserve
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.

2.

Q
preserve
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.

3.

\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
is generated by
\mathcal{K}_{B}
and the following functions:

\displaystyle\nabla_{(A)}\Omega,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{(A_{1}\ldots A_{n}B)}\Omega^{(1)},\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(2)}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{(A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}J^{B}{}_{C)D}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptg_{\Omega\Omega}-\widetilde{\Lambda}\,,

(4.12)

where
J^{B}{}_{CD}\equiv\mathrm{W}^{B}{}_{C\Omega D}
,
\Omega^{(k)}=(\nabla_{\Omega})^{k}\Omega
, and
n\,{\geqslant}\,0
.

In the Lemma and in what follows we use the rescaled cosmological constant
\widetilde{\Lambda}
defined as:
7
7

7

Note that in the standard AdS/CFT notation,
l^{2}\equiv-\frac{2\Lambda}{(D-1)(D-2)}
, and thus
\widetilde{\Lambda}\equiv-l^{2}
.

\widetilde{\Lambda}\equiv\frac{2\Lambda}{(D-1)(D-2)}

(4.13)

Corollary 4.3
.

1) and 2) immediate imply that
\nabla_{A}\equiv[Q,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}]
,
\Gamma_{A}\equiv[Q,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{A}}]
,
\Delta^{A}{}_{B}\equiv[Q,\dfrac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}]
preserve
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.

Proof.

To prove 1) let us note that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt},\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
and
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
are of degree
-1
it is enough to show that these vector fields preserve
\mathcal{K}_{B}
modulo
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
because
I^{(0)}(G_{ab},\nabla_{C})\cup I^{0}(S)
is generated by degree
0
functions. In its turn
\mathcal{K}_{B}
is generated by the left-hand sides of (
4.9
), (
4.10
). Acting on them by
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
one readily finds that the result belongs to
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
. For instance,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}(C_{\Omega}{}^{\Omega}-\xi^{b}\nabla_{b}\Omega^{(1)}+\lambda)=-\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}\in\mathcal{K}^{(0)}

(4.14)

In a similar way one proves the statement for
\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{A}}
.

To prove 2) we need to show that
Q
preserves
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
. Because
Q
preserves
\mathcal{K}_{B}
it is enough to check
Q
-invariance of
I^{(0)}(G_{ab},\nabla_{C})\cup I^{0}(S)
up to
\mathcal{K}_{B}
. First, a straightforward computation shows that
\nabla_{A}
preserves
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
. Then

\displaystyle Q\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}G_{bc}=\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}(QG_{bc})

(4.15)

In fact
QG_{bc}\in\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
because
(Q-\xi^{a}\nabla_{a})G_{bc}\subset I^{0}(G_{bc})
and
\xi^{\Omega}\Omega^{(1)}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}
. Analogously, one has
QS\in\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.

To prove 3) let us first show that using the freedom of adding functions from
\mathcal{K}_{B}
the generators of the ideal
I^{(0)}(G_{bc},\nabla_{A})
can be brought to a simple form. Namely, using
g_{\Omega A}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}
and
G_{bc}=\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}\Omega+g_{ab}\rho
, where
\rho=-\frac{1}{D}(\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla^{\Omega}\Omega+\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\Omega)
one gets:

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}G_{\Omega\Omega}=\nabla_{(C)}(\frac{D-1}{D}\Omega^{(2)}-\frac{g_{\Omega\Omega}}{D}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\Omega)+\mathcal{K}_{B},

(4.16)

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}G_{A\Omega}=\nabla_{(C)}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}+\mathcal{K}_{B},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}G_{AB}=\nabla_{(C)}(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega-\frac{g_{AB}}{D}(\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla^{\Omega}\Omega+\nabla_{D}\nabla^{D}\Omega))+\mathcal{K}_{B}

(4.18)

Using the first equation in the last one and employing (
3.11
), from which it follows that

\displaystyle[\nabla_{C},\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}]\Omega=[\nabla_{C},\nabla_{A}]\nabla^{A}\Omega=W^{A}{}_{bCA}\nabla^{b}\Omega-C^{A}{}_{CA}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}_{B}

(4.19)

one finds that
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
can be generated by
\mathcal{K}_{B}
,
S
and

\displaystyle\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}\Omega^{(2)}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}\Omega\,.

(4.20)

Then we employ a version of Proposition
3.5
to show that generators (
4.20
) can be replaced with

\displaystyle\nabla_{(A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n})}\Omega^{(2)},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n})}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n})}\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}\Omega\,.

(4.21)

It turns out that the “hook” component of the
\nabla_{(A)}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)}
does not give new generators as

[\nabla_{A},\nabla_{B}]\Omega^{(1)}=-\mathrm{W}^{c}{}_{\Omega AB}\nabla_{c}\Omega-\mathrm{C}_{\Omega AB}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}_{B}.

(4.22)

In a similar way

\displaystyle[\nabla_{A},\nabla_{B}]\nabla_{C}\Omega=-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{CAB}\nabla_{d}\Omega-\mathrm{C}_{CAB}\Omega=-J_{ABC}+\mathcal{K}_{B}\,,

(4.23)

giving the following addition generators:

\nabla_{(A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n-1}}J^{B}{}_{A_{n})C}
.
∎

The reason why the ideal
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
turns out to be extremely useful is the following:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
f_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
be such that

\displaystyle(Q-\xi^{a}\nabla_{a})f_{\alpha}\in I^{0}(f_{\alpha}).

(4.24)

Then

\displaystyle QI^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})\subset I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})\cup\mathcal{K}^{(0)}\,,

(4.25)

recall that
I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})\equiv\cup_{i=0}^{N}I(f^{(i)}_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})
,
f_{\alpha}^{(i)}\equiv(\nabla_{\Omega})^{i}f_{\alpha}
.

In other words the ideals
I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})
are
Q
-invariant up to
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
or, equivalently,
Q
is tangent to the zero locus of
I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})\cup\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.

Proof.

Applying
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}
to (
4.24
) gives:

\displaystyle Qf^{(N)}-\xi^{\Omega}f^{(N+1)}\in I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A}).

(4.26)

and hence

\displaystyle Qf^{(N)}\in I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})\cup\mathcal{K}_{B}\,.

(4.27)

Acting on this with
\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}
and using the Corollary
4.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle Q\nabla_{A_{1}}\dots\nabla_{A_{n}}f_{\alpha}^{(N)}\in I^{(N)}(f_{\alpha},\nabla_{A})\cup\mathcal{K}^{(0)}_{.}

(4.28)

∎

We now take into account the remaining components of the equations of motion, which are encoded in
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
,
N>0
. A direct consequence of Proposition
4.4
is that
Q\mathcal{K}^{(N)}\subset\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
,
N\,{\geqslant}\,0
. Also we have the following important:

Theorem 4.5
.

(Subleading Einstein equations
)

1.

Vector fields
\nu_{A}^{(N)}
defined on
E
by (
4.2
) preserve
\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}

2.

The ideal
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
is generated by
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
and the following functions:

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}\Omega^{(i+2)},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(C)}O_{AB}^{(i-1)}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\ i\,{\leqslant}\,N\,,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}O^{(D-3)}_{A}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{(present for $N\,{\geqslant}\,D-1$ only!)}\,.

(4.30)

where

\displaystyle O_{A}^{(j)}\equiv\nabla_{\Omega}^{j}(\nabla^{B}T_{BA}),

(4.31)

\displaystyle O_{AB}^{(j)}\equiv(D-3-j)T_{AB}^{(j)}-j\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{j-1}\nabla^{C}J_{CBA}\,.

Note that
\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}\equiv[\nu_{A}^{(N)},Q]
also preserves
\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}
thanks to 1.

Proof.

As the theorem is mainly technical, the proof is given in Appendix
A
.
∎

4.4
Off-shell boundary system

As a result of the previous sections, we obtain an infinite chain of ideals in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
associated with the
(i)
-subleading orders of the Einstein equations:

\displaystyle 0\subset\mathcal{K}^{(0)}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{K}^{(i)}\subset\mathcal{K}^{(i+1)}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{K}.

(4.32)

According to Proposition
4.4
all these ideals are
Q
-invariant ideals. Consequently, we have a dual chain of
Q
-submanifolds of
E

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow E^{(i+1)}\hookrightarrow E^{(i)}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow E^{(0)}\hookrightarrow E\,,

(4.33)

where
E^{(i)}
is the zero locus of
\mathcal{K}^{(i)}
and
\mathcal{E}
is the zero locus of the entire
\mathcal{K}
. In other words,
\mathcal{E}
is the gPDE induced on the boundary by the asymptotically AdS gravity. In what follows we denote as
b_{i}
the embedding of
E^{(i)}
into
E
.

It turns out that only
E^{(i)}
with
i\,{\leqslant}\,D-3
are actually useful for our analysis. As for
\mathcal{E}
, it is the zero locus of non-linear constraints and we are going to treat it as a sub-gPDE of certain quotient of
E^{(D-3)}
. Moreover, this quotient turns out to be an off-shell gPDE and hence simplifies a lot the analysis of the field theory described by
\mathcal{E}
. In particular, the explicit form of the equations of motion encoded in
\mathcal{E}
can be derived systematically.

As a first step, we provide a more detailed description of
E^{(0)}
. Recall that

E^{(0)}
is a sub-gPDE in
E
singled out by the leading-order Einstein equations:

Proposition 4.6
.

For
D\,{\geqslant}\,5
, as a coordinate system on
E^{(0)}
one can take the following functions on
E

\displaystyle\{\xi^{B},\kern 5.0ptC_{B}{}^{C},\kern 5.0pt\lambda,\kern 5.0pt\lambda^{B},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(N+3)},\kern 5.0ptg_{BC},\kern 5.0pt\nabla_{((A)}\mathrm{W}^{B}{}_{CD)E},\kern 5.0pt\nabla_{(A)}T_{BC}^{(N)},\hskip 10.00002pt|A|\,{\geqslant}\,0,N\,{\geqslant}\,0\}

(4.34)

pulled back to
E^{(0)}
. Here
b_{0}:E^{(0)}\hookrightarrow E
and we use the notations
f^{(N)}\equiv\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}f
,
T_{BC}\equiv W_{\Omega B\Omega C}
. Moreover,
b_{0}^{*}T^{(N)}_{BC}
,
N\,{\geqslant}\,0
are trace-free.

Note that Lemma
4.2
implies that the vector fields
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt},\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{A}{}^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
and
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{B}}$}\kern 1.0pt}

are tangent to
E^{(i)}\subset E
for all
i\,{\geqslant}\,0
. In particular,
\nabla_{A}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}},Q]
is tangent to
E^{(0)}
and we keep denoting its restriction to
E^{(0)}
by
\nabla_{A}
. The coordinates in the degree-
0
sector are generated by
\nabla_{(A)}
applied to the basic coordinates and hence the structure of
Q
is substantially simplified.

Proof.

The ghost degree one sector is obtained directly as the pullback of the ghost degree one coordinates from
E
and taking into account that functions
\xi^{\Omega},C_{\Omega}{}^{A},C_{A}{}^{\Omega},\lambda_{\Omega}
belong to
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
and hence vanish on
E^{(0)}
. Analogously:
b_{0}^{*}g_{\Omega A}=0,b_{0}^{*}g_{\Omega\Omega}=\widetilde{\Lambda}
.

As for the
\Omega
-sector, notice that using Proposition
3.5
we can replace coordinates
\{\nabla_{(a)}\Omega\}
on
E
with
\{\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(N)}\,,N\,{\geqslant}\,0\}
. Furthermore, Lemma
4.2
implies that
\{\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(0)},\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(1)},\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(2)}\}
belong to
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.

In the curvature sector, things are somewhat trickier. To begin with, note that using the symmetries of the Weyl tensor, we can parametrize
\{W_{abcd}\}
as
\{W_{ABCD},J_{ABC},T_{AB}\}
, where
J_{ABC}\equiv W_{AB\Omega C}
and
T_{AB}\equiv W_{\Omega A\Omega B}
.
Next, recall that Bianchi identities (
3.33
) have the form

\displaystyle\nabla_{[a}\mathrm{W}_{bc]de}-\mathrm{C}_{d[ab}g_{c]e}+\mathrm{C}_{e[ab}g_{c]d}=0

(4.35)

and that
C_{bcd}\equiv-\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla_{a}W^{a}{}_{bcd}
. Then we explicitly spilt the indexes into
\Omega,A
and work up to the ideal
I(g_{\Omega A})\subset\mathcal{K}_{B}
generated by
g_{\Omega A}
. Note that
I(g_{\Omega A})
is
\nabla_{b}
-invariant. If three indexes are
\Omega
then all the terms in (
4.35
) vanish identically. For one
\Omega
-index
a=\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}\mathrm{W}_{BCDE}=\nabla_{B}J_{DEC}-\nabla_{C}J_{DEB}+6\mathrm{C}_{D[\Omega B}g_{C]E}-6\mathrm{C}_{E[\Omega B}g_{C]D}\,,

(4.36)

where in view of
C_{bcd}\equiv-\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla_{a}\mathrm{W}^{a}{}_{bcd}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega B}=\frac{1}{D-3}(\nabla_{A}T^{A}{}_{B})+I(g_{\Omega A})\,,\\
&\mathrm{C}_{A\Omega B}=-\frac{1}{D-3}\left(g^{\Omega\Omega}T_{AB}^{(1)}+\nabla_{C}J^{C}{}_{AB}\right)+I(g_{\Omega A}),\\
&\mathrm{C}_{ABC}=-\frac{1}{D-3}(g^{\Omega\Omega}J^{(1)}_{ABC}+\nabla_{D}\mathrm{W}^{D}{}_{ABC})+I(g_{\Omega A}).\end{split}

(4.37)

Note that upon substituting the expression for the Cotton tensor into (
4.36
), the right-hand side of this relation does not involve
\nabla_{\Omega}W^{A}{}_{BCD}
and can be interpreted as its expression in terms of the other variables.

Similarly, in the case where two
\Omega
-indexes are present we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}J_{ABC}=\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC}+6\mathrm{C}_{\Omega[AB}g_{\Omega]C}-6\mathrm{C}_{C[AB}g_{\Omega]\Omega}\,,

(4.38)

giving

\displaystyle\frac{D-4}{D-3}\nabla_{\Omega}J_{ABC}=\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC}+6\mathrm{C}_{\Omega[AB}g_{\Omega]C}+\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla_{D}\mathrm{W}^{D}{}_{CAB}+I(g_{\Omega A})\,.

(4.39)

For
D\,{\geqslant}\,5
this allows us to express
\nabla_{\Omega}J_{ABC}
in terms of the other variables. Note that since
\nabla_{a}
preserves the ideal
I(g_{\Omega A})
, we can proceed analogously for all
\nabla_{(A)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\mathrm{W}^{A}{}_{BCD}
and
\nabla_{(A)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}J_{ABC}
with
N\,{\geqslant}\,1
.

The remaining part of the Bianchi identities can be employed to express all components in terms of the following symmetrized ones:
\nabla_{((A)}W^{B}{}_{C)DE}
and
\nabla_{((A)}J^{B}{}_{C)D}
. Finally, according to Lemma
4.2
we have
\nabla_{(A_{1}\dots A_{N}}J^{B}{}_{C)D}\in\mathcal{K}^{(0)}

so that we indeed arrive at the desired coordinates in the curvature sector.

The vanishing of the trace of
b_{0}^{*}T^{(N)}_{BC}
directly follows from the tracelessness of the original
W^{b}{}_{cde}
and the fact that
g_{\Omega A}\in\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.
∎
