From now on we restrict our general considerations to the case
D\,{\geqslant}\,5
, treating the case
D=4
separately in Section
5.1
. Theorem
4.5
implies that
b_{0}^{*}\mathcal{I}
is generated by
\nabla_{(C)}b_{0}^{*}O^{(D-3)}_{A}
and

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}b_{0}^{*}\Omega^{(j+3)},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(C)}\left((D-3-j)b_{0}^{*}T_{AB}^{(j)}-j\widetilde{\Lambda}b_{0}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{j-1}\nabla^{C}J_{CBA}\right),\hskip 10.00002ptj\,{\geqslant}\,0\,.

(4.40)

Comparing this with the coordinates on
E^{(0)}
given in Proposition
4.6
, we see that these generators (except for the second one with
j=D-3
) can be solved with respect to
T^{(j)}_{AB}
. Strictly speaking, one should also make sure that
b_{0}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{j-1}\nabla^{C}J_{CBA}
does not depend on
b_{0}^{*}T^{(j)}_{AB}
. Although this fact is almost evident from a straightforward counting of the number of
\nabla_{\Omega}
and
\nabla_{A}
acting on the Weyl tensor, the rigorous proof requires a technically involved computation of
J^{(N)}_{ABC}
, which is presented in the Proposition
C.2
in Appendix. Explicitly solving these “simple” equations from
\mathcal{K}
we arrive at:

Definition 4.7
.

Let us call
\widehat{E}
the subbundle of
E^{(0)}
singled out by

\displaystyle\nabla_{(A)}\Omega^{(N+2)}=0\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{(A)}((D-3-j)T_{BC}^{(j)}-j\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{j-1}\nabla^{D}J_{DCB})=0\,\hskip 10.00002ptj\neq D-3\,,N\,{\geqslant}\,0

(4.41)

pulled back to
E^{(0)}
. The embedding
\widehat{E}\to E
is denoted by
\widehat{b}
and for any function
f
on
E
we define
\widehat{f}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}f
. As a fibre coordinate system on
\widehat{E}
we use

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{B},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\lambda},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\lambda}^{B},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{g}_{BC},\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{((A)}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{B}{}_{CD)E},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{BC},\hskip 10.00002pt|A|\,{\geqslant}\,0\,,

(4.42)

where
\mathcal{T}_{AB}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}T_{AB}^{(D-3)}
.

Note that from the tracelessness of the original
W^{a}{}_{bcd}
and the fact that by definition of
\widehat{E}
we have
\widehat{T}^{(0)}_{AB}=0
, it follows that
\widehat{W}^{A}{}_{BCD}
is traceless.

There is an important subtlety to be clarified here: strictly speaking,
\widehat{E}
is not a
Q
-submanifold in
E^{(0)}
since
Q
preserves only the whole ideal
b_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}
. However, there is a natural
Q
-structure on
\widehat{E}

and this is precisely the one employed in defining the coordinates (
4.42
)
through
\nabla_{A}
. It can be constructed as follows: as a first step, we pass from
E^{(0)}
to its sub-gPDE
E^{(D-3)}
by restricting to the zero locus of
b_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}^{(D-3)}
, which is a
Q
-subbundle. As the second step, we note that the following coordinate functions

\displaystyle\xi^{B},\hskip 10.00002ptC_{B}{}^{C},\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda,\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda^{B},\hskip 10.00002ptg_{BC},\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{((A)}\mathrm{W}^{B}{}_{CD)E},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(A)}T_{BC}^{(D-3)},\hskip 10.00002pt|A|\,{\geqslant}\,0

(4.43)

restricted to
E^{(D-3)}
form a subset of the coordinates therein. Moreover, one can verify the subalgebra of functions generated by this subset is closed under
Q
. For all coordinates except
\nabla_{(A)}T_{BC}^{(D-3)}
this follows directly from the
b_{D-3}
-pullback of the
Q
-action on
E
given in (
3.3
), (
3.31
). The case of the
Q
-action on
T_{AB}^{(D-3)}
is technically more involved and relies on the results proved in the Appendix, see Propositions
B.2
and
C.2
. It follows,
E^{(D-3)}
is a
Q
-bundle over
\widehat{E}^{\prime}
which is identified as a manifold whose algebra of functions is generated by
(
4.43
) restricted to
E^{(D-3)}
. Moreover,
\widehat{E}

is a section of
E^{(D-3)}\to\widehat{E}^{\prime}
and hence
E^{(D-3)}
and
\widehat{E}^{\prime}
are diffeomorphic and in this way
\widehat{E}
acquires the
Q
-structure.
More geometrically,
E^{(D-3)}
can be considered as a bundle over
\widehat{E}
by taking as the projection the composition of the projection to
\widehat{E}^{\prime}
and the section map
\widehat{E}^{\prime}\to\widehat{E}
. Then the
Q
-structure on
\widehat{E}
is just the projection that on
E^{(D-3)}
. In other words,
Qf=i^{*}(Q\pi^{*}f)
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\widehat{E})
, where
i
denotes the embedding of
\widehat{E}\to E^{(D-3)}
and
\pi
be a projection
E^{(D-3)}\to\widehat{E}
. The way it is defined guarantees that the restriction of such
Q
on
\widehat{E}
to
\mathcal{E}\subset\widehat{E}
coincides with the restriction of
Q
on
E^{(D-3)}
to
\mathcal{E}\subset E^{(D-3)}
. It follows that implicit gPDE
(\widehat{E},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}})
where
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}=\widehat{b}^{*}\mathcal{I}
is just a way to define the desired gPDE
(\mathcal{E},Q)
. Note that
\widehat{b}^{*}\mathcal{I}=\widehat{b}^{*}\mathcal{K}
.

In order to perform concrete computations on
\widehat{E}
we need an efficient way to transfer the structures from
E
to
\widehat{E}
. If
\widehat{E}
were a
Q
-submanifold of
E
that would have been straightforward. But it is not and a more elaborated technique is in order. Namely, we identify a subalgebra of functions on
E
such that their restrictions to
\widehat{E}
is a
Q
-map. We call
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)

\widehat{E}
-projectable if its restriction to
E^{(D-3)}
is constant along the fibres of
E^{(D-3)}
or, equivalently can be obtained as a pullback from
\widehat{E}
by the projection
E^{(D-3)}\to\widehat{E}
.
8
8

8

The idea here is the same as in the definition of observable is the context of gauge systems. We require the restrictions of
f
to a submanifold (1st class constrained surface in the Hamiltonian approach or stationary surface in the Lagrangian one) to be invariant under the distribution therein (gauge distribution). This can be made precise by picking any smooth extension of the vertical distribution on
E^{(D-3)}
to
E
. Then the projectable functions are those annihilated by the distribution modulo functions vanishing on
E^{(D-3)}
, i.e. precisely the observables from the gauge system perspective.

Examples of
\widehat{E}
-projectable functions include (
4.43
) as well as
T_{AB}^{(N)}
,
J_{ABC}^{(N)}
for
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-3
. A simple criterion: the restriction of a function to
E^{(0)}
does not depend on
T^{(N)}_{AB}
,
\Omega^{(N+2)}
with
N\,{\geqslant}\,D-2
.

Given a vector field tangent to
E^{(D-3)}
and such that its restriction to

E^{(D-3)}
is projectable, it automatically preserves the subalgebra of
\widehat{E}
-projectable functions and hence defines a vector field on
\widehat{E}
in a canonical way. We shall refer to such vector fields on
E
as
\widehat{E}
-projectable and will denote their restriction to
\widehat{E}
by the same symbol. Note that if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)
and
V\in Vect(E)
are
\widehat{E}
-projectable, then we have
V\widehat{f}=\widehat{b}^{*}Vf
. In particular,
Q
on
E
is like that and indeed induces
Q
on
\widehat{E}
in this way i.e.
\widehat{b}^{*}
restricted to
\widehat{E}
-projectable functions is a
Q
-map.
Also, if a ghost degree
-1
vector field
Y
is tangent to
E^{(D-3)}
, then it is automatically tangent to
\widehat{E}\subset E^{(D-3)}
, since the relations defining this submanifold are degree
0
. Consequently, the corresponding
Q
-exact vector fields
[Q,Y]
are
\widehat{E}
-projectable. It then follows from Lemma
4.2
and Theorem
4.5
that the vector fields on
E_{\mathrm{red}}

\nabla_{A}
,
\Gamma_{B}
,
\Delta^{A}{}_{B}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
,
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
, are
\widehat{E}
-projectable, and we will use the same notation for their restrictions to
\widehat{E}
.

As we have just seen the action of
Q

on coordinate functions (
4.43
) pulled back to
\widehat{E}
can be computed on
E^{(D-3)}
which in turn can be computed on
E
. Applying this procedure to (
3.3
) and (
3.31
) and using relations (
4.10
) and Lemma
4.2
, we find that

\displaystyle Q\widehat{g}_{BC}=

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{g}_{AC}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\widehat{g}_{BA}+2\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{g}_{BC},

(4.44)

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\lambda}^{B}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}+\dfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\xi}^{C}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{B}{}_{AC},

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\lambda}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\lambda}_{A},\hskip 10.00002ptQ\widehat{\xi}^{B}=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle Q\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}=

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{C}+\widehat{\lambda}_{B}\widehat{\xi}^{C}-\widehat{\lambda}^{C}\widehat{\xi}_{B}+\delta_{B}^{C}\widehat{\lambda}_{A}\widehat{\xi}^{A}+\dfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\xi}^{D}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}\,

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{E}\nabla_{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}-\widehat{C}_{E}{}^{C}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{E}{}_{BAD}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{EAD}+

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BED}+\widehat{C}_{D}{}^{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAE}\,.

Note that thanks to
[\nabla_{A},Q]=0
, the above coordinates along with the
\nabla_{A}
-prolongations of
\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{B}{}_{CDE}
are closed under
Q
, i.e. the action of
Q
does not involve the remaining coordinates
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{BC}
. Moreover, the underlying
Q
-manifold (whose algebra of function is generated by the above coordinates) coincides with that describing the conformal geometry (see Proposition
3.1

; of course the lowercase indices are to be replaced by the uppercase ones). If we denote by
(E^{conf},Q)
the respective gPDE we have:

Proposition 4.8
.

(\widehat{E},Q)
is a
Q
-bundle over
(E^{conf},Q)
. The fibre coordinates on this bundle are, of course,
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{BC}

On top of having a relatively simple structure,
(\widehat{E},Q)
has an additional very attractive feature of being off-shell, as we going to demonstrate explicitly in Section
5.3
.
From the above analysis it follows that
\mathcal{E}
is the zero locus of the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}\mathcal{I}
, generated by
\nabla_{(C)}O^{(D-3)}_{AB},\nabla_{(C)}O^{(D-3)}_{A}
pulled back to
\widehat{E}
. In what follows we use the notations:

\mathcal{O}_{AB}\equiv-\frac{1}{(D-3)\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widehat{b}^{*}(O^{(D-3)}_{AB})=\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}\nabla^{C}J_{CBA}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{O}_{A}=\widehat{b}^{*}(O^{(D-3)}_{A})=\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}\nabla^{B}T_{BA}\,.

(4.45)

These functions are well-defined as pullbacks of functions from
E
, but it is not entirely obvious what they are in terms of coordinates on
\widehat{E}
. For this, it will be necessary to introduce a certain calculus, which is the subject of the next section. Using this calculus, in Section
5.2
we show that
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
corresponds to the Fefferman-Graham obstruction tensor.

It is obvious that
\widehat{E}
the zero locus of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
coincides with
\mathcal{E}
. As an implicit gPDE what we are interested in is
(\widehat{E},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}})
.

Although their explicit form is not known to us yet, we can already make some general statements:

Proposition 4.9
.

On
\widehat{E}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}{}^{A}=0\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla^{A}\mathcal{O}_{AB}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{O}_{B}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}(D-3)\mathcal{O}_{AB}

(4.46)

where
\Gamma_{A}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial\widehat{\lambda}^{A}},Q]
.

Proof.

The tracelessness condition for
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
follows directly from (
4.45
) together with the fact that
J^{A}{}_{BC}
is traceless (which, in turn, follows from
W^{a}{}_{bac}=0
).

The divergence-free condition is obtained by simply applying
\widehat{b}^{*}
to the formula (
A.24
) that appeared in the proof of Theorem
4.5
. In fact, the origin of this equation can be traced back to the Noether identities for the Einstein equations.

The last formula follows directly from the Proposition
B.3
proven in the appendix.
∎

The first one, as we will see later, is simply the usual divergence-free condition for the obstruction tensor. The second can be interpreted as the statement that the gauge invariance of
\mathcal{O}_{B}
requires imposing
\mathcal{O}_{AB}=0
.

To anticipate a little, functions
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{O}_{AB}
are
constant along the fibres of
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
and hence determine functions on the base
E^{conf}
. It follows they define a
Q
-submanifold
\mathcal{E}^{conf}
(or more precisely a
Q
-subbundle, if we view
E^{conf}
as a bundle over
T[1]\Sigma
) of
E^{conf}
. As we are going to see shortly, this subbundle is precisely the obstruction gPDE, e.g. conformal gravity at the level of equations of motion for
D=5
.

Moreover, functions
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{O}_{A}
restrict fibre coordinates
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{T}_{AB}
only. All in all this means that the gPDE induced on the boundary is itself a bundle over
\mathcal{E}^{conf}
. More precisely, it is a subbundle of the initial bundle
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
pulled back to
\mathcal{E}^{conf}\subset E^{conf}
. In the case of
D=5
this system describes the conserved energy-momentum tensor
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
, as we discuss in more details in the next Section.

4.5
Boundary calculus

Although we have obtained a certain description of the boundary gPDE as
(\widehat{E},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}})
, it is very implicit for the moment. For instance, we do not know the explicit form of the generators
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{O}_{A}
and
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{O}_{AB}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
in terms of the coordinates on
\widehat{E}
. It turns out that the action of
Q
on the coordinates and the constraints themselves can be expressed in terms of the following functions

\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt0\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4

(4.47)

and vector fields

\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt0\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4

(4.48)

defined on
\widehat{E}
. Moreover, these can be explicitly found in terms of the coordinates on
\widehat{E}
via a certain recursive procedure. In what follows we refer to the above system as to
boundary calculus
.

The important technical tool that allows one to represent the objects like
\mathcal{O}_{A}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}\nabla^{A}T_{AB}
in terms of the boundary calculus is the following:

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
f
be a
\widehat{E}
-projectable function on
E
and
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
. Then

\displaystyle\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\widehat{f}^{(i)}=\nabla_{A}\widehat{f}^{(N)}+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\widehat{f}^{(i)}.

(4.49)

Proof.

Follows immediately from Proposition
4.1
and the fact that
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
for
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
are
\widehat{E}
-projectable and hence
\widehat{b}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}=\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}\widehat{b}^{*}
.
∎

Note that an analogous formula holds for all
N
if, instead of
\widehat{E}
, one induces on the on-shell gPDE
\mathcal{E}
.

With the help of this simple Proposition we can express
\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f
in terms of the boundary calculus and the “subleadings”
\widehat{f}^{(i)}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}(\nabla_{\Omega})^{i}f
. In particular, constraints
\mathcal{O}_{A}
and
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
given by (
4.45
) take the form
9
9

9

In the second formula we have already taken into account that the term with
i=0
vanishes thanks to (
4.8
) and
\widehat{T}_{AB}^{(0)}=0
.
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{AB}=\nabla^{C}\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{CAB}+\sum_{i=0}^{D-5}C^{i}_{D-4}\mathcal{D}^{(D-4-i)|C}\widehat{J}^{(i)}_{CAB},

(4.50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}=\nabla^{C}\mathcal{T}_{AC}+\sum_{i=1}^{D-4}C^{i}_{D-3}\mathcal{D}^{(D-3-i)|C}\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{AC}.

(4.51)

In what follows we refer to
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
and
\mathcal{O}_{A}
as to the symbols of the equations of motion for the the boundary system. This is natural because, as we are going to see in Section
5.3
, if
\sigma
is a solution to gPDE
(\widehat{E},Q)
then it is determined by the unconstrained fields parametrizing leading and subleading boundary values. This means that

\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{AB})=0
and
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{A})=0
are differential equations on
\sigma
, ensuring that
\sigma
is a solution to the boundary system , i.e. to the gPDE
(\widehat{E},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}})
.

As for the
Q
-structure on
\widehat{E}
, its action in the conformal geometry sector (the base of
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
) was already determined and is given by (
4.44
) so the only nontrivial computation is the action of
Q
on the coordinate function
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
. We have the following slightly general statement:

Proposition 4.11
.

On
\widehat{E}
we have

\displaystyle Q\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AC}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BA}+\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\Gamma_{A}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}-N\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC},\hskip 10.00002ptN\,{\leqslant}\,D-3

(4.52)

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\sum^{N-2}_{i=0}d^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-2-i)}\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{BC}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widehat{J}^{(N-1)}_{ABC}+\widehat{J}^{(N-1)}_{ACB})\,,\hskip 10.00002ptN\,{\leqslant}\,D-3

(4.53)

where
d^{i}_{N}\equiv C^{i}_{N}(N-1-i)
.

The proof is based on the useful formula

\widehat{b}^{*}[\Gamma^{B},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}]=\widetilde{\Lambda}\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-2-i)|B}\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}+N\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(\widetilde{\Lambda}g^{BC}\Delta^{\Omega}{}_{C}-\Delta^{B}{}_{\Omega}),\hskip 20.00003ptN\,{\leqslant}\,D-2

(4.54)

and is placed in Appendix
B
.

The above discussion confirms that all we need to know is the boundary calculus on
\widehat{E}
. We now turn to the recursive construction of the boundary calculus:

Theorem 4.12
.

(Boundary calculus)

The system
\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}\,,\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}\,,\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}
on
\widehat{E}
satisfy the following relations

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}=\frac{N\widetilde{\Lambda}}{D-3-N}(\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}J_{ABC}),\quad 0\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4,\\
&\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}=\dfrac{N}{N-1}\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})\,,\quad 2\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4,\end{split}

(4.55)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}f=(-\widehat{J}^{(N-1),D}{}_{CA}\Delta^{C}{}_{D}+\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{\Lambda}N}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{CA}\Gamma^{C}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\widehat{T}^{(i),C}{}_{A}\mathcal{D}_{C}^{(N-2-i)})f\,,\quad 1\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4\,.\end{split}

(4.56)

Moreover,

\widehat{J}^{(0)}_{ABC}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\dfrac{1}{D-4}\nabla^{D}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{DCAB}\equiv-\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptT^{(0)}_{AB}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{A}\equiv\nabla_{A}\,,\\

(4.57)

and all the higher order
J^{(\cdot)}_{ABC},T^{(\cdot)}_{BC},\mathcal{D}^{(\cdot)}_{A}
are recursively determined by the above relations where each expression of the form
\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f
is evaluated as

\displaystyle\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\widehat{f}^{(i)}\,,

(4.58)

and the action of
\Gamma_{A}
on
\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}
is given by (
4.53
).

Proof.

The formula for
\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}
is a direct consequence of the definition of
\widehat{E}
, see Definition
4.7
.
The proof of the formula for
\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}
follows from the technically involved Proposition
C.2
placed in the Appendix. Similarly, in Proposition
C.3
the formula for
\nu_{A}^{(N)}
is proven, from which the formula for
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
directly follows. Finally, to prove the iterativity of the proposed procedure, it is important to note that the action of
\Delta^{A}{}_{B}
and
\Gamma_{C}
on functions in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\widehat{E})
can be computed from the action of
Q
on the coordinates given in
(
4.44
)
,
(
4.52
)
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

It follows from the proofs in Appendix
B
and Appendix
C
that formulas
(
4.53
), (
4.54
), (
4.55
) and (
4.56
) remain valid for higher
N
as well, but in this case only modulo the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
(which in fact means that they hold on the on-shell gPDE
\mathcal{E}\subset\widehat{E}
). We do not need this fact in studying gravity and Yang-Mills, but it becomes important for the analysis of the extended GJMS equations in Section
6.2
.

Corollary 4.14
.

Functions

\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}\,,~~\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}\,,~~\mathcal{D}^{(M)}_{A}\widehat{T}^{(L)}_{BC}\,,~~\mathcal{D}^{(M)}_{A}\widehat{J}^{(L)}_{BCD}\,,~~\hskip 20.00003ptM+L\,{\leqslant}\,N

(4.59)

are
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
-independent for
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
and hence are functions pulled back from
E^{conf}
by
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
. In particular, the submanifold
\mathcal{E}^{conf}\subset E^{conf}
singled out by
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{O}_{AB}
is a sub-gPDE.

In this sense the boundary calculus as defined above can be defined solely in terms of the conformal geometry and hence can be considered in the conformal geometry setup. The functions
\widehat{T}_{AB}^{(N)}
for
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
are closely related to what Graham in
[
59
]
reffered to as extended obstruction tensors.

Corollary 4.15
.

The gPDE
\mathcal{E}
induced on the boundary (i.e. a sub-gPDE of
\widehat{E}
singled out by
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
) is a
Q
-bundle over the gPDE
\mathcal{E}^{conf}
.

Indeed, the remaining constraints
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{O}_{A}
do not restrict the base and hence define a subbundle of
\widehat{E}
pulled back to
\mathcal{E}^{conf}
. We can conclude that the boundary system can be seen as the conformal geometry subject to some conformal invariant equations arising form
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
together with the “matter-like” field
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
subject to equations arising from
\mathcal{O}_{A}
.

Another simple consequence of the above theorem is:

Proposition 4.16
.

For any odd integer
i
such that
1\,{\leqslant}\,i\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
we have:

\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{BC}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{A}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{J}^{(i-1)}_{ABC}=0\,.

(4.60)

The proof is relegated to the Appendix
D

5
Boundary structure of gravity

After having developed boundary calculus for asymptotically AdS GR let us now have a close look at the gauge theory induced on the asymptotic boundary. The case of even and odd dimensional boundary are quite different and we treat them separately.

5.1
Odd-dimensional boundary

Now we discuss the case where the boundary dimension
d\equiv D-1
is odd and assume
d\,{\geqslant}\,3
.
Let us first discuss the case
d=3
, which we have excluded since Proposition
4.34
.

Proposition 5.1
.

For
d=3
the boundary gPDE
\widehat{E}
can be described in terms of fibre coordinates

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{B},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\lambda},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\lambda}^{B},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{g}_{BC},\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{((A)}\widehat{J}^{(1)|B}{}_{C)D},\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{BC},\hskip 10.00002pt|A|\,{\geqslant}\,0

(5.1)

where
\mathcal{T}_{BC}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}T^{(1)}_{BC}
, the action of
Q
is

\displaystyle Q\widehat{g}_{BC}=

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{g}_{AC}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\widehat{g}_{BA}+2\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{g}_{BC},

(5.2)

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\lambda}^{B}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}+\dfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\xi}^{C}\widehat{J}^{(1)}{}_{AC}{}^{B},

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\lambda}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\lambda}_{A},\hskip 10.00002ptQ\widehat{\xi}^{B}=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle Q\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}=

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{C}+\widehat{\lambda}_{B}\widehat{\xi}^{C}-\widehat{\lambda}^{C}\widehat{\xi}_{B}+\delta_{B}^{C}\widehat{\lambda}_{A}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\,

\displaystyle QJ^{(1)}_{BCD}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{BCD}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ACD}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{BAD}+\widehat{C}_{D}{}^{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{BCA},

\displaystyle Q\mathcal{T}_{BC}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\mathcal{T}_{AC}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\mathcal{T}_{BA}-\widehat{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{BC}

and we have
\mathcal{O}_{AB}=0
,
\mathcal{O}_{B}=\nabla^{A}\mathcal{T}_{AB}.

Proof.

The main difference here is that in this dimension the Bianchi identities, as follows from (
4.39
), do not fix
J^{(1)}_{BCD}
.
However, by substituting two indices
\Omega
into
\nabla_{[a}C^{b}{}_{cd]}=0
, we observe that
\nabla_{(A)}J^{(N)}_{ABC}
,
N\,{\geqslant}\,2
, are still determined by the Bianchi identities.
Furthermore, from the algebraic symmetries of the Weyl tensor we have that in dimension
d=3

b_{0}^{*}W^{B}{}_{CDE}=0
.
Therefore, using
C_{ABC}=-\nabla_{d}W^{d}{}_{ABC}
, we find that

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{ABC}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\,\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{BCA}

(5.3)

which leads to the formula for
Q\widehat{\lambda}^{B}
. The computation for
Q\mathcal{T}_{AB}
,
QJ^{(1)}_{BCD}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
is straightforward.
∎

Note that again all coordinates except for
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{AB}
can be interpreted as coordinates on the base of the
Q
-bundle
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
, where the action of
Q
on
E^{conf}
coincides with the formulas given for the BRST complex of three-dimensional conformal geometry in
[
55
]
, with
\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}
playing the role of the three-dimensional Cotton tensor.

For a general odd-dimensional boundary, we have:

Proposition 5.2
.

For
d
odd the boundary gPDE
\widehat{E}
introduced in Section
4.4
is such that:

1.

{{\mathcal{O}}}_{AB}=0

2.

\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=0

3.

\mathcal{O}_{A}=\nabla^{B}\mathcal{T}_{BA}

Proof.

Of course, the case
d=3
, as has been shown, satisfies this, and therefore we will prove it for
d>3
.

First of all, Theorem
4.12
and Proposition
5.2
implies:

\frac{D-5}{D-4}\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{ABC}=\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-5}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})=\widehat{b}^{*}([\nabla^{D-5}_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]T_{BC}-[\nabla^{D-5}_{\Omega},\nabla_{B}]T_{AC})\,.

(5.4)

Because
D>5
by the assumption, Proposition
4.16
together with (
4.58
) implies
\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{ABC}=0
. Using this together with, again, Proposition (
4.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{BC}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{ABC}+\widehat{b}^{*}[\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4},\nabla^{A}]J_{ABC}=0\,.

(5.5)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\sum^{D-5}_{i=0}d^{i}_{D-3}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(D-5-i)}\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{BC}+(D-3)\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{ABC}+\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{ACB})=0,\\
&\mathcal{O}_{A}=\nabla^{C}\mathcal{T}_{AC}+\sum_{i=1}^{D-4}C^{i}_{D-3}\mathcal{D}^{(D-3-i)|C}\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{AC}=\nabla^{C}\mathcal{T}_{AC}.\end{split}

(5.6)

∎

Because
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
vanishes identically the gPDE induced on the boundary simply describes conformal geometry with an additional “energy-momentum tensor”
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
subject to the equations of motion emerging from the constraint
\mathcal{O}_{A}\equiv\nabla^{B}\mathcal{T}_{BA}
and its
\nabla_{C}
prolongations. More precisely, the induced boundary gPDE
is a subbundle of
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
singled out by
\mathcal{O}_{A}
and its prolongations.

Moreover, the induced boundary gPDE is a vector bundle over
E^{conf}
because
Q\mathcal{T}_{AB}
is linear in
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{T}_{AB}

as is easily seen from Proposition
4.11
.
In particular, a zero section of this vector bundle is the conformal geometry gPDE
E^{conf}
itself. In particular,

Proposition 5.3
.

For
d
odd, the following functions and vector fields on
\widehat{E}

\widehat{T}^{(2i+1)}_{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{J}^{(2i)}_{ABC}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(2i-1)}_{A}\,,\hskip 10.00002pti\,{\geqslant}\,0\,,

(5.7)

vanish at the zero section of
\widehat{E}\to E^{conf}
, i.e. at
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{T}_{AB}=0
.

The composition of the zero section with
\widehat{b}
realizes
E^{conf}
as a
Q
-submanifold in
E
.

5.2
Even-dimensional boundary

This case is more interesting. Before describing the structure of the boundary gPDE in this case, let us give a couple of low-dimensional examples:

Example 5.4
.

In the case of
D=5
one has

\mathcal{O}_{AB}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{B}_{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{O}_{A}=\nabla^{B}\mathcal{T}_{BA}\,,

(5.8)

where
\widehat{B}_{BC}\equiv\nabla^{A}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{BCA}
is the “symbol of Bach tensor”. Namely, solutions of
E^{conf}
seen as a gPDE are parametrised by configurations of
a metric on
X
along with the choice of local frame. When pulled back by such a solution,
\widehat{B}_{BC}
becomes a Bach tensor of the metric. More detailed discussion of the explicit form of the underlying equations is given in Section
5.3
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle Q\mathcal{T}_{BC}=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\mathcal{T}_{AC}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\mathcal{T}_{BA}-2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\widehat{\lambda}^{A}(\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}+\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{BAC})-2\widehat{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{BC}

(5.9)

which defines gauge transformations of the field
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
. To above assertions are checked by direct computations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{O}_{C}=\nabla^{A}\mathcal{T}_{AC}+\widehat{b}^{*}[\nabla_{\Omega}^{2},\nabla^{A}]T_{AC}=\nabla^{A}\mathcal{T}_{AC},\\
&\mathcal{O}_{BC}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}+\widehat{b}^{*}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla^{A}]J_{ABC}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{B}_{BC},,\end{split}

(5.10)

where we made use of
\widehat{T}^{(0)}_{AC}=0
,
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{A}=0
, and
\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{C}_{CAB}
, see Eqs. (
4.57
) and (
4.16
). The only nontrivial part of the
Q\mathcal{T}_{AB}
computation is:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(0)}_{BC}+2\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}+\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ACB})=-2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}+\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{BAC})\,.

(5.11)

In other words, for
D=5
the boundary gPDE describes Bach-flat geometry (conformal gravity), together with the “subleading” (that corresponds to the boundary energy-momentum tensor in the AdS/CFT context)
\mathcal{T}_{BC}
satisfying the conservation condition. Note, however, that
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=0
is in general not a sub-gPDE of the boundary gPDE, i.e.
Q
is not tangent to it, — as follows from the fact that
\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}
is not zero in general (though it may vanish if an additional condition on the background is imposed). Of course boundary gPDE is a bundle over
\widehat{E}^{conf}
and hence

\widehat{E}^{conf}
can be recovered as the quotient of the boundary gPDE by the fibres.

Proposition 5.5
.

For
D=7
one has:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{O}_{BC}=\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}+2\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{DCAB}\widehat{B}^{AD}+2\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{C}{}^{AD}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{BAD}-4\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{D}{}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{ACD}),\\
&\mathcal{O}_{B}=\nabla^{B}\mathcal{T}_{BC},\\
&\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=6\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}(3\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{B}_{AC}-\nabla_{C}\widehat{B}_{AB}).\end{split}

(5.12)

Proof.

From Proposition
4.16
we have
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{A}=\mathcal{D}^{(3)}_{A}=0
,
\widehat{J}^{(0)}_{ABC}=\widehat{J}^{(2)}_{ABC}=\widehat{T}^{(0)}_{BC}=\widehat{T}^{(1)}_{BC}=\widehat{T}^{(3)}_{BC}=0
, and that
\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}
. Also, analogously to the previous example, using (
4.55
), we obtain
\widehat{T}_{AB}^{(2)}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\widehat{B}_{AB}.

It follows from (
4.50
) and the formulas above that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{BC}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}_{ABC}^{(3)}+3\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}\,.

(5.13)

Using Theorem
4.12
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{ABC}=&\frac{3}{2}\nabla^{A}\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{2}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})=\frac{3}{2}(\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BC}-[\nabla^{A},\nabla_{B}]\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{AC})=\\
&=\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}+\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{DCAB}\widehat{B}^{AD}-2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{DA}{}_{B}(\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CDA}+\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{ADC})\end{split}

(5.14)

where, in the last term, we used Proposition
4.11
, from which
\Gamma_{D}\widehat{T}_{AC}^{(2)}=-2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CDA}+\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{ADC})
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}

\displaystyle=-\widehat{J}^{(1)|D}{}_{E}{}^{A}\Delta^{E}{}_{D}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}+\frac{1}{2\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widehat{T}^{(2)|A}{}_{D}\Gamma^{D}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(-\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{AD}{}_{B}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAD}-\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{AD}{}_{C}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{DAB}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{B}^{AD}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{DCAB})

(5.16)

where we have also made use of
\Gamma^{A}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{BCD}=-\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{A}{}_{BCD}.
Substituting these results (
5.13
), together with the Bianchi identity for the Cotton tensor, yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{BC}=\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}+2\widehat{\mathrm{W}}_{DCAB}\widehat{B}^{AD}+2\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{C}{}^{AD}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{BAD}-4\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{D}{}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{ACD}).

(5.17)

This is precisely the formula for the Fefferman-Graham obstruction tensor in six dimensions, up to corrections by the Schouten tensor which, as we shall see in Section
5.3
, arise on the solution space of the gPDE.
Next, using (
4.50
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{A}=\nabla^{B}\mathcal{T}_{BA}+C^{2}_{4}\mathcal{D}^{(2)|B}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BA}

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(2)|B}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BA}=-\widehat{J}^{(1)|D}{}_{A}{}^{B}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BD}+\frac{1}{2\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{D}{}^{B}\Gamma^{D}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BA}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{BD}{}_{A}\widehat{B}_{BD}-\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}\widehat{B}_{D}{}^{B}(\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{A}{}^{D}{}_{B}+\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{B}{}^{D}{}_{A})=0.

(5.19)

Finally, Proposition
4.11
yields

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=6\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BC}+4\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{ABC}+\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{ACB})=6\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}(3\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{B}_{AC}-\nabla_{C}\widehat{B}_{AB}),

(5.20)

where we used
\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{ABC}=\dfrac{3}{2}b^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{2}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})=\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{B}_{AC})
.
∎

The general situation is described by:

Proposition 5.6
.

For
d
even,
\widehat{E}
describes conformal geometry with an additional field
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
subject to the equations of the following structure:

\displaystyle(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{\frac{D-5}{2}}\widehat{B}_{BC}+\dots=0,

(5.21)

\displaystyle\nabla^{A}\mathcal{T}_{AB}+\sum_{i=0}^{D-4}C^{i}_{D-3}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(D-3-i)}\widehat{T}^{(i)|A}{}_{B}=0\,.

(5.22)

The first equations is
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
-independent and determines a Fefferman-Graham obstruction of the boundary conformal structure to extend to a formal solution to the bulk Einstein equations near the boundary. The second equations is a generalised conservation condition satisfied by the subleading
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
.

Proof.

Only the first formula needs to be proved. Since the conformal-geometry sector is generated by the action of
\nabla_{A}
, it admits a natural filtration which, roughly speaking, counts the number of derivatives (this interpretation will be precisely valid on the solution space of the gPDE). In the present proof we will monitor only the leading order with respect to this filtration and everything below will be denoted by
\dots
. The case
D=5
was already analyzed and is excluded. For
D>5
from Theorem
4.12
we have (here
2\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
):

\displaystyle\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}=\frac{N}{N-1}\nabla^{A}\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})=\frac{N}{N-1}(\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(N-1)}_{BC}-\nabla^{A}\nabla_{B}\widehat{T}^{(N-1)}_{AC})+\dots.

(5.23)

We can rewrite

\displaystyle\nabla^{A}\nabla_{B}\widehat{T}^{(N-1)}_{AC}=\nabla_{B}\nabla^{A}\widehat{T}_{AC}^{(N-1)}+[\nabla^{A},\nabla_{B}]\widehat{T}_{AC}^{(N-1)}=\nabla_{B}\nabla^{A}\widehat{T}_{AC}^{(N-1)}+\dots\,.

(5.24)

But it follows from the proof of Theorem
4.5
that the divergence
\nabla^{A}\widehat{T}_{AB}^{(N-1)}=-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N-2}\mathcal{D}^{(N-1-i)|A}\widehat{T}_{AB}^{(i)}
, and hence it itself belongs to lower-order corrections. Consequently,

\displaystyle\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}=\frac{N}{N-1}\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(N-1)}_{BC}+\dots\,.

(5.25)

Using the formula for
\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}
from Theorem
4.12
, we conclude that (here
\propto
denotes equality up to a nonzero coefficient)

\displaystyle\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{BC}\propto\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}J_{ABC}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(N-1)}_{ABC}+\dots\propto\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(N-2)}_{BC}+\dots\,.

(5.26)

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{BC}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(D-4)}_{ABC}+\dots\propto\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{T}^{(D-5)}_{BC}+\dots\propto(\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A})^{2}\widehat{T}_{BC}^{(D-7)}+\dots\,.

(5.27)

This procedure continues until (depending on the dimension) it terminates at
\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{AB}\propto\widehat{B}_{AB}
.
∎

Finally, similarly to Proposition
5.3
, we have that:

Proposition 5.7
.

For
d
even, the following functions and vector field on
\widehat{E}

\widehat{T}^{(2i+1)}_{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{J}^{(2i)}_{ABC}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(2i+1)}_{A}\,,\hskip 10.00002pti\,{\geqslant}\,0\,.

(5.28)

vanish at zero locus of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
10
10

10

Note that for
2i+1\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
, these functions and vector fields vanish exactly, as follows from Proposition
4.16
.
.

5.3
Solution space of the boundary theory

The on-shell boundary GR is describe by the gPDE
(\mathcal{E},Q)
obtained in the previous Section and it encodes the equations of motion and gauge transformation of the underlying system in the usual way, i.e.
\sigma
is a solution if
\mathrm{d}_{X}\circ\sigma^{*}=\sigma^{*}\circ Q
, see Section
2
. However, this condition typically contains an infinite number of equations on the components of
\sigma
and in this form is not very useful in practice. It is much preferable to work in terms of
\widehat{E}
, which is almost as simple as a jet-bundle. More precisely, if we eliminate the metric
\widehat{g}_{AB}

and the symmetric part of
\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}
then solutions of

\widehat{E}
are 1:1 with normal Cartan connections describing the conformal geometry along with the unconstrained conformal field
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
defined on this background. In the field-theory terminology we are talking about the off-shell system. At the same time, keeping the metric alive turns out to be very convenient and corresponds to describing the same data in terms of not necessary orthonormal frame and, in particular, in the coordinate frame.

To be more precise let us concentrate on the sector of conformal geometry described by
E^{conf}
. The action of
Q
is determined by (
4.44
) which we again list here
11
11

11

In this section, we restrict ourselves to the case
D\,{\geqslant}\,5
; the case
D=4
can be carried out in exactly the same way and uses the action of
Q
given in (
5.2
).
:

\displaystyle Q\widehat{g}_{BC}=

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{g}_{AC}+\widehat{C}_{C}{}^{A}\widehat{g}_{BA}+2\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{g}_{BC},

(5.29)

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\lambda}^{B}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}+\dfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\xi}^{C}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{B}{}_{AC},

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\lambda}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\lambda}_{A},\hskip 10.00002ptQ\widehat{\xi}^{B}=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle Q\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}=

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{C}+\widehat{\lambda}_{B}\widehat{\xi}^{C}-\widehat{\lambda}^{C}\widehat{\xi}_{B}+\delta_{B}^{C}\widehat{\lambda}_{A}\widehat{\xi}^{A}+\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\xi}^{D}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}\,

\displaystyle Q\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}^{E}\nabla_{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}-\widehat{C}_{E}{}^{C}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{E}{}_{BAD}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{EAD}+

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BED}+\widehat{C}_{D}{}^{E}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAE}\,,

and
[Q{,}\,\nabla_{A}]=0
. We introduce a specal notation for the frame field parametrizing configurations for
\xi^{A}
:
\sigma^{*}\xi^{A}\equiv e^{A}\equiv e^{A}{}_{\mu}\theta^{\mu}
.

Analysing the gauge transformations with parameters associated to ghosts
C^{A}{}_{B}
one finds that
any configuration of
e^{A}_{\mu}(x)
can be locally gauge fixed to
e^{A}{}_{\mu}=\delta^{A}_{\mu}
, where
\mu
is a base index. Moreover, this completely eliminates the gauge freedom associated to
\bar{C}_{A}{}^{B}
. Of course, this gauge choice simply corresponds to passing to the coordinate frame. In the same way analysing the gauge freedom associated to
\widehat{\lambda}_{A}
ghosts, one finds that the field
\sigma^{*}(\widehat{\lambda})
can be set to zero. In what follows we actively use this gauge and call it “metric-like”. In this gauge
g_{AB}(x)\equiv\sigma^{*}(\widehat{g}_{AB})
becomes a tensor field on
\Sigma
naturally interpreted as metric. Similarly, slightly abusing notation, we will drop the hat when passing to the solution space and
\mathbf{T}_{AB}(x)\equiv\sigma^{*}\mathcal{T}_{AB}
.

Note that an alternative interpretation of
E^{conf}
is based on Cartan geometry while the resulting formulation of the underlying gauge-field theory is usually refereed to as frame-like one in the literature. This is constructed by setting
g_{AB}(x)=\eta_{AB}
, where
\eta_{AB}
is the constant Minkowski metric, by exploiting the gauge freedom encoded in the symmetric part of
\bar{C}_{A}{}^{B}
. In this gauge the spectrum of ghosts precisely corresponds to the conformal algebra
o(D-1,2)
and the respective
1
-form is to be identified with the Cartan connection encoding the conformal structure, i.e. the
o(D-1,2)
-valued 1-form whose curvature is restricted by (
5.29
). In addition one typically uses an additional gauge where
\lambda=0
. In particular, in this case
\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}
is identified with the Weyl tensor in the frame-like formulation of conformal geometry.

Working in the “metric-like” gauge
e^{A}{}_{\mu}=\delta^{A}_{\mu}
(i.e.
\sigma^{*}\widehat{\xi}^{A}=\theta^{A}
) and
\lambda=0
, we introduce a notation for ghost degree
1
coordinates on
\widehat{E}

C^{I}
:
\sigma^{*}\widehat{C}^{I}\equiv C^{I}{}_{|B}\theta^{B}
. The equation
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}\widehat{\lambda}=\sigma^{*}Q\widehat{\lambda}
implies:

\displaystyle\lambda_{[A|B]}=0.

(5.30)

In a similar way,
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}\widehat{\xi}^{A}=\sigma^{*}Q\widehat{\xi}^{A}
implies

\displaystyle C_{[A}{}^{B}{}_{|C]}=0.

(5.31)

Furthermore,
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}\widehat{g}_{AB}=\sigma^{*}Q\widehat{g}_{AB}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{C}g_{AB}=C_{A}{}^{D}{}_{|C}g_{DB}+C_{B}{}^{D}{}_{|C}g_{AD}\,,

(5.32)

together with (
5.31
), giving

\displaystyle C_{A}{}^{B}{}_{|C}=\Gamma_{AC}^{B}{}[g],

(5.33)

where
\Gamma_{AC}^{B}{}[g]
are the Christoffel symbols constructed from the metric
g_{AB}(x)\equiv\sigma^{*}\widehat{g}_{AB}
.

Considering next equations
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}=\sigma^{*}Q\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}
one finds

\displaystyle\partial_{[A}\Gamma^{C}_{D]B}=\Gamma^{E}_{B[A}\Gamma^{C}_{D]E}+\lambda_{B|[A}\delta^{C}_{D]}-\lambda^{C}{}_{|[A}g_{D]B}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{*}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}.

(5.34)

where we made use of (
5.33
). Introducing
the Riemann curvature of the Levi-Civita connection
\Gamma
the above equations can be rewritten as

\displaystyle R^{C}{}_{BAD}[g]=\sigma^{*}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{C}{}_{BAD}+2\lambda_{B|[A}\delta^{C}_{D]}-2\lambda^{C}{}_{|[A}g_{D]B}.

(5.35)

This expression is the standard decomposition of the Riemann tensor in terms of the Schouten and Weyl tensors and implies that
W^{B}{}_{CAD}[g]\equiv\sigma^{*}\widehat{W}^{B}{}_{CAD}
is indeed the Weyl tensor of the metric
g_{AB}(x)
, while
\lambda_{A|B}
is the Schouten tensor (up to a numerical factor). Indeed, taking a trace gives

\displaystyle R_{BD}[g]=(2-d)\lambda_{B|D}-\lambda^{A}{}_{|A}g_{BD}

(5.36)

which is the standard rewriting of the Ricci tensor in terms of the Schouten tensor and means

\displaystyle\lambda_{A|B}=-P_{AB}[g],

(5.37)

where
P_{AB}[g]
is the Schouten tensor built from the metric. Then equation
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}\widehat{\lambda}_{A}=\sigma^{*}Q\widehat{\lambda}_{A}
gives

\displaystyle 2\nabla_{[A}^{g}P_{B]C}=-\sigma^{*}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}\,,

(5.38)

where
\nabla_{\mu}^{g}
is the usual Levi-Civita covariant derivative. This is the standard expression of the Cotton tensor
C_{CAB}[g]\equiv\sigma^{*}\widehat{C}_{CAB}
through the Schouten tensor.

Finally, by considering the equations involving coordinates
\nabla_{(C)}\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{D}{}_{ABC}
one finds that they simply determine the respective fields in terms of the derivatives of the metric. In other words,
E^{conf}
describes the unconstrained conformal geometry on the boundary.

Remark 5.8
.

The above discussion is a particular explicit realisation of the general feature of gPDE approach that the metric-like and frame-like formulations of a given theory can be unified and, moreover, the frame-like formulation can be systematically derived starting from the metric-like. This is achieved by considering the jet-bundle BV formulation of the metric-like system as a gauge PDE and taking its maximal equivalent reduction (often called minimal model), giving the frame-like formulation of the system. In the case of conformal geometry this path is explained in
[
57
,
30
]
. The general procedure can be found in
[
36
,
53
,
54
]
, see also
[
60
]
. In the linearised setup the construction of was already in
[
37
]
. Having said this, let us stress, however, that what we highlight now is that the metric-like formulation is also naturally encoded in the slight extension of the minimal model extended by
\widehat{g}_{AB}
and the symmetric part of the ghosts
\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}
.

Now we get back to the entire
\widehat{E}
and observe that the analogous considerations also apply to the sector of
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{T}_{AB}
. Because in this sector the ghost variables are absent, there are no additional gauge symmetries while the equations of motion
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{T}_{AB}=\sigma^{*}Q\mathcal{T}_{AB}
simply express
\sigma^{*}(\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{T}_{AB})
in terms of space-time derivatives of
\mathbf{T}_{AB}\equiv\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{AB})
and the fields of
E^{conf}
.
To summarize,
\widehat{E}
seen as a gPDE over
T[1]\Sigma
is off-shell. More formally, it is equivalent to a non-negatively graded (i.e. no antifields) jet-bundle BV system.

Any vector field on the total space
\widehat{E}
of our gPDE gives rise to a vector field on the space of sections. Namely, if
V
is a vector field and
\sigma
a section then the variation of
\sigma
under the prolongation of
V
is given by

\delta\sigma^{*}=\sigma^{*}\circ V

(5.39)

and is interpreted as a value of
V^{prol}
at the point
\sigma
of the space of sections. More formally,
\sigma^{*}\circ V

is a vector field along
\sigma
. Let us see what
V^{prol}
look like in terms of coordinates on the space of sections, i.e. fields
\phi^{i}(x,\theta)\equiv\sigma^{*}\phi^{i}
, where
\phi^{i}
denote all the fibre coordinates of
\widehat{E}
. We have,
\sigma^{*}V\phi^{i}=\sigma^{*}V^{i}(\phi,x,\theta)=V^{i}(\sigma^{*}(\phi),x,\theta)
, where we introduced components
V^{i}(\phi)\equiv V\phi^{i}
.

Let us specialize this construction by taking
\nabla_{A}
as
V
. For an arbitrary degree-
0
function
f
on
\widehat{E}
we have:

\displaystyle Qf=\xi^{A}\nabla_{A}f+C_{A}{}^{B}\Delta^{A}{}_{B}f+\lambda^{A}\Gamma_{A}+\lambda\Delta f\,.

(5.40)

Because
[Q{,}\,\nabla_{A}]=0
, vector fields
\nabla_{A}
are symmetries and hence
\nabla_{A}^{prol}
preserve the solution space, i.e. they are tangent to the subspace of solutions. If
\sigma
is a solution, i.e.
\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}f=\sigma^{*}Qf
we have

\sigma^{*}(\xi^{A}\nabla_{A}f)=\sigma^{*}(QF-\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}\Delta^{B}{}_{A}f-\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\Gamma_{A}-\widehat{\lambda}\Delta f)=\\
=\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}f-\sigma^{*}(\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}\Delta^{A}{}_{B}f+\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\Gamma_{A}+\widehat{\lambda}\Delta f)=\mathrm{d}_{X}\sigma^{*}f-\sigma^{*}((Q-\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A})f)

(5.41)

If in addition, the metric-like gauge is imposed one gets:

\sigma^{*}(\nabla_{A}f)=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}\sigma^{*}f-\Gamma_{CA}^{B}\sigma^{*}(\Delta^{C}{}_{B}f)+P^{B}{}_{A}\sigma^{*}(\Gamma_{B}f)\,.

(5.42)

Note that in this form
\sigma^{*}(\nabla_{A}f)
is a useful object even if
\sigma
is not a solution.

The first two terms in (
5.42
) can be regarded as the Levi-Civita covariant derivative
\nabla^{g}_{A}
:

\nabla^{g}_{A}\sigma^{*}f\equiv\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}\sigma^{*}f-\Gamma_{AB}^{C}\sigma^{*}(\Delta_{C}{}^{B}f)\,,

(5.43)

Note that
\nabla^{g}_{A}
defined this way is, strictly speaking, a vector field along
\sigma
. However, if we use degree-
0
fibre coordinates
\phi^{i}_{0}
such that
\Delta_{C}{}^{B}\phi_{0}^{i}=(\Delta_{C}{}^{B})^{i}_{j}\phi_{0}^{j}
and the coefficients are field-independent one can reinterpret
\nabla^{g}_{A}
as a usual covariant derivative acting on tensor fields in a given linear representation. As for the
total covariant derivative (
5.43
) it can be directly related to the covariant derivative introduced in
[
61
]

and to the Weyl covariant derivative from
[
56
]
. At the same it can be connected to the tractor connection
[
62
]
.

For further applications, it is also useful to be able to rewrite expressions of the form
\sigma^{*}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}f
as Levi-Civita derivatives with correction terms. The following formula holds:

\sigma^{*}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}f=\nabla_{A}^{g}\nabla^{g|A}\sigma^{*}f+(\partial_{A}P)\sigma^{*}\Gamma^{A}f\\
+2P^{AD}\nabla^{g}_{A}\sigma^{*}\Gamma_{D}f+P^{AB}P_{A}{}^{C}\sigma^{*}\Gamma_{B}\Gamma_{C}f+P\sigma^{*}(-\Delta^{A}{}_{A}+\widehat{\Delta})f\,,

(5.44)

where
\widehat{\Delta}\equiv[Q,\frac{\partial}{\partial\widehat{\lambda}}]
and
P\equiv P_{A}{}^{A}
. To prove this formula, it is sufficient to use equation (
5.42
) twice, the standard Riemannian geometry identity
\nabla^{g}_{A}P^{A}{}_{B}=\nabla^{g}_{B}P=\partial_{B}P
, and to commute
\nabla_{A}
and
\Gamma_{B}
in the same way as in (
3.11
).

Let us mention that the interpretation of degree-
0
coordinates on the minimal model of the BRST complex as generalised tensor fields and degree-
1
coordinates as connections was already in
[
58
]
in the general setting and was specialized to conformal geometry in
[
55
,
56
]
. Note however that in the above references the interpretation was done at the purely algebraic level while here the covariant derivatives are defined as acting on space-time fields.

We now use the above technique to show that for
D=5
the function
\widehat{B}_{CA}\equiv\nabla^{B}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}
on
\widehat{E}
indeed corresponds to the Bach tensor on the solution space. Using
\Gamma^{D}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}=-\widehat{\mathrm{W}}^{D}{}_{CAB}
and formula (
5.42
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\widehat{B}_{CA}=\sigma^{*}\nabla^{B}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}=\nabla^{g|B}\sigma^{*}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}+P^{DB}\sigma^{*}\Gamma_{D}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{CAB}=\nabla^{g|B}C_{CAB}-P^{DB}\mathrm{W}_{DCAB}

(5.45)

which is the standard formula for the Bach tensor and we can denote
\sigma^{*}\widehat{B}_{BC}=B_{BC}[g]
. For
D>5
, the same formula provides a natural generalization of the Bach tensor to higher dimensions, which, however, is no longer a conformally invariant tensor, since
\Gamma_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}=-(D-5)(\widehat{C}_{CAB}+\widehat{C}_{BAC}).
12
12

12

Although this formula can be obtained by a direct computation, one can notice that there is a simpler way to derive it using the boundary calculus. Indeed, from equation (
4.55
) we have
(D-5)\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BC}=2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\widehat{B}_{BC}
, and from (
4.53
) it follows that
\Gamma_{A}\widehat{T}_{BC}=-2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\widehat{C}_{CAB}+\widehat{C}_{BAC})
.
As we have seen in Example
5.4
for
D=5

\nabla_{(C)}\widehat{B}_{AB}
generate the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
of the boundary gPDE and hence

\nabla_{(C)}\widehat{B}_{AB}
vanish on solutions. The other way around, if
\sigma
is a solution to
\widehat{E}
such that
\sigma^{*}(\widehat{B}_{AB})=0
then
\sigma^{*}(\nabla_{(C)}\widehat{B}_{AB})=0
. The “conservation equation”
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{B}=0
in this dimension takes the form

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\nabla_{A}\mathcal{T}^{A}{}_{B}=\nabla^{g}_{A}\mathbf{T}^{A}{}_{B}+2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}P^{AC}C_{ABC}=0

(5.46)

For
D=7
, as follows from Example
5.5
, the “obstruction” condition on solutions
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{BC}=0
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\sigma^{*}(\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}+2\mathrm{W}_{DCAB}B^{AD}+2\mathrm{C}_{C}{}^{AD}\mathrm{C}_{BAD}-4\mathrm{C}^{D}{}_{B}{}^{A}\mathrm{C}_{ACD}).\end{split}

(5.47)

Using formula (
5.44
) together with expressions for
\Gamma_{A}B_{BC}
,
\Gamma_{A}C_{BCD}
given above, it is straightforward to rewrite this formula as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla^{g|A}\nabla^{g}_{A}B_{BC}+4(\partial_{A}P)C_{(BC)}{}^{A}+8P^{AD}\nabla^{g}_{A}C_{(BC)D}+4P^{AD}P_{A}{}^{E}W_{ECDB}-4PB_{BC}+\\
+2W_{DCAB}B^{AD}+2\mathrm{C}_{C}{}^{AD}\mathrm{C}_{BAD}-4\mathrm{C}^{D}{}_{B}{}^{A}\mathrm{C}_{ACD}=0\end{split}

(5.48)

This is precisely the formula for the obstruction tensor in this dimension from
[
24
]
. The condition
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{B}=0
takes the form

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{A}\mathbf{T}^{A}{}_{B}+6\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}P^{AD}\sigma^{*}(2\nabla_{D}\widehat{B}_{BA}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{B}_{DA})=0

(5.49)

where
\sigma^{*}\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}
can again be rewritten using (
5.42
).

6
Boundary structure of (gauge)fields

In the previous section we have described the boundary structure of the asymptotically AdS gravity. Now we extend the framework to cover (gauge) fields coupled to the gravity background. For simplicity, we call these fields “matter fields” having in mind that they can have their own gauge symmetries so the case of e.g. Yang-Mills theory on the gravity background is covered.

6.1
Gauge fields over boundary GR

Let us recall that our approach to boundary structure of gravity is to equivalently reformulate it as a conformal-like gravity in the interior and then extend it to the boundary, requiring fields to be well-defined there and, finally, to set the boundary defining field
\Omega
to vanish in a way compatible with gauge symmetry.

We now extend the approach to matter fields defined on gravity background and start with the combined system consisting of gravity and matter. More precisely, we limit ourselves to the case where matter fields do not source gravity so that Einstein equations remain intact. At the same time the equations of motion and gauge transformations of matter fields do depend on the background. In the gPDE language this means that we are dealing with a gPDE over background:

(E_{cl}^{\phi},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl}^{\phi})\;\overset{\pi^{\phi}}{\longrightarrow}\;(E_{cl},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl})\;\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}\;(T[1]X,\mathrm{d}_{X})\,,

(6.1)

where
(E_{cl},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl})
denotes the gPDE for conformal-like GR introduced in Section
2.4
. As always in this work, to keep conventions concise we use
Q
to denote both the
Q
-structure on the total space and its projection to the background gPDE. The same applies to restrictions of
Q
to various submanifolds we encounter. Note
(E_{cl}^{\phi},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl}^{\phi})
is naturally a bundle over
T[1]X
with the projection being
\pi\circ\pi^{\phi}
and can also be considered as an implicit gPDE.

Let us recall that the boundary gPDE
E_{B}
for gravity, which was constructed in the preceding Sections, is obtained in the following steps: i) restricting to the sub-gPDE
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
by equivalent reduction ii) pulling-back
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
to the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
and setting
\Omega=0,Q\Omega=0
followed by an additional equivalent reduction. All in all this gave an embedding (of
Q
-manifolds)

(E_{B},Q)\hookrightarrow(E_{cl},Q)\,.

(6.2)

The ideal
\mathcal{I}_{B}
on
E_{B}
defining the on-shell system is obtained as a pullback of the initial
\mathcal{I}_{cl}
, giving a gPDE
(E_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}_{B})
that describes the boundary structure of asymptotically AdS GR. We refer to the local gauge theory described by
(E_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}_{B})
as to the boundary GR. Finally, by solving part of the equations from
\mathcal{I}_{B}
one arrives at the equivalent formulation of the boundary GR
as an implicit gPDE
(\widehat{E},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}})
, whose advantage is that
(\widehat{E},Q)
is off-shell and its structure can be described explicitly in terms of the coordinates on
\widehat{E}
using the boundary calculus constructed in Section
4.5
.

The embedding of gPDE
(E_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}_{B})
for boundary GR into
(E_{cl},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl})
induces the pullback bundle:

\displaystyle E^{\phi}_{B}\;\longrightarrow E_{B}

(6.3)

which we interpret as the boundary system for the mater field
\phi
on the background of boundary GR. Again, just like
(E_{B},Q)
, gPDE
(E^{\phi}_{B},Q)
comes equipped with the ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{B}
representing what remains of the equations of motion after the restriction to the boundary and the equivalent reductions. More precisely,
\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{B}
is generated by
\mathcal{I}_{B}
pulled back to
E^{\phi}_{B}
and the prolongation of the equations of motion for matter fields. All in all the gPDE describing the boundary structure is
(E^{\phi}_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{B})
. Of course, in order to study what it describes a substantial reformulation of this system is in order.

In these considerations there is an important potential problem to be fixed. In order for the reduction from
E_{cl}
to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
and then to
E_{B}
to work we had to rescale the metric
g
by
\Omega^{2}
and then set
\Omega=0
. This could result in negative powers of
\Omega
appearing in the equations of motion for matter fields after the pull-back to
E_{B}
. The same applies to the
Q
-structure in the sector of matter fields. Moreover, we should be able to prescribe the asymptotic behaviour of the matter fields.

The way to cure this is similar to what we did in deriving
(E_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}_{B})
. Namely, we reformulate the initial equations of motion in such way that they remain equivalent for
\Omega>0
while their restrictions to
\Omega=0
become well-defined.
More precisely, let
\widetilde{\phi},\widetilde{c}
be a collective notations for matter fields and corresponding ghost variables (if any) on the GR background described by metric
\widetilde{g}
. The equations of motion for
\widetilde{\phi}
are assumed to be diffeomorphism-invariant and are denoted by
\widetilde{P}[\widetilde{\phi};\widetilde{g}]=0
. What we actually need is
(E_{cl}^{\phi},Q,\mathcal{I}_{cl}^{\phi})
which is defined to be a lift of this matter system from GR background to the background of conformal-like reformulation of GR, i.e. the extension of GR by the Stueckelberg pair
\{\Omega,\lambda\}
. The lift is constructed by declaring
\widetilde{\phi}
to be inert under the Weyl transformations encoded in
Q
. To control the boundary behaviour of the matter we repeat the trick employed for gravity and switch to the coordinate system
\phi\equiv\Omega^{w}\widetilde{\phi}
,
g\equiv\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}
, where
w\in\mathbb{R}
. This
w
parametrizes the behaviour of our matter fields near the boundary and for the moment we leave it generic.

Let us now turn to our equations of motion
\widetilde{P}[\widetilde{\phi},\widetilde{g}]
written in terms of the new coordinates and try to express
\widetilde{P}[\phi,g]
as a polynomial in
\Omega
times a singular prefactor:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}[\phi;g,\Omega]=\Omega^{-w_{P}}\sum_{k=0}\Omega^{k}P_{k}(\phi,D\phi;g,Dg,D_{a\ldots}\Omega)\,,\hskip 10.00002ptw_{P}\,{\geqslant}\,0

(6.4)

where
P_{0}
is nonvanishing and
\widetilde{P}_{k}
may depend on derivatives of
\Omega
, but not on
\Omega
itself. As we will see shortly the above decomposition exists for most of the standard choices of matter. For the general considerations, we assume from now on that it exists and that only finite number of
P_{k}
are nonvanishing.

Given the decomposition (
6.4
), we consider the rescaled constraints
P[\phi;g]\equiv\Omega^{w_{P}}\widetilde{P}[\phi,g]
which are equivalent to
\widetilde{P}
when
\Omega>0
and are well-defined on the boundary. However, this does not imply that the entire system is well-defined at
\Omega=0
because the gauge transformations for matter fields may still be ill-defined there (in gPDE terms, this means that the action of
Q
on
\phi
contains negative powers of
\Omega
). Typically, this can be fixed by adjusting weight
w
. Moreover, in the case of YM theory, the requirement that
Q
is well-defined on the boundary, uniquely determines
w
. That gauge symmetry fixes the conformal weight of gauge fields is well-known. In what follows we assume that
w
is chosen in such a way that both the rescaled equations and
Q
are well defined. All in all
this gives a gPDE
(E^{\phi}_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{B})
on the boundary, which is a gPDE over the background gPDE
(E_{B},Q,\mathcal{I}_{B})
. This describes the boundary structure of the asymptotically-AdS gravity along with the matter fields defined over it. Note that the boundary structure might generally depend on the choice of
w
but usually
w
is essentially fixed by the consistency of the above procedure.

In the rest of this Section we show how the technical tools introduced to study the boundary structure of gravity extend to the case where matter fields are also present. Not to complicate the exposition, we restrict ourselves to the case where matter fields have closed gauge algebra and no ghosts for ghosts are present. In our approach this means that we can restrict ourselves to the case where the fibre of
E_{cl}^{\phi}
has coordinates of degree
0
and
1
only, which we denote by
D_{(a)}\phi^{i}
and
c^{\alpha}
, respectively.

The matter equations of motion generate the
Q
-invariant ideal
I(P[\phi,g],D_{a})
. Then, the equivalent reduction
r:E_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow E_{cl}
in the gravity sector, described in Section
3
, induces a pullback of the bundle
E_{cl}^{\phi}\rightarrow E_{cl}
to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
, which we denote by
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
. It inherits the gPDE structure
(E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}},Q,\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}})
. By some abuse of notations we also use
r
to denote the embedding
r:E_{\mathrm{red}}^{\phi}\to E_{cl}^{\phi}
, for instance:
\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}=r^{*}\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{cl}
. The following statement is an immediate generalisation of Proposition
3.4
and Corollary
3.6
.

Proposition 6.1
.

If
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})\phi\in I^{0}(\phi)
then as fibre coordinates on
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}\rightarrow E_{\mathrm{red}}
one can take

\displaystyle\{\nabla_{(a)}(r^{*}\phi),\nabla_{(a)}(r^{*}c^{\alpha})\}\,,

(6.5)

where
\nabla_{a}\equiv[Q,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}]
.
13
13

13

Recall, that we use the same notations for the ghosts and their restrictions to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
. The same applies to
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
.
If
(Q-\xi^{a}D_{a})P[\phi,g]\in I^{0}(P[\phi,g])
the pullback of the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\phi}
to
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
takes the form

\displaystyle r^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\phi}=I^{\infty}(r^{*}P[\phi,g],\nabla_{a}).

(6.6)

In fact, most of the statements proved for
E_{\mathrm{red}}
remain true for
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
. One subtlety is that
in addition to restricting to
E_{\mathrm{red}}
it is extremely convenient to perform the additional equivalent reduction in the sector of fibre coordinates in order to end up with a finite number of degree
1
coordinates. This does not bring extra complications and we assume that
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
is the reduced and we keep denoting the remaining fibre coordinates of degree
1
by
c^{\alpha}
. Another subtlety is that the fibre ghost variables
c^{\alpha}
are to be considered on the equal footing with all the ghost degree
1
coordinates on
E_{\mathrm{red}}
. In particular, it is convenient to consider vector field
[Q,\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}]
where now
C^{I}
denote all the ghost variables including
c^{\alpha}
and these vector fields are defined on
(E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
rather than its base
(E_{\mathrm{red}},Q)
. Note that
[Q,\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}]
are projectable with respect to
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}\to E_{\mathrm{red}}
because
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}\to E_{\mathrm{red}}
is a
Q
-bundle and
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C^{I}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
is projectable by construction. This justifies using the same notations for some of these vector fields. In particular:

\Gamma^{a}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{a}},Q]\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}\equiv ad_{\nabla_{\Omega}}^{N}(\nabla_{A})\,.

(6.7)

Repeating the analysis of Sections
4.1
and
4
for the case of
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
, denoting by
E^{\phi}
the pullback of
E^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
to
T[1]\partial X\subset T[1]X
, and dropping
r^{*}
we arrive at:

Proposition 6.2
.

If
(Q-\xi^{a}\nabla_{a})\phi\in I^{0}(\phi)
, the fibre coordinates of
E^{\phi}\rightarrow E
can be chosen as

\displaystyle\{\nabla_{(A)}\phi^{(N)},c^{\alpha}|N\,{\geqslant}\,0\}

(6.8)

where as before we use the conventions:
f^{(N)}\equiv\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}f
. The pullback of the ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\phi}_{\mathrm{red}}
to
E^{\phi}
is generated by
\mathcal{I}
and

\nabla_{(A)}P^{(N)}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptN\,{\geqslant}\,0\,.

(6.9)

The next step in the analysis of the boundary structure involves
restricting
E^{\phi}
to
\widehat{E}\subset E
, where we have an explicit description of the boundary GR and solving a part of the matter equations of motion accounted in
\mathcal{I}^{\phi}
. This leads to the final gPDE
(\widehat{E}^{\phi},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{\phi})
which describes the boundary structure of gravity + matter fields in the asymptotically AdS setup. The explicit structure of
(\widehat{E}^{\phi},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{\phi})
substantially depends on what type of matter fields is being considered and is discussed in details for a few concrete systems in the remaining Sections.

6.2
Scalar field

In this section we illustrate our approach using the example of scalar field subject to the Klein-Gordon (KG) equation. As a fibre of
E_{cl}^{\varphi}
we take a fibre of a jet-bundle for a scalar field with coordinates
D_{a}\widetilde{\varphi}
so that the KG equation reads as:

\widetilde{P}[\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{g}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\widetilde{g}|}}D_{a}(\sqrt{|\widetilde{g}|}\widetilde{g}^{ab}D_{b}\widetilde{\varphi})+m^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}\,.

(6.10)

According to the general procedure explained in the previous Section we can now switch to the fibre coordinates
D_{(a)}\varphi
, where
\varphi=\Omega^{w}\widetilde{\varphi}
. In the new coordinate system we have

\displaystyle Q\varphi=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}\varphi+w\lambda\varphi\,,

(6.11)

where we employed
\nabla_{a}
instead of
D_{a}
(note that in the usual coordinates on
E_{cl}^{\varphi}
we have
\nabla_{a}\varphi=[\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt},Q]\varphi=D_{a}\varphi
). It is straightforward to verify that because it is written in terms of
\nabla_{a}
, this formula remains valid after we pull-back our bundle to
E_{\mathrm{red}}\subset E_{cl}
.

To proceed further we pullback
E_{cl}^{\varphi}
to
E_{\mathrm{red}}\subset E_{cl}

and then pullback the resulting gPDE (as a bundle over
T[1]X
) to the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
. This results in the gPDE
(E^{\varphi},Q)
over
(E,Q)
. Then the Klein-Gordon equation written in terms of
g,\varphi
takes the form
\widetilde{P}=\Omega^{-w}P
, where

\begin{gathered}P[\varphi,g]\equiv\Omega^{2}\nabla_{a}\nabla^{a}\varphi+\Omega(c_{w,1}\nabla_{a}\varphi\nabla^{a}\Omega-w(\nabla_{a}\nabla^{a}\Omega)\varphi)+(w(d+w)\nabla_{a}\Omega\nabla^{a}\Omega+m^{2})\varphi,\\
c_{w,N}\equiv-2w-d+N\end{gathered}

(6.12)

and
d\equiv D-1
denotes the boundary dimension. Note that
P
is polynomial in
\Omega
, and hence the assumption is fulfilled. This form of KG equation is a version of what is known in the literature as a Weyl-covariant formulation; see, e.g.
[
63
,
64
]
. The prolongation of
P
, i.e. functions
\nabla_{(a)}P
, generate the ideal which together with the pull-back of
\mathcal{I}
from the base
E
form a total ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\varphi}
on
E^{\varphi}
. In other words we have reformulated our system as a gPDE
(E^{\varphi},Q,\mathcal{I}^{\varphi})

over background gPDE
(E,Q,\mathcal{I})
.

Just like in the analysis of the gravity sector, we next split the index as
\{a\}=\{\Omega,A\}
and switch to the adapted fibre coordinates
\{\nabla_{(A)}\varphi^{(N)},\,N\,{\geqslant}\,0\}
instead of
\{\nabla_{(a)}\varphi\}
by making use of
(Q-\xi^{a}\nabla_{a})\varphi\in I^{0}(\varphi)
. As usual, we also use notations
f^{(N)}\equiv\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}f
.

At the end of the day we are interested in
E^{\varphi}
pulled back to the on-shell background gPDE
(\mathcal{E},Q)
. However, it is not convenient to work with
\mathcal{E}
and we treat it implicitly. More precisely, we identify functions on
E^{\varphi}
pulled back to
\mathcal{E}\subset E
as equivalence classes modulo the ideal
\mathcal{K}
which is generated by the pullbacks to total space
E^{\varphi}
of functions on the base
E
vanishing on
\mathcal{E}
. Of course, this is a slight abuse of conventions but it does not lead to confusions and we do the same with its sub-ideals
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
. In practice, we analyse the prolongation of KG equations leaving the terms from
\mathcal{K}
implicit. In particular, in this way we can easily evaluate
Q\varphi^{(N)}
as in Proposition
B.2
, giving

\displaystyle Q\varphi^{(N)}=\xi^{A}\nabla_{A}\varphi^{(N)}+\widetilde{\Lambda}\lambda^{A}\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-2-i)}\varphi^{(i)}+(w-N)\lambda\varphi^{(N)}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}\,.

(6.13)

We now turn to the analysis of the
ideal of the KG equation, which is generated by
\{\nabla_{(a)}P\}
. Just like in the case of GR it is convenient to use
\{\nabla_{(A)}P^{(N)},\,N\,{\geqslant}\,0\}
as generators of the ideal. In particular, one has:

P^{(0)}=(\widetilde{\Lambda}w(d+w)+m^{2})\varphi^{(0)}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}\,,

(6.14)

where, as before, we do not explicitly spell out terms from
\mathcal{K}
. This generator is trivial if the mass
m
and Weyl weight
w
satisfy the familiar relation:

m^{2}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}w(d+w)\,.

(6.15)

Although it is possible to give a consistent interpretation of the boundary system even if (
6.15
) does not hold, we refrain from doing this and in what follows assume (
6.15
). Acting with
(\nabla_{\Omega})^{N}
on (
6.12
) and using
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
we obtain the explicit form of the subleading equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\widetilde{\Lambda}N}P^{(N)}=c_{w,N}\varphi^{(N)}+(N-1)\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)},\quad N\,{\geqslant}\,1.\end{split}

(6.16)

It is clear that the structure of the system described by the zero locus of
\nabla_{(A)}P^{(N)}
critically depends on whether
c_{w,N}\equiv-2w+1-D+N
vanishes for some
N=N^{*}
or not. If it does not, all the fibre coordinates
\nabla_{(A)}\varphi^{(N)}
can be eliminated using
\nabla_{(A)}P^{(N)}
and hence the only remaining independent fibre coordinates on the zero locus of the ideal are
\nabla_{(A)}\varphi^{(0)}
. It follows, the resulting system describes the unconstrained scalar field of Weyl weight
w
, defined over the background of
\mathcal{E}
.A more careful analysis shows that the same result holds even for a more general background
\widehat{E}
.
Moreover, because
Q\varphi^{(0)}

depends on
\varphi^{(0)}
and ghosts only,
\varphi^{(0)}
is indeed a conformal primary.

We now turn to the more interesting case
where
c_{w,N^{*}}=0
for some positive integer
N^{*}
.
The analysis is then analogous to the case of gravity. In particular, it turns out very useful to introduce an analogue of the off-shell boundary gPDE
\widehat{E}
. This is done in two steps. First, we restrict our bundle to
\widehat{E}\subset E
because it is
\widehat{E}

where we have a full control over the gravity background. Second, we pass to the subbundle
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
of
E^{\varphi}|_{\widehat{E}}
, defined as the zero locus of the following equations:

\displaystyle\varphi^{(N)}=-\frac{N-1}{c_{w,N}}\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi,\hskip 10.00002ptN\,{\geqslant}\,1,\,N\neq N^{*}\,.

(6.17)

Strictly speaking, the above equations are defined on
E^{\varphi}
and it is their pullback to
E^{\varphi}|_{\widehat{E}}

that defines a subbundle
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}\subset E^{\varphi}|_{\widehat{E}}
.
In practice, for the moment we work with
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
as a submanifold in
E^{\varphi}
and we denote by
\widehat{b}_{\varphi}
the corresponding embedding
\widehat{E}_{B}^{\varphi}\hookrightarrow E^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{red}}
.
For any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E^{\varphi})
we use the notation
\widehat{f}\equiv\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}f
.

Similarly to the gravity case discussed in Section
4.4
, we encounter that
Q
is not tangent to
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
seen as submanifold in
E^{\varphi}
. However, as before, we can naturally define a
Q
-structure on
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
but this time in a slightly weaker sense. Namely, this
Q
-structure is well defined modulo the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}\mathcal{I}
that defines the on-shell system on
\widehat{E}
. This doesn’t complicate the general analysis as at the end of the day we are going to study our matter field coupled to the on-shell background and hence terms from
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
vanish on such configurations. To equip
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
with a
Q
-structure we restrict to the subbundle
E^{\varphi,N^{*}}\subset E^{\varphi}
, singled out by
\nabla_{(A)}P^{(N)}=0
with
N<N^{*}
. The result is a
Q
-subbundle thanks to a straightforward extension of Proposition
4.4
. Furthermore, for
N=N^{*}
equation (
6.13
) implies that
Q\varphi^{(N^{*})}
does not depend on
\varphi^{(k)}
with
k>N^{*}
so that the subalgebra of functions in
\nabla_{(A)}\varphi,\nabla_{(A)}\varphi^{(N^{*})}
and coordinates from
\widehat{E}
is closed under
Q
. This means that
E^{\varphi,N^{*}}
is a
Q
-bundle over another
Q
-manifold that we denote by
\widehat{E}^{\varphi\prime}
. Finally, the subbundle
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
(this can be obtained by imposing
\nabla_{(A)}P^{(N)}=0
with
N>N^{*}
) is a section of
E^{\varphi,N^{*}}\to\widehat{E}^{\varphi\prime}
and hence
the natural
Q
-structure from
\widehat{E}^{\varphi\prime}
can be pushed forward to
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
. Let us stress that in all these steps
Q
is assumed to be defined modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
. Nevertheless, for
N^{*}
sufficiently small one can check that
\mathcal{I}
does not enter the relations and hence
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
can be equipped with the genuine
Q
-structure.

Repeating once again the logic employed in the analysis of gravity, we introduce a subalgebra of
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
-projectable functions in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E^{\varphi})
, which consists of functions whose restrictions to
E^{\varphi,N^{*}}
are constant along the fibres (i.e. can be obtained as pullbacks of functions from
\widehat{E}^{\varphi\prime}
). In particular, for such a function we have
Q\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}f=\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}Qf
modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
. It is straightforward to see that
\varphi^{(N)}
are
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
-projectable for
N\,{\leqslant}\,N^{*}
. It is convenient to introduce the following notations:

\displaystyle\psi\equiv(\widehat{b}_{\varphi})^{*}\varphi^{(N^{*})},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{P}\equiv(\widehat{b}_{\varphi})^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N^{*}-2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi\,,

(6.18)

where
\mathcal{P}=0
for
N^{*}=1
. The fibre coordinates on
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
are then taken to be
\{\nabla_{(A)}\widehat{\varphi},\nabla_{(A)}\psi\}
and the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{\varphi}\equiv\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}\mathcal{I}^{\varphi}
is now generated by
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{P}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
. Recall that
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
is a bundle over
\widehat{E}
. The resulting implicit gPDE
(\widehat{E}^{\varphi},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{\varphi})
describes the boundary structure of the KG field on the background of on-shell boundary gPDE for GR. Of course, in the analysis of KG field one can disregard the gravity gPDE by pulling back
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
to a given on-shell configuration of the boundary GR, i.e. a particular solution of
(\widehat{E},Q,\widehat{\mathcal{I}})
.

By representing
