\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}(\nabla_{\Omega})^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi
in terms of
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-k)}\widehat{\varphi}^{(k)}
one observes that
\mathcal{P}
can be expressed (modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
) in terms of the collection of functions
\widehat{\varphi}^{(k)}
,
1\,{\leqslant}\,N<N^{*}
together with functions
\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}
,
\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}
, which enter through vector fields
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
. This constitutes the extension of the boundary calculus introduced in Section
4.5
to the case where on top of gravity we have a KG field coupled to it. Note that
\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{ABC}
,
\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{AB}
are now seen as function on
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
, i.e. the pullbacks of the corresponding functions from
\widehat{E}
by the canonical projection
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}\to\widehat{E}
. As for the vector fields
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
, they are now defined on
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
from the very start as the restriction of
(ad_{\nabla_{\Omega}})^{N}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}$}\kern 1.0pt}
from
E^{\varphi}
to
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
and are generally defined modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
. However, all their properties remain true as the expression of
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
in terms of
Q
and ghosts is unchanged.

Let us summarize the scalar field extension of the boundary calculus, introduced in Theorem
4.12
:

Proposition 6.3
.

Functions
\widehat{\varphi}^{(N)}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\widehat{E}^{\varphi})
given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}^{(N)}=-\frac{N-1}{c_{w,N}}\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widehat{b}_{\varphi})^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi,\hskip 10.00002pt1\,{\leqslant}\,N<N^{*}

(6.19)

can be recursively determined (modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
) in terms of
\widehat{\varphi}\equiv\widehat{\varphi}^{(0)}
and
T^{(N)}_{AB}
,
J_{ABC}^{(N)}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\widehat{f}^{(i)}=\nabla_{A}\widehat{f}^{(N)}+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\widehat{f}^{(i)}

(6.20)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}f=(-\widehat{J}^{(N-1),D}{}_{CA}\Delta^{C}{}_{D}+\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{\Lambda}N}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{CA}\Gamma^{C}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\widehat{T}^{(i),C}{}_{A}\mathcal{D}_{C}^{(N-2-i)})f\,,\hskip 10.00002ptN\,{\geqslant}\,1\,.

(6.21)

Proof.

Equation (
6.20
) is just a version of Proposition
4.10
while (
6.21
) is just the direct extension to
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}_{B}

of the relations (
4.56
) from Theorem
4.12
. Indeed, because we consider the background to be on-shell, Remark
4.13
implies
that there is no restriction on
N
.
∎

As an illustration of the procedure, let us compute
\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi
:

\displaystyle\widehat{b}_{\varphi}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}(\widehat{b}_{\varphi})^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}\nabla^{A}\varphi=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\sum_{j=0}^{i}C^{j}_{i}\mathcal{D}^{(i-j)|A}\widehat{\varphi}^{(j)}\,.

(6.22)

Here and in the rest of this Section all equalities are assumed to hold modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
. Note that for odd
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
we have
\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}=0
, thanks to the trivial generalization of Proposition
4.16
.

The action of
Q
on the fibre coordinates of
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
is also expressed in terms of the above boundary calculus. Indeed, thanks to (
6.13
) we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q\widehat{\varphi}=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A}\widehat{\varphi}+w\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{\varphi}\,,\\
&Q\psi=\widehat{\xi}^{A}\nabla_{A}\psi+\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{\lambda}^{A}\sum_{i=0}^{N^{*}-2}d^{i}_{N^{*}}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N^{*}-2-i)}\widehat{\varphi}^{(i)}+(w-N^{*})\widehat{\lambda}\psi+\widehat{\mathcal{I}}\,.\end{split}

(6.23)

Before passing to the examples let us note that for
N^{*}
sufficiently small the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
does not actually enter the boundary calculus. One can already see this in formula (
6.16
), where the terms from
\mathcal{K}
in the right-hand side vanish after pulling back to
\widehat{E}_{B}
for
N^{*}
sufficiently small. In this case
\widehat{E}^{\varphi}
can be considered a genuine gauge PDE which is off-shell both in the sector of GR and in the matter sector.

Having a boundary calculus at our disposal we now look at a number of particular cases.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
N^{*}=2w+d
be an odd positive integer. If
d
is odd, assume in addition that the background gPDE is in addition subject to
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{T}_{BC}=0
. Then
\mathcal{P}
vanishes identically, i.e. the ideal is trivial, and the boundary system describes just two unconstrained scalar fields of weights
w
and
w-N^{*}=-w-d
. Moreover,
\Gamma_{D}\psi=0
, i.e.
\psi
is also a conformal primary.

Proof.

By induction, (
6.19
) and Propositions
5.7
,
5.3
, one can prove that
\widehat{\varphi}^{(N)}=0
for all odd
N\,{\leqslant}\,N^{*}~-~2
.
∎

In the standard conventions used in the context of AdS/CFT,
\varphi

and
\psi
are known as leading and sub-leading boundary values. Their conformal weight are
\Delta_{-}=-w
and
\Delta_{+}=d-\Delta_{-}=w+d
, see e.g.
[
15
]
and references therein.

Before moving on to the case of generic even
N^{*}
which we henceforth parametrize as
N^{*}\equiv 2\ell
, let us discuss a couple of examples:

Example 6.5
.

Conformal Laplacian:
\ell=1
, or equivalently,
-w=\frac{d}{2}-1
. In this case:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}\,.

(6.24)

Note that
-w=\frac{d}{2}-1
indeed corresponds to the correct Weyl weight of the usual conformally coupled scalar field. Note that the above formula is expressed in terms of covariant derivatives
\nabla_{A}
rather then the usual Levi-Civitta ones and therefore does not explicitly contain the familiar scalar curvature correction. The correction becomes explicit in the actual equations of motion
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{P})=0
, where
\sigma
is a lift of a configuration
\varphi(x)
to a solution of
(\widehat{E}^{\phi}_{B},Q)
, see Section
5.3
for more details.
We also observe that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}\psi=\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{A}\widehat{\varphi}

(6.25)

so that, as in the gravitational case, we cannot in general set the subleading to zero. Such a condition is not compatible with the background gauge transformation, i.e. it is not conformally invariant.

Example 6.6
.

Fradkin–Tseytlin–Paneitz operator
[
65
,
66
]
:
d\,{\geqslant}\,4
,
l=2
. Using
(
6.22
) together with (
6.19
) we can easily get
\widehat{\varphi}^{(1)}=0
,
\widehat{\varphi}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}
. Hence, using (
6.21
) we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\sum_{i=0}^{2}C^{i}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(2-i)}\sum_{j=0}^{i}C^{j}_{i}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(i-j)}\widehat{\varphi}^{(j)}=\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}+\nabla_{A}\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{\varphi}+\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}^{(2)}=\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}}{2}(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{2}\widehat{\varphi}

(6.26)

and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}\psi=3\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{A}(\nabla_{B}\nabla^{B})\widehat{\varphi}\,.

(6.27)

Example 6.7
.

Order 3 extended GJMS-like:
l=3
. For
d\,{\geqslant}\,4
direct computations give:

\displaystyle\frac{8}{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}\mathcal{P}=(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{3}\widehat{\varphi}+\frac{8}{\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{AB}\nabla^{A}\nabla^{B}\widehat{\varphi}\,.

(6.28)

An interesting new feature shows up here: for
d=4
,
\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{AB}
entering
\mathcal{P}
is not a local function in the conformal geometry (gravitational leading) but is a gravitational subleading
\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{AB}\equiv\mathcal{T}_{AB}
.
In other words, in this case
\varphi
is coupled to both the conformal geometry and the gravity subleading
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
which is itself a conformal field coupled to the conformal geometry background. Unlike the previous examples, for
d=4
equation (
6.28
) is conformally-invariant only if the background is Bach-flat. This example gives an elegant resolution of the puzzle of what is a natural generalisation of the 3rd order GJMS operator in 4 dimensions that is well-defined on conformal gravity background.

For
d>4
, Theorem
4.12
gives
\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{AB}=\frac{2\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{D-5}\widehat{B}_{AB}
so that

\displaystyle\frac{8}{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}\mathcal{P}=(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{3}\widehat{\varphi}+\frac{16}{D-5}\widehat{B}_{AB}\nabla^{A}\nabla^{B}\widehat{\varphi}

(6.29)

does not depend on the gravity subleading. This describes the 6th order GJMS operator
[
61
,
67
]
. Up to a different normalization of the Bach tensor, the above form coincides with that from
[
68
]
.

Proposition 6.8
.

For
N^{*}=2\ell
, i.e.
-w=\frac{d}{2}-\ell
, gPDE
\widehat{E}_{B}^{\varphi}
describes conformal scalar field
\widehat{\varphi}
with an additional unconstrained subleading field
\psi
. The leading
\widehat{\varphi}
is subject to the equation of the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\sim(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{l}\widehat{\varphi}+\dots

(6.30)

which doesn’t involve the subleading
\psi
. Moreover, for
l\,{\leqslant}\,\frac{d}{2}
or, in other words,
w\,{\leqslant}\,0
, this equation does not involve the subleading gravitational field
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
and coincides with order-
2\ell
GJMS operator.

When
\mathcal{P}
does involve the gravitational subleading, we refer to it as to the
extended
GJMS.

14
14

14

Note that if one is interested in global solutions to the bulk equations rather than in the near-bounbdary
analysis, it turns out that the subleading is not unconstrained but is actually uniquely determined as a functional of the leading if one asks solution to behave well in the interior. The respective functional is called Dirichlet-to-Neumann map. Restricting the extended GJMS to the globally well defined gravity background then gives a generally nonlocal operator defined on the conformal geometry.

Proof.

The proof that the operator has this form is completely analogous to the proof of Proposition
5.6
and uses equations (
6.19
), (
6.18
), (
6.22
). Tracking the leading order in
\nabla_{(A)}\widehat{\varphi}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=(\widehat{b}^{\varphi})^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{2l-2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\varphi=\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}^{(2l-2)}+\dots\propto(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{2}\widehat{\varphi}^{(2l-4)}+\dots

(6.31)

and so on until it terminates at
\widehat{\varphi}^{(0)}
.

The proof that this equation does not involve the subleading gravitational field for
l\,{\leqslant}\,\frac{d}{2}
is slightly more subtle. To begin with, note that the subleading gravitational field can enter this equation only through the vector fields
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
,
N\,{\geqslant}\,D-3
. Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\sum_{i=0}^{2l-2}C^{i}_{2l-2}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(2l-2-i)}\sum_{j=0}^{i}C^{j}_{i}\mathcal{D}^{(i-j)|A}\widehat{\varphi}^{(j)}

(6.32)

we see that the subleading
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
definitely do not enter this equation when
(2l-2)<D-3
or equivalently
l<\frac{d}{2}
15
15

15

Strictly speaking, we should also verify that the subleading term does not enter the equations for
\widehat{\varphi}^{(N)}
,
N\,{\leqslant}\,2l-2
, but since the equations for these functions have the same structure, only of lower order, this is obviously satisfied for them as well.
. It remains to verify that this still holds for
l=\frac{d}{2}
. The only terms that can produce
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
are

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(D-3)}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}+\nabla_{A}\mathcal{D}^{(D-3)|A}\widehat{\varphi}.

(6.33)

Using (
6.21
) it is clear that the gravity subleading can enter only through the following combination:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{AB}\Gamma^{B}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}

(6.34)

but this vanishes since
\Gamma^{B}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}\propto g^{AB}\widehat{\varphi}
, as can be easily verified by a direct computation, and
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
is traceless.
∎

Note that for an odd-dimensional boundary one can consistently set gravitational subleading to zero by imposing the additional condition
\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{T}_{AB}=0
, giving a conformal field
\varphi
defined on a generic conformal geometry background for all
l\,{\geqslant}\,1
. Indeed, for
d
-odd
the gravitational subleading is unconstrained and describes a generic conformal geometry.
At the same time, for
d
even, we cannot set the gravitational subleading to vanish while the leading is subject to the obstruction equation. In this case
\varphi
is guaranteed to be conformal only if the background is off-shell. However, a more careful analysis shows that for
l\,{\leqslant}\,d/2
, these equations are conformally-invariant on any conformal geometry background.

Finally, let us mention that we have omitted a number of special cases. Namely, the case not covered by Proposition
6.4
is where both
N^{*}
and
d
are odd while the gravitational subleading is not set to zero. Without try to be maximally general let us give an example of such systems:

Proposition 6.9
.

Let the dimension of the boundary be odd and
N^{*}=d+2
(i.e.,
w=1
). Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=-\frac{D-2}{2}\mathcal{T}^{AB}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\widehat{\varphi}

(6.35)

Proof.

Similarly to Proposition
6.4
,one shows that
\widehat{\varphi}^{(i)}=0
for odd
i<d+2
. Also, analogously to Proposition
4.16
we have
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(j)}=0
for odd
i<d-2
. Hence,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{P}&=\sum_{i=0}^{D-1}C^{i}_{D-1}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(D-1-i)}\sum_{j=0}^{i}C^{j}_{i}\mathcal{D}^{(i-j)|A}\widehat{\varphi}^{(j)}=\mathcal{D}^{(D-1)}_{A}\nabla^{A}\widehat{\varphi}=\\
&=-\frac{1}{D-1}\sum_{i=0}^{D-3}d^{i}_{D-1}\widehat{T}^{(i)|A}{}_{C}\mathcal{D}^{(D-3-i)|C}\nabla_{A}\widehat{\varphi}=-\frac{D-2}{2}\mathcal{T}^{AC}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{C}\widehat{\varphi}.\end{split}

(6.36)

∎

Constraint (
6.35
) defines a nonlinear conformally invariant equation involving the conformal fields
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
and
\widehat{\varphi}
defined on the generic conformal geometry background. A possible interpretation of this system is to ask this equation to hold for any configuration of
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
, i.e. to require trace-free part of
\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\widehat{\varphi}
to vanish. This condition can be recognised as the almost Einstein equation on
\widehat{\varphi}
. Note that
w=1
is precisely the weight required for the conformal invariance of the almost Einstein equation. One can also speculate that this is the Killing condition for the depth-
2
conformal spin-
2
gauge field
[
69
,
69
]
, see also
[
27
,
70
]
, if one identifies
\widehat{\varphi}
as the corresponding gauge parameter.

6.3
Yang-Mills theory

Now we turn to the example where the matter field defined on the gravity background possesses gauge invariance and its equations of motion are generally nonlinear. The Yang-Mills theory can be defined on a general pseudo-Riemannian background (no constraints on background metric) and the fibre of
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
is coordinatized by the components of the Yang-Mills field
A_{b}^{I}
, ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{I}
and all their space-time derivatives. We often use the gauge Lie algebra valued
A_{b},\mathcal{C}
as short-hand notations. The BRST differential is defined in a standard way:

QA_{b}=\xi^{c}D_{c}A_{b}+(D_{b}\xi^{c})A_{c}+D_{b}\mathcal{C}+[A_{b}{,}\,\mathcal{C}]\,,\hskip 20.00003ptQ\mathcal{C}=\xi^{c}D_{c}\mathcal{C}-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathcal{C}{,}\,\mathcal{C}]

(6.37)

and encode the usual transformation law of the Yang-Mills field and ghost under diffeomorphisms and Yang-Mills gauge transformations.

The Yang-Mills equations

\widetilde{Y}^{b}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\widetilde{g}|}}(D_{a}+[A_{a}{,}\,\cdot])(\sqrt{|\widetilde{g}|}\,\,\widetilde{g}^{ac}\widetilde{g}^{bd}F_{cd})\,,\hskip 20.00003ptF_{cd}\equiv D_{c}A_{b}-D_{b}A_{c}+[A_{c}{,}\,A_{b}]\,,

(6.38)

can be rewritten in terms of
g_{ab}\equiv\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}_{ab}
and then it turns out that
Y^{b}\equiv\Omega^{D-4}\widetilde{Y}^{b}
seen as a local function in
A,g,\Omega
is regular at
\Omega=0
. The jet-prolongations of
Y^{b}
together with
\mathcal{I}_{cl}
generate the ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}_{cl}
defining the gPDE that describes YM coupled to the conformal-like GR. Note that we took
A,\mathcal{C}
to be inert under the Weyl transformations and hence the uplift of YM system from a generic Riemannian background to conformal-like GR is straightforward.

According to the general prescription described in Section
6.1
the next step is to pull back
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\to E_{cl}
to
E_{\mathrm{red}}\subset E_{cl}
. However, it turns out to be very convenient to first perform an equivalent reduction of
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\to E_{cl}
that only affects the fibre, i.e. the YM sector. This reduction is well known in the literature in the case of YM on the gravity background. But because the lift to conformal-like reformulation of gravity is trivial in our case (
A
,
\mathcal{C}
are inert under Weyl), the reduction is identical and amounts to the restriction to the surface singled out by:

D_{(a_{1}}D_{a_{2}}\ldots D_{a_{k-1}}A_{a_{k})}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003ptQ\left(D_{(a_{1}}D_{a_{2}}\ldots D_{a_{k-1}}A_{a_{k})}\right)=0.

(6.39)

As the remaining fibre coordinates one can take

\{\nabla_{((a)}F_{b)c},\mathcal{C}\}
pulled back to the surface (
6.39
), where
\nabla_{a}\equiv[Q{,}\,\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}$}\kern 1.0pt}]
.

The next step is to pullback the reduced bundle to
E_{\mathrm{red}}\subset E_{cl}
and then pullback the resulting gPDE (as a bundle over
T[1]X
) to the boundary
T[1]\Sigma
giving a gauge PDE
(E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}},Q,\mathcal{I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}})
over
(E,Q,\mathcal{I})
, where
\mathcal{I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
is generated by the ideal
\mathcal{I}
of the gravity sector and
\nabla_{a}
-prolongations of

\displaystyle Y_{b}=(4-D)(\nabla^{a}\Omega)F_{ab}+\Omega\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\,,

(6.40)

where we keep using the same notations for the fibre coordinates and generators
Y_{b}
. Directly analogous form of the YM equations was employed in
[
25
]
within a different framework and called conformally-compact YM equation. It is easy to see that for
\Omega=const
(
6.40
) reduces to the ordinary Yang-Mills equation.

The action of
Q
on the fibre coordinates of
E^{YM}
, namely
\{\nabla_{((a)}F_{b)c},\mathcal{C}\}
, is determined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q\mathcal{C}=-\frac{1}{2}[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{a}\xi^{b}F_{ab},\\
&QF_{ab}=\xi^{c}\nabla_{c}F_{ab}+C_{a}{}^{c}F_{cb}+C_{b}{}^{c}F_{ac}-[\mathcal{C},F_{ab}]\,.\end{split}

(6.41)

Recall that the action of
Q
on any function of the form
\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\ldots F_{cd}
(e.g. on coordinate functions) is determined by the above relations and
[Q{,}\,\nabla_{a}]=0
. If one disregards gravity sector by e.g. picking a fixed background, (
6.41
) define a minimal model of the gPDE formulation of YM theory. These relations are well-known in the context of local BRST cohomology
[
71
,
33
,
58
]
.

The ghost degree
1
coordinates on
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
are spilt into the ghosts of the conformal-like gravity sector
C^{M}=\{\xi^{a},\lambda,C_{a}{}^{b},\lambda_{a}\}
and the YM ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{I}
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{I}t_{I}
, where
t_{I}
are basis elements of the YM gauge algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Introducing
R_{I}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathcal{C}^{I}},Q]
, one finds:

\displaystyle[R_{I},R_{J}]=-U^{K}_{IJ}R_{K},\hskip 10.00002pt[R_{I},[\frac{\partial}{\partial{C}^{M}},Q]]=0\,,

(6.42)

where
U^{K}_{IJ}
are the structure constants of
\mathfrak{g}
, i.e.
[t_{I}{,}\,t_{J}]=U_{IJ}^{K}t_{K}
. In the gravity sector, all the formulas (
3.11
) for the commutators remain intact save for

\displaystyle[\nabla_{a},\nabla_{b}]=-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cab}\Delta^{c}{}_{d}-\mathrm{C}_{dab}\Gamma^{d}-F_{ab}^{I}R_{I}-(Q\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cab})\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{d}}-(Q\mathrm{C}_{dab})\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{d}}-(QF^{I}_{ab})\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathcal{C}^{I}}.

(6.43)

This happens because the underlying Lie algebra is the direct sum of the algebra in the gravity sector and the YM gauge algebra.

The next step is to split the space-time index as
\{a\}=\{\Omega,A\}
. Then the YM Bianchi identities
\nabla_{[a}F_{bc]}=0

encoded in
Q^{2}\mathcal{C}^{I}=0
give:

\nabla_{\Omega}F_{BC}=\nabla_{B}F_{\Omega C}-\nabla_{C}F_{\Omega B}\,.

(6.44)

These can be used to chose the following fibre coordinates:

\displaystyle\{\nabla_{(C)}J^{(N)}_{A},\nabla_{((A)}F_{B)C},\mathcal{C}\},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002ptJ^{(N)}_{A}=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}F_{\Omega A}\,.

(6.45)

Then we can rewrite the
\nabla_{a}
-prolongations of the Yang-Mills equations
Y_{b}
as
\nabla_{A}
-prolongations of
Y^{(N)}_{b}
for
N\,{\geqslant}\,0
. Analogously to the scalar field case,
Y^{(N)}_{b}
can be computed modulo the ideals
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
:

Proposition 6.10
.

The total ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
on
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
is generated by
the gravity ideal
\mathcal{I}
along with functions

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Y^{(0)}_{B}=(4-D)J^{(0)}_{B}+\mathcal{K}_{B}\,,\\
&Y^{(N)}_{B}=(4-D+N)J^{(N)}_{B}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}F_{AB}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)},\quad N\,{\geqslant}\,1\,,\\
&Y^{(D-3)}_{\Omega}=(D-3)\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}\nabla^{A}J^{(0)}_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(D-4)}\,,\end{split}

(6.46)

and their
\nabla_{A}
-prolongations.

Proof.

Y^{(N)}_{b}
are easily computed by acting with
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}
on (
6.40
) and taking into account that
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
;
\nabla_{\Omega}\Omega-\widetilde{\Lambda},\nabla_{A}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}_{B}
. The only nontrivial point is to show that
Y^{(N)}_{\Omega}
, for
N\neq D-3
can be expressed as a linear combination of
\nabla^{A}Y^{(i)}_{A}
with
i\,{\leqslant}\,N
, and therefore they can be omitted from the set of generators. To see this, notice that

\displaystyle\nabla^{b}Y_{b}=(3-D)\nabla^{a}\Omega\nabla^{b}F_{ab}+\Omega\nabla^{b}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}=(3-D)\nabla^{a}\Omega\nabla^{b}F_{ab}\,,

(6.47)

where we made use of
\nabla^{a}\nabla^{b}F_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}[\nabla^{a},\nabla^{b}]F_{ab}=0
. Applying
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}
to (
6.47
) and using
\nabla^{a}Y_{a}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-1}Y^{(1)}+\nabla^{A}Y_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
together with
Y^{(N)}_{\Omega}=N\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}J_{A}^{(0)}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{(3-D-N)}{N\widetilde{\Lambda}}Y_{\Omega}^{(N)}=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}Y_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)}\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{N-1}I(Y_{B}^{(i)},\nabla_{A})\cup\mathcal{K}^{(N)}.

(6.48)

The inclusion follows from the decomposition of
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}Y_{A}
as a sum
\mathcal{D}^{(N-1-i)|A}Y^{(i)}_{A}
and from the fact that
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(i)}=[Q,\nu_{A}^{(i)}]
, where
\nu_{A}^{(i)}
has degree
-1
and preserves
\mathcal{K}^{(i+1)}
by Theorem
4.5
.
∎

Note that equation (
6.48
) follows from equation (
6.47
) which in turn is the Weyl-covariant extension of the usual Noether identities for the Yang-Mills equation. The analogous phenomenon occurs in the gravity sector, see Appendix
A
.

Similarly to the analysis of gravity and scalar field, almost all the equations in (
6.46
) can be solved with respect to a part of the coordinates. More specifically, we introduce the Yang-Mills extension
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
of the off-shell boundary gPDE
\widehat{E}
from Section
4.4
.

Definition 6.11
.

Bundle
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\to\widehat{E}
is the submanifold of
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
singled out by the ideal generated by
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}=\ker\widehat{b}

together with

\displaystyle J^{(N)}_{B}=\frac{N}{(D-4-N)}\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}F_{AB},\hskip 10.00002ptN\neq D-4.

(6.49)

The embedding
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\hookrightarrow E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
is denoted by
\widehat{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
and for any function
f
on
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
we define
\widehat{f}\equiv\widehat{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}^{*}f
.

Because
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
can be obtained as subbundle of
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
pulled back to
\widehat{E}\subset E
it is naturally a bundle over
\widehat{E}
with projection
\widehat{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
.
Note that, when restricted to functions that are constant along the fibres of
\pi^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
,
\widehat{b}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}^{*}
can be identified with the
\widehat{b}^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\widehat{E})
in the gravity sector.

Just like in the case of gravity and scalar field,
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
is not a
Q
-submanifold of
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
, but we can still define a natural
Q
-structure on
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
. To construct it, we consider the submanifold singled out by
\mathcal{K}^{(D-3)}
together with the equations (
6.49
) for
0\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-5
. This is a
Q
-submanifold which is also a bundle over
E^{(D-3)}
in a natural way. We denote it by
E^{(D-5)|{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}\to E^{(D-3)}
. Then, we observe that the subalgebra of functions on
E^{YM|(D-5)}
generated by the following coordinate functions on
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
:

\displaystyle\xi^{B},C_{B}{}^{C},\lambda,\lambda^{B},\hskip 10.00002ptg_{BC},\nabla_{((A)}\mathrm{W}^{B}{}_{CD)E},\nabla_{(A)}T_{BC}^{(D-3)}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{((A)}F_{B)C},\,\mathcal{C},\,\nabla_{(A)}J_{B}^{(D-4)},\hskip 10.00002pt|A|\,{\geqslant}\,0

(6.50)

pulled back to
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|(D-5)}
is closed under
Q
. In the gravity sector, this observation is entirely analogous to the one in Section
4.4
. For
\nabla_{(A)}J_{B}^{(D-4)}
it
it is a direct generalisation of Proposition
B.2
while for the remaining generators the statement is immediate. It follows
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|(D-5)}
can be considered a
Q
-bundle
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|(D-5)}\to\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\,\prime}
. Indeed, one simply takes functions in (
4.43
) as functions pulled back from
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\prime}
. Moreover,
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
can be understood as a section of this bundle and the desired
Q
-structure on
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
is simply obtained as a pushforward of that on
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\prime}
.

Again following the logic of Section
4.4

one can describe functions on
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
as a subquotient of functions on
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
as follows: considereng the subalgebra of
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
-projectable functions in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}})
, consisting of functions whose restriction to
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|(D-5)}
is constant along the fibres of
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|(D-5)}\to E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\prime}
. The quotient of
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
-projectable functions by those vanishing on
E^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|(D-5)}

is naturally identified with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}})
. Moreover, for

\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
-projectable functions we have
Q\widehat{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}^{*}f=\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}Qf
. In particular,
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
-projectable functions include all
\widehat{E}
-projectable functions on
E
, e.g. , e.g.
T^{(N)}_{AB}\,,\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-3
, pulled back to
E^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
. It is straightforward to see that
J^{(N)}_{A}
are also
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
-projectable if
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
.

As a coordinate system on the fibres of
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}_{B}
we take

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}=\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\mathcal{C}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{((C)}\widehat{F}_{A)B}=\nabla_{((C)}\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}F_{A)B}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{(C)}\mathcal{J}_{B}\equiv\nabla_{(C)}\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}J^{(D-4)}_{B}\,,

(6.51)

The ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}\equiv\widehat{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}^{*}\mathcal{I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
is generated by the pullback of the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
from
\widehat{E}
and the
\nabla_{A}
-prolongations of functions

\mathcal{Y}_{B}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-5}\nabla^{A}F_{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{Y}\equiv\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}\nabla^{A}J_{A}.

(6.52)

Note that, unlike the scalar field case, ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
is always strictly
Q
-invariant (recall that
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{\varphi}
is generally
Q
-invariant modulo
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
only). In particular, the on-shell boundary YM system is well defined on an arbitrary, not necessarily obstruction-flat, background. This is a direct consequence of the fact that
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
appearing in formulas (
6.46
) for
N\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
vanishes upon pullback to
\widehat{E}
.

The action of
Q
on some of the coordinates is immediately obtained by restricting (
6.41
) to
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}_{B}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q\widehat{\mathcal{C}}=-\frac{1}{2}[\widehat{\mathcal{C}},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}]+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\xi}^{A}\widehat{\xi}^{B}\widehat{F}_{AB},\\
&Q\widehat{F}_{AB}=\widehat{\xi}^{C}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB}+\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{C}\widehat{F}_{CB}+\widehat{C}_{B}{}^{C}\widehat{F}_{AC}-[\widehat{\mathcal{C}},\widehat{F}_{AB}]\,.\end{split}

(6.53)

Notice that the action of
Q
on
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
and
\nabla_{((C)}\widehat{F}_{A)B}
does not affect the sector
\mathcal{J}_{B}
, and therefore
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}_{B}
itself is a
Q
-bundle with the fibre coordinates
\nabla_{(A)}\mathcal{J}_{B}
. This is identical to the situation we had in the case of pure gravity and scalar field. Now
\mathcal{J}_{A}
is interpreted as a YM subleading.

Just like in the case of gravity and scalar field, gauge PDE
(\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}},Q)
considered without restricting to the zero locus of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
is a good starting point to discuss the explicit space-time realisation of equations of motion because if we disregard the gravity part, i.e. restrict to a particular solution of
(\widehat{E},Q)
, the system does not encode any nontrivial differential equations. More precisely,

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
\sigma_{G}:T[1]\Sigma\to\widehat{E}
be a solution to
(\widehat{E},Q)
and
A_{B}(x)
and
\mathbf{J}_{B}(x)
are coefficients of generic 1-form on
\Sigma
with values in the gauge algebra. Then there exists a unique solution
\sigma:T[1]\Sigma\to\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
such that

\widehat{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}~\circ~\sigma=\sigma_{G}
and

\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{C})=A_{B}(x)\theta^{B}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{J}_{B})=\mathbf{J}_{B}(x)\,.

(6.54)

In other words, the restriction of
(\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}},Q)
to
\sigma_{G}
is an off-shell system.
16
16

16

The definition of an off-shell gauge PDE can be made precise as follows: it is a gPDE that is equivalent to a jet-bundle of a nonnegatively-graded fibre bundle, with
Q
-structure being
Q=\mathrm{d_{h}}+\gamma
, where
\gamma
is an evolutionary homological vector field and
\mathrm{d_{h}}
is the horizontal differential seen as a vector field.
We see that solutions are 1:1 with unconstrained configurations of fields
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
and
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{J}_{B})
. In particular, the equations of motion in terms of
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
and
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{J}_{B})
can be explicitly written as

\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{Y}_{B})=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{Y})=0\,.

(6.55)

More detailed description of the solution space
\widehat{E}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
is given in Appendix
F
. Let us emphasize that in the above Proposition
\sigma_{G}
is a solution of the off-shell boundary GR. In other words, we do not impose
\sigma_{G}^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{I}}=0
.

To proceed further, we need the action of
Q
on functions
\widehat{J}_{A}^{(N)}
and coordinate functions
\mathcal{J}_{B}
in particular. The following is a straightforward generalisation of Proposition
4.11
:

Proposition 6.13
.

On
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
one has:

Q\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A}=\widehat{\xi}^{B}\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A}+\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{B}\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{B}+\widehat{\lambda}^{B}\Gamma_{B}\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A}-[\widehat{\mathcal{C}},\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A}]-(N+1)\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A},\hskip 10.00002ptN\,{\leqslant}\,D-4\,,

(6.56)

where

\Gamma_{C}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(N)}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{C}^{(N-2-i)}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(i)}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{F}^{(N-1)}_{CB}\,.

(6.57)

We are now ready to formulate the Yang-Mills theory extension of the boundary calculus, introduced in Theorem
4.12
:

Theorem 6.14
.

(Yang-Mills boundary calculus)

The system
\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A}\,,\widehat{F}^{(N)}_{AB}\,,\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}
on
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
satisfies the following relations

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{F}^{(N)}_{AB}=\widehat{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(\nabla_{A}J_{B}-\nabla_{B}J_{A})\,,\quad 1\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-5\,,\\
&\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{B}=\frac{N\widetilde{\Lambda}}{D-4-N}\widehat{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}^{*}(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{A}F_{AB}),\quad 0\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-5,\end{split}

(6.58)

and for any degree zero function
f
and
1\,{\leqslant}\,N\,{\leqslant}\,D-5
one has:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}f=(-\widehat{J}^{(N-1),D}{}_{CA}\Delta^{C}{}_{D}+\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{\Lambda}N}\widehat{T}^{(N)}_{CA}\Gamma^{C}-J^{(N-1)|I}{}_{A}R_{I}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\widehat{T}^{(i),C}{}_{A}\mathcal{D}_{C}^{(N-2-i)})f\,.\\
\end{split}

(6.59)

Moreover,

\widehat{F}^{(0)}_{AB}=\widehat{F}_{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{J}^{(0)}_{A}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(0)}_{A}\equiv\nabla_{A}\,,\\

(6.60)

and all the higher order
F^{(\cdot)}_{AB},J^{(\cdot)}_{BC},\mathcal{D}^{(\cdot)}_{A}
are recursively determined by the above relations and the gravity boundary calculus Theorem
4.12
, where each expression of the form
\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f
is evaluated as

\displaystyle\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}f=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\widehat{f}^{(i)}\,,

(6.61)

and the action of
\Gamma_{A}
on
\widehat{J}^{(N)}_{A}
is given by (
6.13
).

Proof.

The first line in (
6.58
) can be proven simply by using the Bianchi identity (
6.44
) in the form
\nabla_{\Omega}F_{AB}=\nabla_{A}J_{B}-\nabla_{B}J_{A}
and acting on it with
\widehat{b}^{*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}
. The second line follows from Proposition
6.10
and the definition of
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
. To prove (
6.59
), note that when acting on functions of ghost degree
0
, as follows from (
6.43
), the formula for
[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]
differs only by a term of the form
J^{I}_{B}R_{I}
. Using the fact that
[\nabla_{A},R_{I}]=0
, it is straightforward to see that the formula for
\mathcal{D}^{(N)}_{A}
in the gravity sector in Theorem
4.12
differs only by a term of the form
\widehat{J}^{(N-1)|I}_{A}R_{I}
.∎

It is easy to see that, as in the case of pure gravity, the generators of the ideal
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}
can be expressed in terms of the boundary calculus:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{Y}_{B}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}^{(D-5)}_{AB}+\sum_{i=0}^{D-6}C^{i}_{D-5}\mathcal{D}^{(D-5-i)|A}\widehat{F}_{AB}^{(i)},\\
&\mathcal{Y}\equiv\nabla^{A}\mathcal{J}_{A}+\sum_{i=0}^{D-5}C_{D-4}^{i}\mathcal{D}^{(D-4-i)|A}\widehat{J}^{(i)}_{A}.\end{split}

(6.62)

Functions
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}
does not involve the subleading sector and corresponds to a certain Weyl-invariant equation imposed on the leading sector while
{\mathcal{Y}}
is the modified conservation law for subleading
\mathcal{J}_{A}
. One may view
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
as direct analogues of the obstruction tensor
\mathcal{O}_{AB}
and the modified conservation law
\mathcal{O}_{A}
in the sector of gravity. In particular, an analogue of Proposition
4.9
is the following:

Proposition 6.15
.

On
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}
we have:

\displaystyle\nabla^{A}{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\Gamma_{A}{\mathcal{Y}}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}(D-4){\mathcal{Y}}_{A}\,,

(6.63)

where
\Gamma_{A}\equiv[\frac{\partial}{\partial\widehat{\lambda}^{A}},Q]
.

Proof.

Again, the first equation is a distant consequence of the Noether identities for the Yang-Mills equations, which can be obtained by applying
\widehat{b}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}}^{*}
to (
6.48
) with
N=D-3
. The second equation states that the Weyl invariance of the conservation equation for the Yang-Mills subleading
\mathcal{J}_{A}
is equivalent to imposing the obstruction equation
{\mathcal{Y}}_{B}=0
and can be proved by a straightforward calculation using Lemma
B.1
.
∎

Finally, the explicit form of the conformal YM equation and the conformal conservation equation can be found in terms of the above boundary calculus by using Proposition
6.12
and repeating the analysis of Section
5.3
in the YM sector. In the rest of this section we discuss the structure of the boundary YM system in more details.

A direct analogue of Proposition
4.16
is the following:

Proposition 6.16
.

For any odd integer
i
such that
1\,{\leqslant}\,i\,{\leqslant}\,D-4
we have:

\widehat{J}^{(i-1)}_{A}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{A}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{AB}=0\,.

(6.64)

The case of an odd-dimensional boundary is again relatively simple (see Proposition
5.2
, the proof is analogous).

Proposition 6.17
.

For
d
odd we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{B}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{Y}=\nabla^{A}\mathcal{J}_{A},\hskip 10.00002pt\Gamma_{B}\mathcal{J}_{A}=0.

(6.65)

It follows that if we fix a background solution
\sigma_{G}
in the boundary GR sector, the system describes unconstrained field
A_{B}(x)
and field
\mathbf{J}_{B}(x)
(current) subject to the conservation condition that follows from
\sigma^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}=0
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}^{g}\mathbf{J}^{A}(x)=0\,,

(6.66)

where
\nabla_{A}^{g}
is the Levi-Civita covariant derivative, twisted by the Yang-Mills field
A_{B}(x)
. Weyl transformations,
\mathbf{J}_{A}
can be read-off from
Q\mathcal{J}
. Assuming that
\sigma_{G}
is taken in the metric gauge one finds:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bar{\lambda}}\mathbf{J}_{A}=-(D-3)\bar{\lambda}(x)\mathbf{J}_{A}\,,

(6.67)

where
\bar{\lambda}(x)
is the Weyl transformation parameter. In other words

\mathbf{J}_{A}
has Weyl weight
-(D-3)
.

As before, we begin the analysis of the even-dimensional boundary case by first considering a few examples in lower dimensions:

Example 6.18
.

In the case of
d=4
one has

\mathcal{Y}_{B}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{Y}=\nabla^{A}\mathcal{J}_{A},\hskip 10.00002pt\Gamma_{B}\mathcal{J}_{A}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{F}_{BA}.

(6.68)

Choosing a background solution
\sigma_{G}
to
\widehat{E}\to T[1]\Sigma
in the metric-like gauge (see Section
5.3
), the solution space for
\widehat{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{YM}}|_{\sigma_{G}}
is described by two
\mathfrak{g}
-valued fields,
A_{B}(x)
and
\mathbf{J}_{B}(x)
, subject to equations

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{A}{}_{B}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{A}^{g}\mathbf{J}^{A}=0\,,

(6.69)

where
F_{AB}
is the usual Yang-Mills curvature for
A_{B}(x)
.
See Appendix
F
for details.
Note that the leading part of the boundary system is the usual 4d YM. This is compatible with the fact that the Yang-Mills equation is conformally invariant in dimension 4.

Under Weyl transformations,
\mathbf{J}_{A}
transforms as

\displaystyle\delta_{\bar{\lambda}}\mathbf{J}_{A}=\widetilde{\Lambda}(\partial^{B}\bar{\lambda})F_{BA}-2\bar{\lambda}\mathbf{J}_{A}

(6.70)

Example 6.19
.

In the case of
d=6
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{Y}_{B}=\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}}{2}((\nabla_{C}\nabla^{C})\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB}+4\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{DA}{}_{B}\widehat{F}_{DA}-2[\widehat{F}_{AB},\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}^{CA}]),\\
&\mathcal{Y}=\nabla^{A}\mathcal{J}_{A},\quad\Gamma_{C}\mathcal{J}_{A}=3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}(\nabla_{C}\nabla^{B}\widehat{F}_{BA}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}\nabla^{B}\widehat{F}_{BC}).\end{split}

(6.71)

Analogously to the previous example, the equations satisfied by
A_{B}(x)
and
\mathbf{J}_{A}(x)
read as (see Appendix
F
for details):

\Box^{g}\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{A}{}_{B}+2\partial_{C}PF^{C}{}_{B}+4P^{AC}\nabla^{g}_{A}F_{CB}-3P\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{A}{}_{B}+4C^{DA}{}_{B}F_{DA}-2[F_{AB},\nabla_{C}^{g}F^{CA}]=0

(6.72)

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}^{g}\mathbf{J}^{A}+\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}P^{AC}(\nabla^{g}_{C}\nabla^{g|B}F_{BA})=0\,,

(6.73)

where
P_{AB}(x)
is a Schouten tensor and
C_{ABC}(x)
is a Cotton tensor for the background conformal geometry described by metric
g_{AB}(x)
.
Following
[
25
]
, where (
6.72
) was originally found, we call it the higher conformal Yang-Mills equation in dimension
6
.
The subleading field
\mathbf{J}_{A}
, in addition to satisfying the modified conservation equation (
6.73
), also transforms nontrivially under Weyl rescalings:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bar{\lambda}}\mathbf{J}_{A}=3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\partial^{C}\bar{\lambda}(\nabla_{C}^{g}\nabla^{g}_{B}F^{B}{}_{A}+2P^{D}{}_{C}F_{DA}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{A}^{g}\nabla^{g}_{B}F^{B}{}_{C}-P^{D}{}_{A}F_{DC})-4\bar{\lambda}\mathbf{J}_{A}

(6.74)

Note that the inhomogeneous terms originate from
\Gamma_{C}\mathcal{J}_{A}
term in
Q\mathcal{J}_{A}
.

Let us give some details on how (
6.71
) is derived. Using Proposition
6.16
together with Theorem
6.14
we obtain:

\displaystyle\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}=\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}}{2}\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{F}^{(2)}_{AB}=\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}.

(6.75)

Then from (
6.62
) and (
6.59
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{B}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}^{(2)}_{AB}+\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{F}_{AB}=\nabla^{A}(\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A})+\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{DA}{}_{B}\widehat{F}_{DA}-[\widehat{F}_{AB},\widehat{J}^{(1)|A}]\,,

(6.76)

where we have also made use of the fact that
\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\mathrm{C}_{CAB}
thanks to (
4.57
). Using

\displaystyle\nabla^{A}\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}=[\nabla^{A},\nabla_{B}]\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}=-\widehat{\mathrm{C}}_{D}{}^{A}{}_{B}\Gamma^{D}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}-\widehat{F}^{J|A}{}_{B}R_{J}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{DAB}\widehat{F}^{I}_{DA}+[\widehat{F}^{A}{}_{B},\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}]

(6.77)

and substituting the previously computed
\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}
one finds the explicit expression for
\mathcal{Y}_{B}
from (
6.71
). The formula for
\mathcal{Y}
is obtained from (
6.62
) using
\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}=0
.
Finally, using the above
in (
6.57
) one finds the explicit expression for
\Gamma_{C}\mathcal{J}_{A}
.

Example 6.20
.

In the case
d=8
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}_{B}=&(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{B}+\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda^{2}}}{2}(\widehat{B}^{AC}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB}-\widehat{F}_{CA}\nabla^{A}\widehat{B}_{B}{}^{C}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{B}^{A}{}_{B}\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{CA})+\\
&+6\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{C}^{AC}{}_{B}(2\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}_{C}^{(1)}-\nabla_{C}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A})+2[\widehat{J}^{(3)|A},\widehat{F}_{AB}]+6[\widehat{J}^{(1)|A},2\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(1)}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}_{A}^{(1)}]_{,}\\
\mathcal{Y}=&\nabla^{A}\mathcal{J}_{A}+\frac{15}{4}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\widehat{B}^{AC}\nabla_{C}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}-5[\widehat{J}^{(3)|A},\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}].\end{split}

(6.78)

where
\widehat{J}^{(1)|I}_{A}
and
\widehat{J}^{(3)|I}_{A}
are computed using Theorem
6.14
and are given by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}=\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}}{4}\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB},\\
&\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{B}=\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{8}((\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{CB}+8\widehat{\mathrm{C}}^{DA}{}_{B}\widehat{F}_{DA}-2[\widehat{F}_{AB},\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{C}{}^{A}]).\end{split}

(6.79)

The details of the derivation the explicit form of
\mathcal{Y}_{B}
and
\mathcal{Y}
can be found in Appendix
E
.

Repeating the analysis of the previous examples one finds that
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{Y}_{B}=0
is a higher conformal Yang-Mills equation in dimension
8
, which for a fixed metric-like background
\sigma_{G}
can be written as:

\Box^{g}j^{(3)}_{B}+2(\partial^{D}P+2P^{AD}\nabla_{A}^{g})(2\nabla_{D}^{g}j^{(1)}_{B}-\nabla_{B}^{g}j^{(1)}_{D}+8P_{D}{}^{C}F_{CB}-4P_{B}{}^{C}F_{CD})+\\
+2(-5P_{AD}P^{AD}j^{(1)}_{B}+8P_{AE}P^{AD}\nabla^{g}_{D}F^{E}{}_{B}+2P_{AD}P^{A}{}_{B}j^{(1)|D})-5Pj^{(3)}_{B}+\\
+4(B^{AC}\nabla^{g}_{C}F_{AB}-F^{CA}(\nabla^{g}_{A}B_{BC}-4P^{E}{}_{A}C_{BEC}-4P^{E}{}_{A}C_{CEB})+\frac{1}{2}B^{A}{}_{B}\nabla^{g}_{C}F^{C}{}_{A})+\\
+4C^{AC}{}_{B}(2\nabla^{g}_{A}j_{C}^{(1)}-\nabla^{g}_{C}j^{(1)}_{A}+8P_{A}{}^{D}F_{DC}-4P_{C}{}^{D}F_{DA})+\\
+2[j^{(3)|A},F_{AB}]+[j^{(1)|A},2\nabla^{g}_{A}j_{B}^{(1)}-\nabla^{g}_{B}j_{A}^{(1)}+8P_{A}{}^{C}F_{CB}-4P_{B}{}^{C}F_{CA}]=0\,,

(6.80)

where
B_{AB}(x)
is a Bach tensor and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&j^{(1)}_{B}=\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{A}{}_{B},\\
&j^{(3)}_{B}=\Box^{g}\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{A}{}_{B}+4\partial_{C}PF^{C}{}_{B}+8P^{AC}\nabla^{g}_{A}F_{CB}-3P\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{A}{}_{B}+8C^{DA}{}_{B}F_{DA}-2[F_{AB},\nabla_{C}^{g}F^{CA}]\,.\end{split}

(6.81)

To the best of our knowledge, this operator has not been found before.

From
\sigma^{*}{\mathcal{Y}}=0
we have the following conservation equation:

\sigma^{*}\mathcal{Y}=\nabla^{g}_{A}\mathbf{J}^{A}+\frac{5\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}}{4}P^{CB}(C_{C}{}^{D}{}_{B}j^{(1)}_{D}+B_{CD}F^{D}{}_{B}+3\nabla_{C}^{g}j_{B}^{(3)}+6P_{C}{}^{E}\nabla_{E}^{g}j^{(1)}_{B})+\\
+\frac{15\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}}{16}B^{AC}(\nabla^{g}_{C}j^{(1)}_{A})-\frac{15\widetilde{\Lambda}^{3}}{32}[j^{(3)|A},j^{(1)}_{A}].

(6.82)

For the derivation of equations (
6.80
) and (
6.82
), see Appendix
F
.

The general situation is described by:

Proposition 6.21
.

For
d
even, the boundary system describes higher conformal YM theory together with an additional field
\mathcal{J}_{A}
. The symbols of the equations of motion for these fields have the following structure:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{Y}}_{B}&\equiv(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{CB}+\dots,\\
{\mathcal{Y}}&\equiv\nabla^{A}\mathcal{J}_{A}+\sum_{i=0}^{d-4}C_{d-3}^{i}\mathcal{D}^{(d-3-i)|A}\widehat{J}^{(i)}_{A}\,.\end{split}

(6.83)

Moreover,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{B}
does not involve
\mathcal{T}_{AB}
,
\mathcal{J}_{A}
and their
\nabla_{A}
-derivatives.
\sigma^{*}({\mathcal{Y}}_{B})=0
is the (higher-dimensional) conformal YM equation on
A_{B}(x)\theta^{B}\equiv\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{C})
. The second equation gives a generalised conservation condition satisfied by the subleading
\mathbf{J}_{A}(x)\equiv\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{J}_{A})
.

The proof is a straightforward generalisation of that of Proposition
5.6
.

Remark 6.22
.

Suppose that we are given with an Einstein metric
g_{AB}(x)
and a YM field
A_{B}(x)
on
\Sigma
,
\dim\Sigma=4,6,8
such that YM equations are satisfied:
\nabla^{g}_{A}F^{AB}=0
. Then
A,g
also satisfy the higher conformal Yang-Mills equation, as can be seen from formulas (
6.72
),(
6.80
). This statement is a YM analogue of the known fact that higher conformal gravities contain Einstein sector
[
72
]
, see also
[
73
]
. These should extend to higher even dimensions.
Notice also, that conservation equations (
6.73
) and (
6.82
) reduce simply to
\nabla_{A}^{g}\mathbf{J}^{A}=0
over an Einstein-YM background.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
4.5

The ideal
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
is generated by
\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)}
together with

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\nabla_{(C)}(\Omega^{(N+2)}-\frac{1}{D}g_{\Omega\Omega}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega),\\
&\nabla_{(C)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}+\mathcal{K}_{B},\\
&\nabla_{(C)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega-\frac{1}{D}g_{AB}\nabla_{c}\nabla^{c}\Omega).\end{split}

(A.1)

Note that using

\displaystyle[\nabla_{a},\nabla_{b}]\nabla_{c}\Omega=-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{cab}\nabla_{d}\Omega-\mathrm{C}_{cab}\Omega

(A.2)

and the tracelessness of the Weyl and Cotton tensors one can show that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega=\nabla^{a}\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla_{a}\Omega-\mathrm{W}^{ca}{}_{\Omega a}\nabla_{c}\Omega-\mathrm{C}^{a}{}_{\Omega a}\Omega=g^{\Omega\Omega}\Omega^{(3)}+\nabla^{A}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}+\mathcal{K}_{B}.

(A.3)

Consequently, the generators of the ideal (
A.1
) for
N\,{\geqslant}\,1
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\nabla_{(C)}\Omega^{(N+2)},\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{(C)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)},\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{(C)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega.

(A.4)

The first step in the proof is formulated as a lemma.

Lemma A.1
.

a) Generators of the ideal
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)},\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega

(A.5)

can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(N-1)\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\nabla^{B}T_{BA}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)},\\
&(D-3-N+1)T^{(N-1)}_{AB}-(N-1)\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\nabla^{C}J_{CBA}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)}.\end{split}

(A.6)

b) The vector fields
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}
preserve
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction.

Base case: for
N=1

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}=\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(2)}-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{\Omega A\Omega}\nabla_{d}\Omega-\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}^{(0)},\\
&\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega=\nabla_{A}\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla_{B}\Omega-\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{B\Omega A}\nabla_{d}\Omega-\mathrm{C}_{B\Omega A}\Omega=-g^{\Omega\Omega}T_{BA}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)},\end{split}

(A.7)

where we also use the fact that
\nabla_{A}
preserves
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
. The proof of the base case for part b) of the lemma follows from Lemma
4.2
since
\nu_{B}^{(0)}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{A}}
.

Now suppose that the statement of the lemma holds for
i\,{\leqslant}\,N
. We check for
N+1
. Part a):

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N+1}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\Omega^{(1)}+\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(2)}

(A.8)

Consider the second term. By virtue of Proposition
4.1
and the induction assumption

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(2)}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\Omega^{(i+2)}=\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}\Omega^{(2)}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)},

(A.9)

since by the fact that
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}
preserves
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
it in particular follows that
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-1)}\equiv[\nu_{A}^{(N-1)},Q]
preserves this ideal.

Using
Q\Omega=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega+\lambda\Omega
a straightforward computation shows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q\nabla_{b}\Omega=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\Omega+C_{b}{}^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega+\lambda_{b}\Omega+\lambda\nabla_{b}\Omega,\\
&Q\nabla_{c}\nabla_{b}\Omega=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{c}\nabla_{b}\Omega+C_{c}{}^{a}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\Omega+C_{b}{}^{a}\nabla_{c}\nabla_{a}\Omega+\lambda^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega g_{bc}+\lambda\nabla_{c}\nabla_{b}\Omega.\end{split}

(A.10)

Then, using the explicit formula for
\nu_{A}^{(N)}
(
4.8
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu^{(N)}_{A}=&-(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{c\Omega A}+(N-1)\mathcal{P}^{db}_{c\Omega}\nabla^{N-2}_{\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{d\Omega A})\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{b}}-\\
&-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{d\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{d}}+(N-1)\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}+\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N-1}(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-1}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\frac{N-i-1}{i+1}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-2}\mathcal{P}^{db}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{{d\Omega A}})\nu^{(i-1)}_{b},\hskip 9.24994ptN\,{\geqslant}\,1\end{split}

(A.11)

we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}\Omega^{(2)}=\nu_{A}^{(N)}Q\Omega^{(2)}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(1)}.\end{split}

(A.12)

Substituting this result first into (
A.9
), and then into (
A.8
) we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N+1}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\Omega^{(1)}-\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}.

(A.13)

At the same time

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\Omega^{(1)}=-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}(\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\Omega)+\mathcal{K}_{B}=-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\widetilde{\Lambda}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-2)}

(A.14)

and finally we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N+1}\nabla_{A}\Omega^{(1)}=-2\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=\frac{2\widetilde{\Lambda}}{D-3}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\nabla^{e}\mathrm{W}_{e\Omega\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}.

(A.15)

which coincides with the desired one, since
\nabla^{e}\mathrm{W}_{e\Omega\Omega A}=-\nabla^{E}T_{EA}
.

The next generator of the ideal:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N+1}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{B}\Omega+\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)}.

(A.16)

As in the previous case, consider the second term:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)}=\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(1)}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}.

(A.17)

Using formulas (
A.10
), (
A.11
) one verifies that
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}\nabla_{B}\Omega^{(i)}\in\mathcal{K}^{(1)}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N+1}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{B}\Omega+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=\\
=-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}(\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{B\Omega A}\nabla_{d}\Omega+\mathrm{C}_{B\Omega A}\Omega)+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=\\
=T^{(N)}_{BA}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{B\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}.\end{split}

(A.18)

Using

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{B\Omega A}=-\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla^{e}\mathrm{W}_{eB\Omega A}=-\frac{1}{D-3}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-1}T^{(1)}_{BA}+\nabla^{E}J_{EBA})+\mathcal{K}_{B}

(A.19)

we arrive at the original statement.

Part b) of the lemma is proved by explicit calculation. Since
\nu_{A}^{(N)}
has degree
-1
, it suffices to verify that
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}\mathcal{K}_{B}\subset\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
. Let us act with (
A.11
) on the generators of
\mathcal{K}_{B}
of degree 1 (
4.9
), (
4.10
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}Q\Omega=&\frac{N-3}{2}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-4}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\widetilde{\Lambda}+\sum_{i=2}^{N-2}C^{i}_{N-2}(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-2}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\\
&+\frac{N-i-2}{i+1}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-3}\mathcal{P}^{db}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{{d\Omega A}})\nu^{(i-1)}_{b}\Omega+\mathcal{K}_{B}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N-2)},\\
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}(C_{\Omega}{}^{\Omega}-\xi^{b}\nabla_{b}\xi^{\Omega}+\lambda)&\in\mathcal{K}^{(N-2)},\\
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}(C_{\Omega}{}^{B}-\xi^{c}\nabla_{c}\nabla^{B}\Omega)=&-(-T^{(N-2)|B}{}_{A}-(N-2)\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-3}\mathrm{C}^{B}{}_{\Omega A})+\mathcal{K}^{(N-2)}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)},\\
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}(\lambda_{\Omega}+g^{\Omega\Omega}\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}\Omega^{(2)})=&-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-2)}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N)},\\
\nu_{A}^{(N-1)}(C_{B}{}^{\Omega}+g^{\Omega\Omega}\xi^{c}\nabla_{c}\nabla_{B}\Omega)\in&\mathcal{K}^{(N)}.\end{split}

(A.20)

∎

It is obvious that the proof of the theorem almost follows from Lemma
A.1
and Proposition
4.4
. The only remaining issue is that at the moment as new generators of the ideal, we have all
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{A}T_{AB}
,
N\,{\geqslant}\,0
, while under the theorem only
N=D-3
is singled out. We will show that the others can be discarded from the generating set.

Consider one of the generators of the ideal
\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}
(see (
A.18
))

\displaystyle\nabla^{B}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{B}\Omega=[\nabla^{B},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}][\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{B}\Omega+\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{B}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{B}\Omega.

(A.21)

Analogously to the proof of the lemma, the first term belongs to
\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
, since
\mathcal{D}_{B}^{(i)},i<N
preserve
\mathcal{K}^{(i+1)}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(N)|B}\nabla_{\Omega}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}^{(1)}

(A.22)

, which is easy to prove by explicit calculation. Then, expanding the second term,

\nabla^{B}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]\nabla_{B}\Omega=-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{B}(\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{B\Omega A}\nabla_{d}\Omega+\mathrm{C}_{B\Omega A}))+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=\\
=-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{B}T_{BA}+\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}(\nabla^{B}\mathrm{C}_{B\Omega A}\Omega)+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=\\
=-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{B}T_{BA}-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}(\nabla^{\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\Omega)+\mathcal{K}^{(N)}=\\
=-\frac{D-3-N}{D-3}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla^{E}T_{EA}+\mathcal{K}^{(N)},

(A.23)

where we used the consequence of the Bianchi identity (
3.33
)
\nabla^{a}\mathrm{C}_{abc}=0
. For
N\neq D-3
this means that part of the generators are already contained in the prolongations of the others with respect to
\nabla_{A}
and therefore can be excluded. Note that for
N=D-3
this leads to:

\displaystyle\nabla^{B}O_{AB}^{(D-3)}\in\mathcal{K}^{(D-3)}.

(A.24)

Appendix B
Action of
Q
on
T^{(N)}_{AB}

First of all, let us prove the following auxiliary lemma, which will be very useful:

Lemma B.1
.

On
E
we have:

\displaystyle[\Gamma^{B},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}P^{Bc}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}^{(N-2-i)}_{c}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}+N\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(g^{BC}\widetilde{\Lambda}\Delta^{\Omega}{}_{C}-\Delta^{B}{}_{\Omega})+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}.

(B.1)

Proof.

First, let us show that:

\displaystyle[\Gamma^{B},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}P^{Bc}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}^{(N-2-i)}_{c}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}-NP^{Bc}_{d\Omega}\nabla^{N-1}_{\Omega}\Delta^{d}{}_{c}

(B.2)

To prove this, similarly to
[\nabla_{\Omega}^{N},\nabla_{A}]
, we expand
[\nabla^{N}_{\Omega},\Gamma^{B}]
as:

\displaystyle[\nabla^{N}_{\Omega},\Gamma^{B}]=\sum^{N-1}_{i=0}C^{i}_{N}\underbrace{[\nabla_{\Omega},\dots,[\nabla_{\Omega}}_{N-i},\Gamma^{B}]\dots]\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}.

(B.3)

Using the commutation relations (
3.11
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[\nabla_{\Omega},\Gamma^{B}]=P^{Bc}_{d\Omega}\Delta^{d}{}_{c},\\
&\underbrace{[\nabla_{\Omega},\dots,[\nabla_{\Omega}}_{i},\Gamma^{B}]\dots]=P^{Bc}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}^{(i-2)}_{c},\hskip 9.24994pti\,{\geqslant}\,2.\end{split}

(B.4)

Thus,

\displaystyle[\nabla^{N}_{\Omega},\Gamma^{B}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}C^{i}_{N}P^{Bc}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}_{c}^{(N-i-2)}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}+NP^{Bc}_{d\Omega}\Delta^{d}{}_{c}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}.

(B.5)

In the second term, we want to move
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}
to the left, for which we need:

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1},\Delta^{d}{}_{c}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}C^{i}_{N-1}\underbrace{[\nabla_{\Omega},\dots,[\nabla_{\Omega}}_{N-i},\Delta^{d}{}_{c}]\dots]\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}

(B.6)

From the same commutators (
3.11
) we get

\displaystyle\underbrace{[\nabla_{\Omega},\dots,[\nabla_{\Omega}}_{i},\Delta^{d}{}_{c}]\dots]=\delta^{d}_{\Omega}\mathcal{D}^{i-1}_{c},\hskip 9.24994pti\,{\geqslant}\,1.

(B.7)

Substituting everything into (
B.5
) gives us

\displaystyle[\nabla^{N}_{\Omega},\Gamma^{B}]=\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}(C^{i}_{N}-NC^{i}_{N-1})P^{Bc}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}^{(N-2-i)}_{c}\nabla^{i}_{\Omega}+NP^{Bc}_{d\Omega}\nabla^{N-1}_{\Omega}\Delta^{d}{}_{c}.

(B.8)

It is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle C^{i}_{N}-NC^{i}_{N-1}=-(N-1-i)C^{i}_{N}=-d^{i}_{N}

(B.9)

which completes the proof of (
B.2
).
The formula in the lemma is trivially obtained from the definition of
P^{ab}_{cd}=(-g^{ab}g_{cd}+\delta^{a}_{c}\delta^{b}_{d}+\delta^{a}_{d}\delta^{b}_{c})
and from the fact that
g_{C\Omega}
and
(g_{\Omega\Omega}-\widetilde{\Lambda})
belong to
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the following:

Proposition B.2
.

On
E
we have

\displaystyle QT^{(N)}_{BC}=\xi^{A}\nabla_{A}T^{(N)}_{BC}+C_{B}{}^{A}T^{(N)}_{AC}+C_{C}{}^{A}T^{(N)}_{BA}+\lambda^{A}\Gamma_{A}T^{(N)}_{BC}-N\lambda T^{(N)}_{BC}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)},\hskip 18.49988pt\text{where}

(B.10)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{A}T_{BC}^{(N)}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}^{(N-2-i)}_{A}T^{(i)}_{BC}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}(J^{(N-1)}_{ABC}+J_{ACB}^{(N-1)})+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}.

(B.11)

Proof.

Notice that, by (
4.10
) and Lemma
4.2
, on
E
we have

QT^{(N)}_{BC}=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}T^{(N)}_{BC}+C_{d}{}^{e}\Delta^{d}{}_{e}T^{(N)}_{BC}+\lambda^{a}\Gamma_{a}T^{(N)}_{BC}+\lambda\Delta T^{(N)}_{BC}=\\
=\xi^{A}\nabla_{A}T^{(N)}_{BC}+C_{D}{}^{E}\Delta^{D}{}_{E}T^{(N)}_{BC}+\lambda^{A}\Gamma_{A}T^{(N)}_{BC}+\lambda(\Delta-\Delta^{\Omega}{}_{\Omega})T^{(N)}_{BC}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}

(B.12)

For
N=0
, the action of
\Delta_{D}{}^{E}
,
\Gamma_{A}
,
\Delta
,
\Delta^{\Omega}{}_{\Omega}
is obviously read off from the action of
Q
on
\mathrm{W}_{bcde}
(
3.31
), namely

\displaystyle QT_{BC}=QW_{\Omega B\Omega C}=\xi^{a}\nabla_{a}T_{BC}+C_{\Omega}{}^{A}J_{ABC}+C_{B}{}^{A}T_{AC}+C_{\Omega}{}^{A}J_{ACB}+C_{C}{}^{A}T_{BA}+2C_{\Omega}{}^{\Omega}T_{BC}+2\lambda T_{BC}.

(B.13)

For higher
N
, we must commute these vector fields with
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}
using the commutator formulas (
3.11
). For example,

\displaystyle\Delta^{D}{}_{E}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}T_{AB}=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\Delta^{D}{}_{E}T_{AB}+[\Delta^{D}{}_{E},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}]=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\Delta^{D}{}_{E}T_{AB}=\delta^{D}_{A}T_{EB}^{(N)}+\delta^{D}_{B}T^{(N)}_{AE}.

(B.14)

The only nontrivial commutator here is
[\Gamma^{B},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}]
, for which one should use Lemma
B.1
proved above.∎

Using Lemma
B.1
, we can also prove the following

Proposition B.3
.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{C}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}\nabla_{A}T^{A}{}_{B}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}(D-3)\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}\nabla_{D}J^{D}{}_{CB}+\mathcal{K}^{(D-4)}.

(B.15)

Proof.

First, observe that as a consequence of
C_{abc}\equiv-\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla_{d}W^{d}{}_{abc}
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}\nabla_{A}T^{A}{}_{B}=(D-3)\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}C_{\Omega\Omega A}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}.

(B.16)

Using Lemma
B.1
and
\Gamma^{c}C_{abd}=-W^{c}{}_{abd}
we can compute

\Gamma^{C}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}C_{\Omega\Omega A}=\sum_{i=0}^{D-5}d^{i}_{D-3}P^{Cd}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}_{d}^{(D-5-i)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}C_{\Omega\Omega A}+(D-3)\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}(g^{BC}\widetilde{\Lambda}\Delta^{\Omega}{}_{B}-\Delta^{C}{}_{\Omega})C_{\Omega\Omega A}+\\
+T^{(D-3)|C}{}_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}.

(B.17)

In the proof of Theorem
4.4
, it was shown (see (
A.23
)) that for
i<D-3
one has

\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}C_{\Omega\Omega A}\in\mathcal{K}^{(i+1)}
.
Using this and the fact that the corresponding operators
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N)}
preserve this ideal as a consequence of the theorem itself,
we conclude that the entire sum
\sum_{i=0}^{D-5}d^{i}_{D-3}P^{Cd}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathcal{D}_{d}^{(D-5-i)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}C_{\Omega\Omega A}
belongs to
\mathcal{K}^{(D-4)}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
\Delta^{C}{}_{\Omega}C_{\Omega\Omega A}=0
and that
g^{BC}\Delta^{\Omega}{}_{B}C_{\Omega\Omega A}=C^{C}{}_{\Omega A}+C_{\Omega}{}^{C}{}_{A}
. Hence

\displaystyle\Gamma^{C}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}C_{\Omega\Omega A}=(D-3)\widetilde{\Lambda}(\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}C^{C}{}_{\Omega A}+\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}C_{\Omega}{}^{C}{}_{A})+T^{(D-3)|C}{}_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(D-4)}.

(B.18)

Since, as a consequence of the Bianchi identities (
3.33
), we have
\nabla_{\Omega}C_{\Omega AB}=\nabla_{A}C_{\Omega\Omega B}-\nabla_{B}C_{\Omega\Omega A}
, it is easy to see that
\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}C_{\Omega CA}\in\mathcal{K}^{(D-4)}
. Then, substituting the formula (
4.37
), which expresses
C_{A\Omega B}
in terms of the components of the Weyl tensor, we obtain

\displaystyle\Gamma^{C}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-3}C_{\Omega\Omega A}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{D-4}\nabla_{E}J^{EC}{}_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(D-4)}.

(B.19)

Substituting this into (
B.16
), we arrive at the desired formula.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of the formulas for
J^{(N)}_{ABC}
and
\nu_{A}

We begin with the following lemma:

Lemma C.1
.

On
E
, we have the following:

\displaystyle J^{(N)}_{ABC}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{CAB}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}.

(C.1)

Proof.

From (
A.4
) we have
\nabla_{A}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}^{(N)}
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle[\nabla_{A},\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}]\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}\Omega=-\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(N-i)}\nabla_{\Omega}^{i}\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}\Omega\in\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}

(C.2)

which follows from Theorem
4.5
.
Next, observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(N)}\ni\nabla_{A}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}\Omega-\nabla_{B}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{C}\Omega=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}[\nabla_{A},\nabla_{B}]\nabla_{C}\Omega+\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}.

(C.3)

Expanding the commutator yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}\ni\nabla_{\Omega}^{N}(\mathrm{W}^{d}{}_{CAB}\nabla_{d}\Omega+\mathrm{C}_{CAB}\Omega)=J^{(N)}_{ABC}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{CAB}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-1)}.

(C.4)

∎

We now prove the formula for
J^{(N)}_{ABC}
:

Proposition C.2
.

On
E
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&J^{(1)}_{ABC}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{E}W^{E}{}_{CAB}+\mathcal{K}^{(1)},\\
&J^{(N)}_{ABC}=\frac{N}{N-1}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})+\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)},\hskip 18.49988ptN>1.\end{split}

(C.5)

Proof.

Substituting two
\Omega
indices into the Bianchi identity (
3.33
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}J^{(1)}_{ABC}=&\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC}+6\mathrm{C}_{\Omega[AB}g_{\Omega]C}-6\mathrm{C}_{C[AB}g_{\Omega]\Omega}=\\
=&\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC}-\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega B}g_{AC}+\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}g_{BC}-\mathrm{C}_{CAB}g_{\Omega\Omega}+I^{0}(g_{\Omega A}),\end{split}

(C.6)

where
I^{0}(g_{\Omega A})\subset\mathcal{K}_{B}
denotes the ideal generated by
g_{\Omega A}
. Note that this ideal is stable under
\nabla_{a}
, since
\nabla_{a}g_{bc}=0
. Expanding

\displaystyle C_{CAB}=-\frac{1}{D-3}(\nabla_{e}\mathrm{W}^{e}{}_{CAB})=-\frac{1}{D-3}(g^{\Omega\Omega}J_{ABC}^{(1)}+\nabla_{E}\mathrm{W}^{E}{}_{CAB})+I^{0}(g_{\Omega A})

(C.7)

and moving the term with
J^{(1)}_{ABC}
to the left-hand side, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{D-4}{D-3}J^{(1)}_{ABC}=\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC}-\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega B}g_{AC}+\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}g_{BC}+\frac{g_{\Omega\Omega}}{D-3}\nabla_{E}\mathrm{W}^{E}{}_{CAB}+I^{0}(g_{\Omega A}).

(C.8)

Since
T_{AB}\in\mathcal{K}^{(1)},

\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega B}=\frac{1}{D-3}\nabla_{E}T^{E}{}_{C}\in\mathcal{K}^{(1)}
, we arrive at the desired formula (valid for
D>4
):

\displaystyle J^{(1)}_{ABC}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\frac{1}{D-4}\nabla_{E}\mathrm{W}^{E}{}_{CAB}+\mathcal{K}^{(1)}.

(C.9)

Applying
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}
to
(
C.6
)
and using Lemma
C.1
to express
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{CAB}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{N-1}{N}J^{(N)}_{ABC}=\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega B}g_{AC}+\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}g_{BC}+\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}.

(C.10)

Since (see (
A.15
)) we have
\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega B}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}
, we arrive at the desired formula.
∎

Proposition C.3
.

The following is true on
E
:

\displaystyle\nu_{A}^{(N)}=-J^{(N-1),D}{}_{CA}\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{C}{}^{D}}+\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{\Lambda}N}T^{(N)}_{CA}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{C}}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N-2}d^{i}_{N}T^{(i),C}{}_{A}\nu_{C}^{(N-2-i)}+\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}\,

(C.11)

Proof.

First of all (see (
3.14
)) we have

\displaystyle\nu_{A}^{(1)}=r_{\Omega A}=-W^{d}{}_{c\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{d}}-C_{c\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{c}}.

(C.12)

By repeatedly applying
[\nabla_{\Omega},\cdot]
to this formula, we arrive at the following iterative relation:

\nu^{(N)}_{A}=-(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{c\Omega A}+(N-1)\mathcal{P}^{db}_{c\Omega}\nabla^{N-2}_{\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{d\Omega A})\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{c}{}^{b}}-\\
-\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{d\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{d}}+(N-1)\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-2}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}+\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N-1}\Bigl(\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-1}\mathrm{W}^{b}{}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\frac{N-i-1}{i+1}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-2}\mathcal{P}^{db}_{\Omega\Omega}\mathrm{C}_{{d\Omega A}}\Bigr)\nu^{(i-1)}_{b}.

(C.13)

Note that from the definition of
P^{db}_{ca}
, we have

\displaystyle P^{dB}_{C\Omega}=\delta^{B}_{C}\delta^{d}_{\Omega}+\mathcal{K}_{B},\hskip 9.24994ptP^{d\Omega}_{\Omega\Omega}=\delta^{d}_{\Omega}+\mathcal{K}_{B},\hskip 9.24994ptP^{dB}_{\Omega\Omega}=-g^{dB}\widetilde{\Lambda}+\mathcal{K}^{(0)}.

(C.14)

From formulas (
A.15
) and (
A.18
) it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{\Omega\Omega A}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)},\\
&T_{DA}^{(N)}+N\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-1}\mathrm{C}_{D\Omega A}\in\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}.\end{split}

(C.15)

Thus, the remaining coefficients are:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-1}\mathrm{W}^{B}{}_{\Omega\Omega A}+\frac{N-1-i}{i+1}\nabla_{\Omega}^{N-i-2}P^{dB}g_{\Omega\Omega}\mathrm{C}^{B}{}_{\Omega A}=

(C.16)

\displaystyle=-T^{(N-i-1)|B}{}_{A}+\frac{1}{i+1}T^{(N-i-1)|B}{}_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-i)}=-\frac{i}{i+1}T^{(N-i-1)|B}{}_{A}+\mathcal{K}^{(N-i)}.

(C.17)

Consequently, the formula for
\nu_{A}^{(N)}
takes the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{A}^{(N)}=-J^{(N-1)|B}{}_{CA}\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{C}{}^{B}}+\frac{1}{N\widetilde{\Lambda}}T^{(N)}_{AB}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{A}}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}C^{i}_{N-1}\frac{i}{i+1}T^{(N-i-1)|B}{}_{A}\nu_{B}^{(i-1)}+\mathcal{K}^{(N+1)}.\end{split}

(C.18)

Then, using the properties of the binomial coefficient and changing the summation index in the sum, we arrive at the desired formula.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Proposition
4.16

Proof.

Starting from

\widehat{J}^{(0)}_{ABC}=0\,,\hskip 18.49988pt\widehat{T}^{(1)}_{A_{1}A_{2}}=0

(D.1)

and
\nu_{A}^{(1)}=0
which follows from (
C.18
) we can employ the induction in
i
. Assume

\displaystyle\widehat{T}^{(2k-1)}_{BC}=0\hskip 9.24994pt\nu_{A}^{(2k-1)}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\widehat{J}^{(2k-2)}_{ABC}=0

(D.2)

for all
1<k<\dfrac{D-4}{2}-1
we check that

\displaystyle\widehat{T}^{(2k+1)}_{BC}=0\hskip 9.24994pt\nu_{A}^{(2k+1)}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\widehat{J}^{(2k)}_{ABC}=0.

(D.3)

To see this, let us compute
\widehat{J}^{(2k)}
.
(
4.55
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{2k-1}{2k}\widehat{J}^{(2k)}_{ABC}=\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{2k-1}(\nabla_{A}T_{BC}-\nabla_{B}T_{AC})=\widehat{b}^{*}([\nabla^{2k-1}_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]T_{BC}-[\nabla^{2k-1}_{\Omega},\nabla_{B}]T_{AC})=0,

(D.4)

where in the second equality
\nabla^{2k-1}_{\Omega}
passes through
\nabla_{A}
and we use
\widehat{T}^{(2k-1)}_{BC}=0
. Then

\displaystyle\widehat{b}^{*}[\nabla^{2k-1}_{\Omega},\nabla_{A}]T_{BC}=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-2}C^{i}_{N}\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(2k-1-i)}\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{BC}=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-2}C^{i}_{N}[Q,\nu_{A}^{(2k-1-i)}]\widehat{T}^{(i)}_{BC}=0,

(D.5)

by assumption (the upper index of
\nu_{A}
or
T_{AB}
is odd).

Now consider
\widehat{T}^{(2k+1)}_{AB}
. Equations (
4.55
) imply:

\displaystyle 0=(D-4-2k)\widehat{T}^{(2k+1)}_{BC}-(2k+1)\widetilde{\Lambda}(\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(2k)}_{ABC}+\widehat{b}^{*}[\nabla_{\Omega}^{2k},\nabla^{A}]J_{ABC})\rightarrow\widehat{T}^{(2k+1)}_{BC}=0,

(D.6)

where the second term vanishes thanks to (
D.4
), while

\displaystyle\widehat{b}^{*}[\nabla_{\Omega}^{2k},\nabla^{A}]J_{ABC}=0.

(D.7)

by the assumption (each term vanishes separately because it contains either even
\widehat{J}_{ABC}
or odd
\mathcal{D}_{A}
).

Finally, consider
\nu_{A}^{(2k+1)}
. Using (
C.18
) one gets:

\displaystyle\nu_{A}^{(2k+1)}=-\widehat{J}^{(2k),D}{}_{CA}\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{C}{}^{D}}+\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{\Lambda}(2k+1)}\widehat{T}^{(2k+1)}_{CA}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{C}}-\sum_{i=1}^{2k}C^{i}_{2k}\frac{i}{i+1}\widehat{T}^{(2k-i),C}{}_{A}\nu_{C}^{(i-1)}.

(D.8)

As we have seen, the first two terms vanish. The remaining terms also vanish by the induction assumption.
∎

Appendix E
d=8

Proof.

Analogously to the examples for

and

we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{T}^{(2)}_{BC}=\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{2}\widehat{B}_{BC},\hskip 9.24994pt\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{ABC}=-\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{C}_{CAB},\hskip 9.24994pt\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{ABC}=\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{4}(\nabla_{A}\widehat{B}_{BC}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{B}_{AC})\\
&\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}=\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}}{4}\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB},\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{C}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(1)}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{F}_{CB},\\
&\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{B}=\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{8}((\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{CB}+8\widehat{C}^{DA}{}_{B}\widehat{F}_{DA}-2[\widehat{F}_{AB},\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{C}{}^{A}]),\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla^{B}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(3)}=0,\\
&\widehat{F}^{(2)}_{AB}=\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A},\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma_{C}\widehat{F}^{(2)}_{AB}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB}+2\widehat{g}_{CB}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}-2\widehat{g}_{CA}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B},\\
&\Gamma_{C}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(3)}=3\widetilde{\Lambda}(2\nabla_{C}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}_{C}^{(1)})\end{split}

(E.1)

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{B}=\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB}^{(4)}+\mathcal{D}^{(4)|A}\widehat{F}_{AB}+6\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{F}_{AB}^{(2)}.

(E.2)

Expanding:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{D}^{(4)|A}\widehat{F}_{AB}&=-\widehat{J}^{(3)|C}{}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{F}_{AC}+[\widehat{J}^{(3)|A},\widehat{F}_{AB}]-\frac{1}{4}d^{2}_{4}\widehat{T}^{(2)|CA}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB}=\\
&=-\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{4}\nabla_{C}\widehat{B}_{BA}\widehat{F}^{AC}+[\widehat{J}^{(3)|A},\widehat{F}_{AB}]-\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{4}\widehat{B}^{CA}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB},\\
\mathcal{D}^{(2)|A}\widehat{F}^{(2)}_{AB}&=-\widehat{J}^{(1)|C}{}_{B}{}^{A}\widehat{F}_{AC}^{(2)}+[\widehat{J}^{(1)|A},\widehat{F}_{AB}^{(2)}]+\frac{\widehat{T}^{(2)|AC}}{2\widetilde{\Lambda}}\Gamma_{C}\widehat{F}^{(2)}_{AB}=\\
&=\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{C}^{AC}{}_{B}(\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{C}-\nabla_{C}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A})+[\widehat{J}^{(1)|A},\widehat{F}_{AB}^{(2)}]+\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}\widehat{B}^{AC}}{4}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB}+\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{B}^{A}{}_{B}}{2}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A},\\
\nabla^{A}\widehat{F}_{AB}^{(4)|I}&=\nabla^{A}\widehat{b}^{*}\nabla_{\Omega}^{3}(\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}_{B}-\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}_{A})=(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{B}-\nabla^{A}\nabla_{B}\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{A}+3\nabla^{A}(\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{A}\widehat{J}_{B}^{(1)}-\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{B}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A}).\end{split}

(E.3)

But using

=3\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{C}^{DA}{}_{B}(2\nabla_{D}\widehat{J}_{A}^{(1)|I}-\nabla_{A}\widehat{J}_{D}^{(1)|I})-[\widehat{F}_{AB},\widehat{J}^{(3)|A}].

(E.4)

∎

Using this and
\mathcal{D}_{A}^{(2)}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\widehat{C}_{A}{}^{C}{}_{B}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{C}+\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}^{2}}{4}\widehat{B}_{AC}\widehat{F}^{C}{}_{B}+[\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A},\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}]

-[\widehat{F}_{AB},\widehat{J}^{(3)|A}]^{I}+6[\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{A},\nabla^{A}\widehat{J}^{(1)}_{B}].

(E.5)

Therefore, (
E.2
) takes the form

\mathcal{Y}_{B}=(\nabla_{A}\nabla^{A})\widehat{J}^{(3)}_{B}+\frac{3\widetilde{\Lambda^{2}}}{2}(\widehat{B}^{AC}\nabla_{C}\widehat{F}_{AB}-\widehat{F}_{CA}\nabla^{A}\widehat{B}_{B}{}^{C}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{B}^{A}{}_{B}\nabla^{C}\widehat{F}_{CA})+\\

Using the formulas above, it is straightforward to compute
